Schneider Electric · SU-FR · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Schneider Electric

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 55,556 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Schneider sits in the strongest external demand environment of its modern history: two structural drivers (AI data-centre power and grid modernisation) with two-plus years of order-led visibility, layered on an early short-cycle recovery, with law-dated regulation adding a slower third leg. Competitive intensity is rising in portfolio breadth but price conduct remains benign while capacity is scarce, and the main disruption force (800V DC) is being pushed beyond the forecast horizon while its hybrid phase adds content. The external headwinds that matter for earnings are moderate and mostly dated: the capacity wave and lead-time normalisation from 2027, and commodity costs that are being priced through. The headwinds that matter most right now are market-level, not company-level: discount rates, financing of the AI build-out and risk-off rotation, which are compressing the multiple while estimates rise. The Consumer should model above-trend organic growth in 2026 and 2027 that decelerates but does not reverse, a modest price-cost tailwind in 2027, a currency translation headwind, and keep the 800V DC risk out of the 18-month revenue line but in the scenario set for 2029 onward. The single external variable to stress-test is hyperscaler financing, because it is the one external force that could convert a deceleration into a decline.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0246C-0554C-0740C-0954C-3558C-3945
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C+4610th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Competitive intensity is rising in breadth but not yet in price. Every major rival is assembling the same "grid-to-chip" portfolio that was Schneider's differentiator, but capacity scarcity still allocates volume, so pricing remains disciplined. The direction for Schneider is neutral for the next 12 to 18 months and mildly negative over two to four years, as competitor capacity arrives and integrated offers converge.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Portfolio convergence: Eaton bought Boyd Thermal (liquid cooling, about USD 1.7bn of sales) and Fibrebond (modular enclosures) and made about USD 11bn of acquisitions in early 2026; Vertiv is buying UtilityInnovation Group (up to USD 2.6bn) to reach upstream to the utility interconnect; Legrand completed seven acquisitions in H1 2026 (about EUR 450m of revenue, including Girtz modular power); ABB is investing in direct-current protection (Infinitus) and a Mitsubishi Electric joint venture. Schneider's integrated power-plus-cooling edge is narrowing in the white space.
  • Share signals: Eaton's data-centre orders rose about 85% and revenue 65% in Q2 2026, with Eaton described as gaining North American share; Vertiv leads liquid cooling with over 20% share against Schneider's under 20%; ABB Electrification orders rose 70% in Q3 2026. Schneider still leads specification: more than 20% of power spend for 64% of surveyed procurement managers against 36% for Vertiv.
  • Price conduct is benign: Siemens raised Digital Industries prices 5% to 20%; Chinese automation leader Inovance raised human-machine-interface prices 8% to 35%; Legrand has never cut average prices in twenty years. The one warning is that Schneider itself "occasionally competes aggressively on price to win large data center projects" (Vertiv expert).
  • The future intensity driver is capacity: Eaton is investing about USD 1.5bn across 24 North American sites, Siemens over USD 400m, and Bernstein counts about USD 6bn of new global capacity ramping over 18 months; benchmark US switchgear lead times have already normalised to about 40 weeks. A former Schneider regional president expects "intense pricing pressure as supply catches up" over 18 to 24 months.
  • Automation: Chinese domestic brands hold 59% of servos and 61% of robots in China; this intensity is structural but confined to Schneider's smaller, lower-margin segment.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

B+545th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Input costs were a clear headwind in H1 2026 and are turning neutral to slightly positive in H2 2026 and 2027 as accelerating price increases catch up and carry over. Availability (semiconductors, electrical steel, skilled labour) is a constraint on conversion speed, not a margin threat. The net effect over the forecast horizon is a mild tailwind if metal prices stabilise, and a renewed headwind if the energy-led inflation in the macro regime (Brent at USD 111) pushes copper and silver higher again.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Size of exposure: raw materials are about 15% of cost of goods sold, about 9% of revenue; copper and silver are about half of the raw-material bill (so about 4.5% of revenue). Silver rose nearly 100% and copper about 30%; steel is up 32% year to date, aluminium 10%.
  • H1 2026 bridge: gross product pricing +EUR 280m against raw materials −EUR 330m and tariffs −EUR 104m, giving net price −EUR 154m; the gross margin still improved 10 basis points organically to 42.5% only because industrial productivity reached EUR 535m.
  • Turn: product price increases accelerated from about 2% (Q1) to about 4% (Q2) to 5.1% (Q3 2026); management expects to be net price positive for the year, implying an H2 price-cost tailwind of at least EUR 150m (Deutsche Bank). Price carry-over into 2027 is positive while costs, if they plateau, stop rising.
  • Hedging lag: Bernstein assumes about 20% of the top three commodities are hedged over a twelve-month taper, so spikes pass through with a lag of months; list-price pass-through runs three to six months. Systems contracts carry indexation clauses.
  • Concentration: dependence on Taiwanese semiconductors; grain-oriented electrical steel concentrated in few producers; China controls about half of silicon-carbide supply relevant to 800V DC; Schneider's countermeasures include a Singapore active-electronics hub, copper cathode localisation (2025), C-level semiconductor agreements, dual sourcing ("Power of Two") and a rare-earth partnership with Torngat Metals.
  • Labour: shortages of skilled engineering and site labour are an industry-wide conversion constraint; prefabrication (factory labour instead of site labour) is partly Schneider's answer.

For the model: treat 2026 as roughly neutral price-cost for the full year (negative H1, positive H2) and 2027 as a modest positive (carry-over), with copper and silver sensitivity explicitly flexed.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

C4015th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Macro forces bite hardest at the valuation level of the sector and industry, not at Schneider's earnings. The discount rate (US 10-year near 5.10%, a December Fed hike above 90% priced), the financing crack in the AI chain and French sovereign stress are de-rating long-duration quality capital goods. At company level the macro bite is narrower: currency translation and weak residential construction.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Company level: non-euro revenue is 81% of the group; FX cut FY2025 revenue by EUR 1,555m and adjusted EBITA by EUR 482m (about 40 basis points of margin), and H1 2026 revenue by EUR 747m and EBITA by EUR 173m; FY26 FX on revenue is now guided at −EUR 300m to −400m with margin roughly flat. A 10% US-dollar move changes revenue by EUR 1,505m and adjusted EBITA by EUR 427m (a 28% drop-through). Broker forecasts put EUR/USD at 1.16 to 1.20 by mid-2027, so translation stays a modest headwind into 2027. Residential construction remains weak in the US, China (property investment −26% year on year in July) and Europe.
  • Sector level: the O.4 Sector fell 3.5% over three months; every one of the 16 stocks in the dashboard sector carries technical momentum D or F; the Fitness for Fighting regime is "risk-off confirmed and broadening", with Momentum moved to Underweight and Fragility at Double Underweight.
  • Industry level: the AI capital-spending theme "is cracking at its financing, not its demand", with credit leading; Goldman's risk-appetite indicator turned negative; technology net selling heaviest since January 2025. Schneider sits in the "supplier" half of the AI chain, which is the wrong half if leadership rotates to the investors.
  • Where it shows: Schneider's 24-month-forward P/E fell to 16.33x (11th percentile of five years) while FY27 EPS estimates rose 7% over three months: the macro is acting through the multiple.

For the model: use a currency line (US dollar 38% of revenue, a falling dollar is a translation headwind) and a residential-buildings line growing at or below zero in 2027; keep macro out of the data-centre and grid volume lines unless the financing crack spreads to orders.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Three other dynamics matter. Positive: procurement is consolidating towards a few trusted, integrated, high-capacity vendors (single-sourcing of prefabricated modules), which favours Schneider structurally. Negative: the capacity wave and lead-time normalisation from 2027 will erode scarcity pricing; and the AI build-out's financing is now the market's main worry. Neutral to positive: the chip-architecture race makes NVIDIA, AMD and Foxconn the de facto specifiers, and Schneider is inside their reference designs.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Vendor consolidation: hyperscale designers are risk-averse and prefer proven brands to avoid downtime; vendor switching is "extremely rare"; non-hyperscalers cede design decisions to vertically integrated manufacturers (Bernstein); a global data-centre operator reduced Legrand procurement to buy "more complicated" equipment from Schneider.
  • Reference-design gatekeeping: Schneider is in the AMD Helios reference design, the NVIDIA DSX POD partnership and an NVIDIA/HPE physical-AI collaboration; being designed-in at the chip vendor raises Schneider's share of new builds.
  • Capacity and lead-time normalisation: about USD 6bn of new capacity ramps over 18 months (Bernstein); the Industry Digest says capacity dates "set the window and cannot be pulled forward"; margins on old low-price backlogs rise to 2028, and the market will debate the plateau after 2028.
  • Financing: "a capital-intensive theme cracks at its financing before its demand" (Wisdom Library, 16-Oct-26); neocloud spreads widened; Deutsche Bank notes the sustainability of debt-financed hyperscaler spending is debated. A financing failure would not be evidence against demand, but it would defer orders.
  • Talent and water: skilled labour is the binding constraint on project execution; water stress drives a new digital-water demand pocket (small).
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

C+5810th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. New-entrant pressure is low to moderate overall. Adjacent entrants (cooling and power-electronics companies moving into each other's space) are the most active and the most serious, but they are mostly existing peers broadening. Standard entrants (Chinese, Korean and Indian manufacturers) are a real threat in standardised products and in emerging markets. Orthogonal entrants (solid-state power-electronics start-ups, semiconductor firms, software-defined control from cloud providers) are a long-dated threat to Secure Power and automation hardware. None threatens Schneider's 2026 to 2028 earnings materially.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Adjacent: Vertiv into the utility interconnect; Eaton into liquid cooling; Kingspan into data-centre power distribution (doubling wallet share to USD 1m per megawatt); Foxconn as a manufacturing partner today, a potential competitor tomorrow; Bloom Energy (fuel cells generating 800V DC natively) bypassing transformers. Threat: medium.
  • Standard: Shanghai Liangxin supplying North American data centres through Eaton and Vertiv; Korean transformer makers at 19% of US imports; Chinese T&D makers (TBEA) winning international orders on cost; Inovance and Estun in China automation. Threat: medium in standard products, low in certified systems (UL 2140, IEC, utility pre-qualification).
  • Orthogonal or innovative: Heron Power (40 GW a year target, RWE pilot April 2027), DG Matrix (ABB-backed solid-state power routers), Sungrow, Enphase and SolarEdge (solid-state transformer modules), Infineon and Navitas (silicon carbide platforms); hyperscalers and AWS-type cloud control making automation "software-defined"; agentic AI bypassing legacy software layers. Threat: low before 2028, rising after 2029.
  • Entry barriers holding: certifications taking six to 24 months; one-year utility qualification tests; zero-leakage and downtime requirements; installed base and service networks.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

D4521st of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Suppliers are a moderate source of risk rather than a support: commodity prices (copper, silver, steel) and concentrated component supply (Taiwanese semiconductors, grain-oriented steel, silicon carbide) are the main constraints, and supplier capacity limits how fast backlog converts. Schneider's scale and local-for-local sourcing make it more resilient than smaller peers, and its purchasing power is an advantage, but no supplier dynamic is a tailwind today.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Scale of purchasing: about 20,000 suppliers and partners; purchases of about EUR 11bn of a EUR 21bn cost of goods sold (Redburn).
  • Commodities: raw-material inflation −EUR 330m in H1 2026; component prices stepped up 5% to 10% in the industry to offset copper and silver; hedging covers about 20% of the top three commodities over twelve months.
  • Concentration: heavy reliance on Taiwanese fabricators for semiconductors; few qualified grain-oriented steel producers; silicon carbide supply concentrated (China about half); rare earths (Torngat Metals memorandum of understanding, June 2026).
  • Mitigation: Singapore active-electronics hub (2024); copper cathode localisation (2025); "Power of Two" dual sourcing; C-level long-term semiconductor agreements; 98% of strategic suppliers engaged under the Decent Work programme; contract manufacturers and Foxconn for capacity.
  • Peer reference: ABB's three-to-six-month commodity pass-through lag cost 270 basis points of Electrification gross margin in Q1 2026; the risk is sector-wide.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0182C-0358C-0468C-0888C-3278C-3770C-4368
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

A823rd of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Demand is changing structurally upward in the parts of the portfolio that carry the growth (data centres, about 30% of sales on Redburn's measure, and power grid and infrastructure), and cyclically upward in short-cycle products and discrete automation; only residential buildings remain cyclically weak. The structural part carries two or more years of visibility; the cyclical recovery carries about twelve months. For the model this means FY27 organic growth in the high single digits to low double digits is well underwritten by orders already booked, and the debate is the shape of 2028.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Acceleration is real and broad: organic growth was 8.9% in FY2025, then 11.2% in Q1 2026, 16.5% in Q2 and 16.9% in Q3 (Energy Management +18.8%, Industrial Automation +8.1%). Product growth stepped from 9% (Q1) to 13% (Q2) to 17% (Q3), which is the short-cycle signal; China and East Asia grew 26.1% in Q3 and North America 23.1% in Q2.
  • Visibility is order-led: pure data-centre orders grew at triple-digit rates in both Q2 and Q3 2026; the backlog was EUR 25.4bn at end-2025 (+18%); hyperscaler commitments run two to three years, Morgan Stanley reports 18 to 24 months of order-book visibility and the chief executive says data-centre visibility extends into 2028. Because data-centre systems convert 12 to 24 months after order, the Q2 and Q3 2026 order surge underwrites 2027 revenue even if orders normalise.
  • The demand drivers are multi-year: US hyperscaler capex +45% in 2026 (Deutsche Bank); US investor-owned utilities propose at least USD 1.4 trillion of capex over five years; European network capex +10% to 12% in 2026; the EU F-gas ban on SF6 in medium-voltage switchgear from January 2026 forces replacement. Management guides data centres to a 12% to 14% CAGR through 2030, and Bernstein estimates data centres provide close to 50% of group organic growth to 2030.
  • The cyclical parts: discrete automation recovered after destocking (Q2 Industrial Automation +11%); residential demand is weak in the US and China, and the European construction recovery is "a 2027 story" (Redburn). Non-residential (healthcare, public buildings) is resilient.
  • Normalisation path: Jefferies expects data-centre growth to settle at 15% to 20% over 18 months; Bernstein notes ABB already faces a 33% data-centre order comparison in Q4. Normalisation is a fall from triple digits to mid-teens, not a contraction.

What would change it. The structural demand is debt-financed at the margin: the Fitness for Fighting digest records credit spreads widening for neoclouds and hyperscalers. A financing-led pause would hit orders first and revenue 12 to 24 months later; grid-connection queues (three to five years) can delay conversion without cancelling demand.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

C+5810th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The main disruption force, 800-volt direct current with solid-state transformers inside AI data centres, is being disproved as a near-term threat and confirmed as a long-term one. Over 2026 to 2028 it acts positively on Schneider (hybrid "sidecar" architectures and prefabrication raise content per megawatt); after 2029 it threatens part of Secure Power (traditional AC UPS, roughly a third of data-centre revenue, so roughly 10% of group sales on the file's figures). The second disruption force, modular prefabricated construction, acts clearly in Schneider's favour now. The third, agentic AI eroding seat-based software, is a valuation headwind on the software segment more than an earnings one.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Timing evidence is pushing the threat out: NVIDIA's Kyber NVL576 debut has slipped to 2028; industry specialists expect no meaningful solid-state transformer volume before 2029; native DC is expected to be about 10% of global data-centre installations by 2030 (Redburn); Bernstein's base case keeps AC at 85% share through 2030; solid-state transformers cost about 3.5 times (and solid-state breakers up to 10 times) conventional equipment; safety standards (arc flash, UL 2877) are immature.
  • Schneider is positioned rather than exposed: it has developed its own solid-state transformer and is testing it with "a couple of customers"; 87% of surveyed data-centre customers say they are likely to buy Schneider's solid-state solution (Bernstein) and 73% are aware of it; it secured the first UL 2140 certification for prefabricated data-centre power assemblies; it was named a certified partner for NVIDIA's DSX POD modular reference design (October 2026).
  • Content gain in the hybrid phase: addressable content per megawatt rises from about USD 2.5m to over USD 3m (Jefferies, Legrand data); prefabricated electrical rooms lift content from USD 1.8m to USD 2.8m per megawatt (Bernstein); modular builds are projected to rise from 20% to 60% of builds by 2030; Bernstein argues prefabrication drives single-sourcing that favours Schneider.
  • Where it acts negatively: in a native DC design, AC switchgear, UPS and rack PDUs are disintermediated; Bernstein records Secure Power at 32% of Schneider's data-centre revenue; Eaton launched an IEC-certified medium-voltage solid-state transformer (MVSST 2.0) and holds ten prototype orders, so the eventual winners are not pre-determined.
  • Software: Schneider says about 90% of its software revenue is insulated from AI seat-erosion because it is tied to physical infrastructure, and AVEVA prices on consumption; the market nevertheless prices industrial software as an AI victim (Jefferies estimates the market values Schneider's industrial automation at zero to eight times operating profit).

For the model: no revenue loss to 800V DC before 2029; a modest content tailwind in data centres in 2026 to 2028; the risk belongs in terminal value and in the multiple, not in the 18-month forecast.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

B+685th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Regulatory change is extensive and is, net, a demand tailwind dated by law (SF6 phase-out, building-automation mandates, grid funding, local-content rules), but the friction is rising on the conversion side: data-centre permitting halts, tariffs and trade-bloc fragmentation raise costs and can delay revenue. The net bearing is positive for volumes and pricing in grid and buildings, and a two-sided timing risk in data centres.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Positive, law-dated demand: the revised EU F-gas Regulation (in force 1 January 2026) bans SF6 in new medium-voltage switchgear up to 24 kV, with further steps for high voltage in 2028, up to 52 kV in 2030 and the rest in 2032; SF6-free switchgear is 10% to 15% more expensive (Redburn) and Schneider already achieves an 80% substitution rate (AirSeT). EU rules made building automation mandatory from 2025 for non-residential buildings above 290 kW. The US SPARK programme (31 projects, USD 1.9bn federal plus USD 3.35bn cost share) and Build America, Buy America rules favour local suppliers; Germany's infrastructure stimulus should support 2026 to 2027 investment.
  • Protection from competition: "Made in Europe" criteria under the Industrial Accelerator Act (2028 at the earliest, so less than told) and US local-content rules lengthen the pricing window for localised incumbents; Schneider's multi-hub model targets 90% of sales produced in-region.
  • Costs and friction: H1 2026 net tariff impact was −EUR 104m, partly offset by a EUR 100m tariff refund in Q2; 11% of employees sit in Mexican manufacturing (tariff exposure); export controls and fragmented standards raise SG&A. Texas paused data-centre environmental permits; Virginia tightened behind-the-meter gas rules; the US House passed the Ratepayer Protection Act 417 to 3; Spain proposed hourly renewable matching. Each delays projects rather than removing demand.
  • Legal: the French Competition Authority fine of EUR 207m (paid in 2025, under appeal; EUR 104m provision balance referenced in 2026).
  • Country risk: French sovereign spreads at decade highs (Fitness for Fighting: country FR at Double Underweight); for Schneider this acts through the discount rate rather than earnings, since euro revenue is only about 19% of the group and France a fraction of that.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

A882nd of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Strongly. Two sector drivers (AI data-centre power and grid modernisation) are accelerating Schneider's demand now and are the main reason growth more than doubled from 2025 rates. Schneider captures them disproportionately because it sells the integrated, prefabricated, single-source package that time-constrained buyers now prefer.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Data centres: triple-digit order growth in Q2 and Q3 2026, led by hyperscale and colocation in North America and accelerating in China; management guides a 12% to 14% data-centre CAGR to 2030; Bernstein estimates data centres contribute close to 50% of group organic growth to 2030 and about 4.5 percentage points of annual EPS growth.
  • Speed to power: the Sector Digest's "speed to power" story (Big) says buyers "pay for the delivery date", so pricing power attaches to time and is strongest where capacity is shortest; Schneider grew prefabricated capacity about fourfold to more than one million square feet on eight sites, added Foxconn (asset-light North American capacity) and a SoftBank-linked Dunkirk module plant (up to 5 GW of French AI capacity by 2031), and signed capacity agreements (Compass, USD 3bn over five years; Switch, a 1.9 billion supply capacity agreement, currency not stated in the file).
  • Behind-the-meter power: 22% of data-centre capacity under construction and 40% of the active pipeline (Aterio via Bernstein) is behind the meter, multiplying electrical content per megawatt (substations, eHouses), where Schneider is ranked among the purest European plays.
  • Grid: US utility long-term budgets +22% year on year; European network capex +10% to 12%; the SF6 phase-out; Infrastructure is now an independent growth driver rather than an adjunct to data centres (Redburn).
  • Relative capture: Schneider's Q3 organic growth of 16.9% led ABB (14%) and Legrand (about 10.7%); its 2030 growth target of 7% to 10% is above Siemens (6% to 9%) and ABB (5% to 7%) but below Vertiv (20% to 22%), the pure play.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

A783rd of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Customer demand strongly supports the thesis: hyperscalers, colocation operators, utilities and non-residential building owners are buying more, earlier, and increasingly from single integrated vendors, paying for delivery certainty rather than lowest price. The support is fragile mainly through customer financing (debt-funded AI capex, colocation margin pressure) and through conversion bottlenecks outside Schneider's control (grid connections, permits, labour), rather than through any loss of customer preference.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Behaviour: hyperscale designers are risk-averse and prefer proven global brands because downtime penalties are severe; "developers are shifting orders toward vendors with proven delivery capability rather than lowest pricing"; customers reserve production slots years ahead (capacity agreements with Compass and Switch); data-centre procurement activity rose 20% to 30% year on year in Q2 2026 (procurement expert); internal tracking covers about 200 global customers representing 260 GW of demand with two-to-three-year volume commitments.
  • Customer quality: the largest customer is under 10% of revenue; distribution spreads credit risk; hyperscalers and colocation operators are concentrated buyers at the project level.
  • Other end markets: non-residential (healthcare, public administration, retail chains) resilient; combined power-and-process installations can pay back in under six months; residential weak in the US, China and Europe; industrial customers "prioritizing selective cost-saving investments" in Germany; Middle East weakened by geopolitics in Q1 to Q2 2026.
  • Fragility: colocation operators face margin compression as bargaining power shifts to hyperscaler end-users; neocloud credit spreads have widened; grid queues of three to seven years and permit pauses (Texas) delay energisation and therefore customer handover; SoftBank deferred a transformer order in August 2026 due to state-level permitting bans (an early warning cited by Jefferies).
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

A704th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Regulators and politicians are, on balance, helping: they mandate the demand (SF6 phase-out, building automation, grid investment, local content) on legal timetables that favour qualified incumbents. They hurt at the margin through tariffs, export controls, antitrust and, increasingly, through local resistance to data centres (power bills, water, noise), which slows conversion. The helpful position is robust because it is legislated and dated; the hurtful one is more volatile but affects timing more than demand.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Helping: EU F-gas steps (2026, 2028, 2030, 2032); EU building-automation mandates (non-residential above 290 kW from 2025); EU Electrification Action Plan (46% electricity share target by 2040); US SPARK grid funding; Build America, Buy America; RTE's "first-ready, first-served" connection reform in France (Morgan Stanley expects it to accelerate prefabricated campuses); Germany's infrastructure plan.
  • Hurting: US tariffs (net −EUR 104m in H1 2026); possible Mexico tariffs (11% of employees in Mexico); export controls raising SG&A French antitrust fine (EUR 207m, under appeal); Texas permit pause and audit of ERCOT data-centre requests; Virginia's emissions framework for behind-the-meter gas; the US Ratepayer Protection Act directing states to consider dedicated tariffs for loads above 100 MW; Spain's proposed hourly renewable matching.
  • Means, motive, opportunity: Morgan Stanley's test finds local regulators have all three to drive a non-consensus slowdown in AI data-centre expansion, which would "abruptly halt the high-multiple capex boom".
  • Fragility: low for grid and buildings mandates; medium for data-centre permitting, where politics is moving against local load growth.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

C-6817th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. The addressable market is large relative to Schneider (company estimate above EUR 600bn by 2030, so Schneider holds roughly 7% to 8%), and it is growing at 6% to 7% a year with the data-centre and grid sub-markets growing two to three times faster. It allows 7% to 10% organic growth over the mid term (market growth plus price plus share gains in data centres) without any share-gain heroics. The binding constraint on growth over the forecast horizon is capacity and conversion, not market size.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Company framework: accessible market expanding at 6% to 7% a year driven by AI and electrification; mid-term organic growth target 7% to 10% to 2030.
  • Data centres: hyperscalers and colocation developers are projected to invest roughly USD 800bn in electrical equipment globally through 2030 (Deutsche Bank); global leased capacity rising from 82 GW to 219 GW by 2030 (McKinsey); Schneider's content ranges from USD 1.2m to 3.3m per megawatt depending on scope, and rises with prefabrication; management guides a 12% to 14% data-centre CAGR to 2030.
  • Grid and industry: US electricity demand +2.6% a year; data centres 20.3% of US electricity demand by 2035; automation and software in the power sector +10% a year to 2030 against a broad industrial automation market (EUR 185bn in 2025) that grew only 4.7% a year.
  • Software: PTC triples Schneider's industrial-software addressable market (company).
  • Arithmetic: with data centres about 30% of sales growing low-to-mid teens and the rest growing 4% to 6%, the group grows about 7% to 9% before price; adding 1% to 2% price gives the 7% to 10% framework. The 2026 rate (12% to 14%) exceeds it because of the order surge and short-cycle recovery.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0670C-1080
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

B+704th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Schneider is near the peak rate of growth of an up-cycle, not at the peak of the cycle. Order intake and backlog are at records and still rising, short-cycle products are accelerating, and only the growth rate (the second derivative) is likely to turn down in 2027 as data-centre comparisons toughen. Nothing in the evidence points to a revenue downturn inside 18 months.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Phase markers: record quarterly revenue of EUR 11,460m in Q3 2026; triple-digit data-centre orders in two successive quarters; backlog at record highs with "no signs of delays"; channel sell-in and sell-out balanced, with no pre-buying (Jefferies, Q1 2026).
  • Short cycle is early in its upturn: products grew 17% in Q3 after a 2025 destocking trough; Industrial Automation turned from 3% organic growth in FY2025 and 4.4% in Q1 2026 to 11% in Q2 and 8.1% in Q3; DNB Carnegie says distributor destocking is largely complete across the sector.
  • The guide implies a deceleration: the 12% to 14% FY26 range implies about 8% organic growth in Q4 at the midpoint (Deutsche Bank), against 10.7% in Q4 2025; that is partly conservatism (see C-71) and partly comparison.
  • 2027 shape: data-centre growth normalising to 15% to 20% (Jefferies) from triple-digit orders; non-data-centre buildings recovering only in 2027; grid steady. A reasonable reading is group organic growth of high single digits to about 10% in 2027, still above the 7% to 10% mid-term framework's midpoint.
  • Peer cross-check: ABB's Electrification backlog grew to USD 16bn on a 1.41x book-to-bill in Q3 2026; Eaton's backlog USD 24.1bn (+43% in Electrical); Siemens Energy's backlog EUR 162bn. The cycle is sector-wide, which is both reassurance (structural) and a warning (the same capacity wave arrives for all).
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

A801st of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The external picture is strongly positive on fundamentals and negative on market conditions. At value-chain and industry level the tailwinds (data-centre power, grid, speed-to-power pricing, law-dated regulation, the short-cycle turn) clearly outweigh the headwinds (capacity wave from 2027, Chinese competition in low-end automation, commodity inflation, long-dated 800V DC risk). At market level the headwinds (discount rate, AI-financing fear, risk-off rotation, French spreads) currently dominate the share price. The gap between the two is the core of the investment case: earnings estimates rising while the multiple falls.

Taking the Sector Digest's five stories in turn:

  1. Contracted margin (Big): "a price plateau at a high level is two to three more years of rising margin as contracts won before 2022 run off". Tailwind for Schneider's systems backlog margins through 2028. Strength: strong.
  2. Speed to power (Big): pricing attaches to the delivery date; Schneider is a named beneficiary through prefabrication. Strength: strong; watch for the 2027 "bridge or permanent" debate on on-site gas.
  3. Two automation engines (Big, early): hardware recovery from a five-quarter destocking trough plus software priced as an AI victim. Schneider has both, and its industrial automation is valued at near zero by the market (Jefferies). Strength: moderate tailwind to earnings, potential tailwind to the multiple.
  4. 800-volt (early): priced about four years before it can bite; the hybrid phase adds content. Strength: neutral near term, a terminal-value question.
  5. Law-dated distribution grid (early, slow): SF6 steps 2026, 2028, 2030, 2032; the qualified majors (Schneider, ABB, Siemens) get the price. Strength: moderate, durable tailwind.

Headwinds: the capacity wave (moderate, from 2027); raw materials (moderate, fading); currency (mild); residential construction (mild); permitting and grid queues (timing only); macro valuation pressure (strong but non-fundamental).

Overall: at value-chain level, strongly positive; at industry level, positive with a 2027 to 2029 normalisation risk; at sector level, positive in earnings and negative in sentiment. For an 18-month model the external forces support above-trend growth and rising margins; they do not support assuming multiple expansion.

Internal change forces/risks

–not yet rated

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The internal change is a genuine operational turnaround inside a business that was already growing: the board replaced an external-style chief executive with a 31-year insider who has visibly tightened cost, pricing and cash discipline, and the results (two guidance raises, half the five-year margin plan in year one, record H1 cash) are tangible. That self-help is the most reliable internal driver of 2026 and 2027 earnings and supports modelling margin expansion at or above the 50 basis points a year of the mid-term plan. Against it, the capital-allocation pattern is unchanged and has intensified: roughly EUR 31bn to 32bn committed to acquisitions at record multiples within twenty months, funded by debt, new equity and a buyback pause, with incentives that do not measure returns on capital. For the Consumer this splits cleanly: operating profit should be modelled on the strong internal forces; earnings per share and the balance sheet must carry the financing drag of PTC (interest, new shares, no buybacks in 2027 and 2028) and a lower-confidence synergy line; and the multiple should not be assumed to recover until integration evidence appears. Leadership is a strength for delivery and a question mark for capital allocation.

2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement55C-1476C-8635C-5956C-8152D-0360
Group judgement

Risks

B+557th of 38

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The core growth engine is not decelerating; it is running at nearly twice the company's normal rate, and the input force is still accelerating in orders. The risks are therefore forward and correlated: the fundamental tails (a financing-led hyperscaler pause, permitting freezes, an AI credit event) all hit the same data-centre engine and would land in 2027 just as PTC lifts leverage to about 2.3 times and removes the buyback. Software integration is the main idiosyncratic tail. Sentiment risks are high (crowding, an equity overhang, risk-off rates, 800V DC narratives, French spreads) but have already driven a 26% fall from the high and a 24-month-forward P/E at the 11th percentile of five years, so the stock already sits near stressed multiples: the multiple-only trough is about 20% to 25% lower, and deeper falls need earnings cuts. For the second set, the risk case to model is a 2027 data-centre order pause with a 2028 revenue effect, flexed against leverage, rather than a near-term earnings collapse.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

A762nd of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Standards are improving materially and measurably in cost discipline, pricing and cash, which is the main internal change force behind the 2026 earnings upgrade. The improvement is partly the harvesting of fat built up in 2022 to 2025, so it is real but not all of it is repeatable at the 2026 rate.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Cost: industrial productivity of EUR 535m in H1 2026, against EUR 596m for the whole of 2025, beating consensus by EUR 272m (design-to-cost and centralised procurement); support-function costs down 1.1 points of sales in H1 (two-thirds of the five-year target in six months); central functions and digital costs down to 1.7% of revenue from 1.9%.
  • Where the fat came from: Energy Management headcount rose 24% in three years against 1% to 7% at peers (Redburn), and average annual productivity fell to 0.7% of sales after 2020 from a 1.5% ten-year average (Bernstein). Morgan Stanley judges the H1 margin expansion "headcount-driven rather than efficiency-led" and expects the next leg from operational efficiency and AI.
  • Pricing discipline: a new pricing methodology from 1 January 2026; a "gross margin obsession" mandate with selectivity on systems projects; a proactive dual-pricing strategy; product pricing accelerated to 5.1% by Q3.
  • Cash: record H1 free cash flow of EUR 1,631m (+244%, 17% above consensus); net capex down to 3.2% of revenue from 3.7%.
  • Governance: clawback and malus provisions since 2023; 92% board independence; clean audits; whistleblowing system (2,756 alerts in 2025).
  • Weak spots: Industrial Automation's H1 margin of 14.0% missed consensus by 60 to 70 basis points; a EUR 128m impairment of capitalised development costs in H1 2026 on range simplification; trade working capital consumed EUR 1,208m in H1 as factoring was lost (debtor days +4).
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

D3524th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The negative developments cluster around capital allocation and integration: an expensive, leveraged, dilutive software deal on top of three other expensive transactions, a buyback pause, and a talent base that is thinner in software than the strategy now requires. On the operating side, the negatives are smaller and largely known: restructuring cash, Industrial Automation margins and working capital.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Present: PTC financing (EUR 16bn to 17bn of debt, EUR 5bn to 6bn of equity, buyback pause); restructuring costs of about EUR 450m in 2026, elevated against history and outside adjusted EBITA; a EUR 128m capitalised-R&D impairment; R&D capitalisation of EUR 367m in FY2025 (15% of EUR 2,380m innovation spend) flattering reported margins against Eaton, which expenses all R&D H1 trade working capital outflow of EUR 1,208m and loss of factoring; contract assets up to EUR 2,740m from EUR 2,244m; voluntary turnover up to 13.9% from 12.6%; "significant" loss of AI and software talent over 18 months; R&D step-up to 7% of sales (a margin headwind Morgan Stanley views with "mixed feelings").
  • Governance design: the long-term incentive plan has no return-on-capital metric (40% adjusted EPS, 35% relative TSR, 25% carbon), so dilutive acquisitions carry no penalty for executives; the company's ROCE excludes EUR 10.2bn of minority buyouts.
  • Plausible: integration bugs and customer friction across AVEVA, Cognite and PTC; revenue-synergy shortfalls leading to impairments; a second acquisition-driven capital raise; rising Industrial Automation competition from Chinese domestic suppliers; a disappointment versus the now-high FY26 margin guide if copper rises again.
  • Absent: no evidence of accounting restatements, of guidance cuts or of strategic drift in Energy Management.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

C-5616th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Congruence is good in the electrical operating system and weak in capital allocation and software. In operations, goals (growth and margin), incentives (70% of the bonus on organic growth and margin), culture (growth-aggressive, locally empowered, fact-based challenge) and execution reinforce each other. That alignment breaks in two places: incentives do not measure the return on capital that the stated RONIC goal implies, so acquisition decisions are not disciplined by the system; and the formal, matrix-heavy French corporate culture is repelling the software and AI talent the strategy now depends on. Schneider is not a Lifco- or Ryanair-style total system.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Congruent: decentralised multi-hub model with significant autonomy for country presidents; lower power distance than Honeywell (employees challenge decisions with data); a horizontal commercial organisation and an independent data-centre P&L that package cross-business solutions; 61% employee participation in shareholding plans; Net Satisfaction Score 4.3 (above target).
  • Incongruent: the long-term plan rewards adjusted EPS (40%), relative TSR (35%) and carbon (25%), with no ROCE, while the stated framework targets 25% to 30% post-tax returns on new organic capital and excludes buyouts from ROCE; the decision culture includes "informal coffee-table discussions"; "pushy" execution has caused cross-cultural friction (India) and sometimes ignores plant realities; bureaucracy and pay limits drive software engineers to start-ups; former sales directors describe disjointed go-to-market between hardware and acquired software.
  • Risk to the model: congruence in operations supports confidence in the Energy Management margin path; incongruence in software supports a haircut to PTC and Cognite synergies.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

B529th of 38

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. The fundamental tail risks are few but correlated: most of them (a hyperscaler capex pause, a permitting freeze, a credit event in the AI chain) hit the same data-centre engine that provides about half of group growth, and they would arrive in 2027 when leverage peaks after PTC. The software-integration tail is the main idiosyncratic one. None is likely within 18 months, but their joint occurrence is the scenario the model must stress.

Risks, with my plausibility over 18 months and estimated magnitude:

  1. Hyperscaler or neocloud capex pause, financing-led (low to medium plausibility): data centres are about 30% of sales and about half of growth; a 30% to 50% fall in data-centre orders would cut 2028 revenue growth by 5 to 8 points with a 12-to-24-month lag; Morgan Stanley's bear case (growth 6% in 2027, flat margins) gave a value of EUR 220 to 230.
  2. PTC integration failure (medium plausibility of revenue-synergy shortfall; low of outright failure): impairments, a lower software multiple, margin dilution; the Deutsche Bank warning of "severe margin dilution and writedowns".
  3. Commodity and tariff shock (medium): H1 2026 showed a gross headwind of about EUR 430m in one half (raw materials plus tariffs); a renewed copper and silver spike or a wider US tariff (11% of employees in Mexico) would cost 1 to 2 points of margin for one to two halves before price catches up.
  4. Permitting and grid freeze (medium): Texas pause, Virginia rules; mostly a timing effect on revenue recognition.
  5. Accelerated technology shift (low): hyperscalers standardising native 800V DC or leapfrogging to 1,500V DC (Deutsche Bank black-swan) would threaten Secure Power (about 10% of group sales) from 2028.
  6. Product quality or safety failure (low): Siemens Energy's EUR 7bn one-day loss in 2023 is the sector precedent; arc-flash risk in new DC architectures.
  7. Geopolitical supply shock (low): Taiwan semiconductors; Chinese control of half of silicon-carbide supply.
  8. Currency (medium): a 10% fall in the US dollar costs EUR 427m of adjusted EBITA (about 5% of EPS).
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

B608th of 38

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The drivers are concentrated, but they divide into two kinds with opposite implications for predictability. The operating difference between the forecast and history rests on three drivers, of which one dominates: data-centre conversion supplies roughly half of the organic growth in 2026 and 2027, with price carry-over and overhead leverage supplying most of the margin gain. The per-share difference rests on three financing drivers (the share count, the cost of the acquisition debt and the PTC closing date), of which two are now fixed in fact and the third is bounded. So the earnings change is concentrated in a small number of visible, measurable drivers rather than diffused across many, which makes it easier to track than a diffuse story but exposes it to one external variable: the financing of the data-centre build-out, which no company disclosure can show the Consumer until orders have already turned. Concentration is high; the predictability cost of that concentration is moderate over 18 months because the concentrated driver is largely booked, and high beyond.

The decomposition, FY2025 to FY2027 (my base; adjusted EBITA EUR 7,508m to about EUR 10,477m; EPS EUR 8.59 to about EUR 11.77).

Driver Contribution Kind Status at 29 October 2026
Data-centre volume (about 30% to 33% of sales, +25% to +30% in 2026, +18% in 2027) About 8 points of the 13.9% organic growth in 2026 and about 5.5 of the 10.5% in 2027; roughly EUR 1.3bn of the EUR 3.0bn EBITA increase Operating, external demand Largely booked: triple-digit orders in Q2 and Q3 2026 convert over 12 to 24 months; record backlog at end-Q3; management visibility "into 2028"
Rest of group volume (grid, non-residential, products, Industrial Automation) About 6 points in 2026, about 5 in 2027; roughly EUR 0.9bn of EBITA Operating, partly cyclical Short-cycle products +17% in Q3; destocking over (Rexel inventory right-sized); residential still weak
Net price-cost About zero in 2026, about +EUR 400m in 2027 (two-thirds of the 2027 margin gain) Operating, company and commodity Carry-over of 2026 increases is contractual; the cost side depends on copper, silver, tariffs and the hedging taper
Overhead leverage net of R&D step-up About 70 basis points of 2026's +120, about 30 of 2027's +75 Operating, growth-dependent Demonstrated in H1 2026; conditional on growth above about 5%
Restructuring falling from EUR 450m to 250m About +2.5 points of 2027 EPS growth Accounting, company choice Guided framework; the one positive driver nobody discusses
Share count (+25m shares, no buyback in 2027 and 2028) About −4.5% of 2027 EPS Financing Fixed in fact since 21 October 2026
Interest on about EUR 16bn of new debt plus pre-funding carry About −3.5% of 2027 EPS, about −7% of 2028 EPS, against the 2025 financing line Financing EUR 6bn fixed at 4.3%; EUR 10bn open, currency-dependent
PTC closing date (one quarter in 2027) Each quarter of slippage about −1% to −1.5% of 2027 EPS Financing and legal Vote, HSR and CFIUS outstanding; company says by Q3 2027
Currency Assumed neutral in 2027 External Unhedged translation; 10% in the dollar is about 5% of EPS

How this compares with the last four to six years. From 2019 to 2025 the drivers of the 7.2% EPS CAGR were diffuse and partly offsetting: organic growth averaging about 7% across four end markets and four regions, margin expansion of about 45 to 55 basis points a year from many small levers, with currency (−EUR 482m of EBITA in 2025 alone), rising interest and acquisitions absorbing a large part. Predictability was poor not because any one driver was large but because several medium ones moved in different directions. The next two years invert that: one large positive driver (data centres), two medium positive drivers (price-cost in 2027, overhead leverage) and two large known negatives (shares and interest). The AlphaSense dispersion report puts it the same way: "a highly concentrated set of drivers, primarily data-centre revenue conversion, pricing resilience, and the financing mechanics of the PTC acquisition."

What the concentration means for monitoring. Because the drivers are few, each has a leading indicator the Consumer can watch, and most are external to the company's own disclosure: hyperscaler quarterly capex guidance (Microsoft's USD 45.2bn missed consensus of USD 50.7bn in Q1 FY2027 on delayed third-party capacity; Alphabet's doubled to USD 51.4bn with free cash flow of −USD 9.1bn; Meta is raising USD 95bn of capital); ABB's, Vertiv's and Eaton's order prints and book-to-bill; Morgan Stanley's distributor survey (supplier price increases less widespread in Q3, 54% from 68%; lead times lengthening for 48%); benchmark switchgear lead times (40 weeks, from 60); copper and silver; the coupon on the remaining debt tranches; the PTC proxy and regulatory filings. The one driver with no good leading indicator is the financing of marginal data-centre demand, which shows first in credit (CoreWeave's 2032 bonds near a 15% yield, five-year credit default swaps at about 1,140 basis points; data-centre asset-backed spreads at multi-year highs, AAA at 200 basis points, single-A at 325) and only later in orders.

Peer cross-check. Legrand's forward estimates cluster tightly because its drivers (white space at 32% of sales, low capital intensity, no large deal) are diffuse and small; Vertiv's cluster widely because one driver (AI data-centre orders) is everything; Schneider sits between them, with the PTC financing adding a second axis of dispersion that peers do not have.

Score reasoning. Concentration in itself lowers predictability. Here it is offset because the dominant operating driver is backlog that is already booked for the 18-month window, and the financing drivers have moved from estimate to fact during October 2026. The residual risk, that data-centre financing turns orders down in 2027 with an effect on 2028 revenue, is the one that would convert a concentrated case into a volatile one.

Internal corporate change forces
