C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

A704th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The leadership has changed almost completely in two years, and every change points the same way: a board, led by former chief executive and chairman Jean-Pascal Tricoire, enforcing execution speed, cost discipline and pricing discipline through long-tenured insiders. The change has demonstrably altered execution (margins, productivity, pricing). It has not altered the capital-allocation philosophy: the PTC, Cognite and India deals continue the chairman-era conviction that software and full ownership justify high multiples. The Consumer should therefore model the new team as better operators of the same strategy, not as a new strategy.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Chief executive: the board removed Peter Herweck in November 2024 after about 18 months for "divergences in executing the strategic roadmap" and collaboration issues, and appointed Olivier Blum (joined 1993, 31 years at the group; ran Energy Management from 2022, where revenue rose from EUR 26.44bn in 2022 to EUR 31.13bn in 2024 and margin rose 170 basis points to 22.1%). His stated philosophy is "speed over perfection"; the Leadership digest's archetype is a "pushy executor" with partial strategic agency.
  • Finance and investor relations: Nathan Fast became chief financial officer on 6 April 2026 (19 years at the group; previously head of investor relations and group controlling; he helped build the December 2025 Capital Markets Day plan); Antoine Sage became head of investor relations on 1 June 2026 (20-year finance veteran from Power Systems and Secure Power). Hilary Maxson left for Oracle. The handover was flagged in advance and was smooth.
  • Board: 92% independent (11 of 12 non-employee directors); Ellyn Shook (ex-Accenture, human capital) joined in May 2026; Julia Liuson appointed observer in July 2026 for enterprise-software expertise; Linda Knoll leaves after 12 years. Tricoire holds 823,872 shares and keeps "a tight strategic grip" (Jefferies).
  • What the signal is and is not: H1 2026 margin expansion of 120 basis points, productivity of EUR 535m and support-function costs down 1.1 points of sales show the execution signal is real. The PTC deal, announced five weeks before the Q3 print and after management had said acquisitions were not a prerequisite for its targets, shows that the strategic direction remains the chairman's.
  • Weaknesses in the change: the team is entirely internal (insularity risk flagged in all four research families); no external software operator sits in the top roles while the group is absorbing AVEVA, Cognite and PTC; the Herweck severance drew only 68% say-on-pay support.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F5529th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Strategy has changed twice in ten months. The December 2025 Capital Markets Day was a well-articulated, well-resourced operational reset; the October 2026 PTC acquisition is a strategic step-change towards software that the reset did not signpost and that consumes the balance-sheet flexibility the reset had promised to shareholders. The operating strategy is clear and numeric; the software strategy is directionally clear but its economics (how three platforms integrate and when revenue synergies arrive) are not yet articulated.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • The December 2025 reset ("Advancing Energy Tech"): organic growth of 7% to 10% a year to 2030; +250 basis points of cumulative organic adjusted EBITA margin 2026 to 2030; about 100% free cash flow conversion; return on capital employed of 15% to 20% and a post-tax return on new organic capital of 25% to 30%; Digital Flywheel above 70% of revenue and software and services at 25% (doubling recurring revenue); support-function costs down 1.5 to 2 points of sales; industrial productivity of EUR 2bn to 2.5bn; Multi-Hub 2.0 delayering into four regions (from January 2026); 90% local-for-local; disposals of EUR 1bn to 1.5bn of revenue; R&D stepping up to 7% of sales; a EUR 2.5bn to 3.5bn buyback to 2030.
  • The October 2026 step-change: PTC (CAD and product-lifecycle software) closes the discrete-manufacturing software gap; Schneider says it triples its industrial-software addressable market; pro forma software and services rise to about 24% of revenue and recurring revenue to about EUR 3.5bn (still below Siemens Digital Industries' EUR 5.7bn). It comes on top of Cognite (USD 3.1bn, June 2026) and AiDASH.
  • Articulation gaps: cost synergies are specific (EUR 250m by year three); revenue synergies of about EUR 800m are not dated in the file and are disputed (Bernstein finds about EUR 300m of conventional cross-sell); the integration of AVEVA, Cognite and PTC data models is described by a former AVEVA R&D head as "highly complex".
  • Resourcing: operational priorities are well resourced (prefabricated capacity up about fourfold, asset-light Foxconn capacity, R&D step-up, AI training for 160,000 employees). The software pivot is resourced by EUR 16bn to 17bn of new debt, EUR 5bn to 6bn of new equity and a buyback pause in 2027 and 2028, so it competes directly with the shareholder-return commitment made ten months earlier.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

A851st of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Guidance has been raised decisively and repeatedly, and the mid-term ambition was set higher than the prior cycle. More important for the model, the first year of the five-year margin plan is delivering roughly half of the five-year margin target, which makes the 2030 framework look conservative and an upward revision at some point likely.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • FY26 organic growth guidance: 7% to 10% (February 2026), raised to 10% to 13% (July), raised to 12% to 14% (October).
  • FY26 organic margin guidance: +50 to 80 basis points (implied 19.1% to 19.4%), raised to +70 to 100 basis points (19.4% to 19.7%), raised to +100 to 120 basis points (19.7% to 19.9%), with organic adjusted EBITA growth of +18% to +21%.
  • Mid-term: organic growth of 7% to 10% a year extended to 2030 (Morgan Stanley notes this keeps Schneider ahead of Siemens' 6% to 9% and ABB's 5% to 7%); margin ambition of about 50 basis points a year, against a 2021 framework of 30 to 70 basis points a year (Jefferies). Deutsche Bank calls the EUR 2bn to 2.5bn productivity target "rather conservative" after EUR 535m in one half, and sees upside to the 250 basis points.
  • Arithmetic the Consumer should use: 18.7% (FY2025) + 250 basis points = about 21.2% by 2030 on a standalone organic basis (Morgan Stanley's figure); the Business Foundations digest cites 21.7%; PTC adds a further 90 to 100 basis points mechanically before integration costs (Deutsche Bank). With 100 to 120 basis points delivered in 2026, the remaining four years need only about 35 basis points a year to hit the target.
C-14To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline?A762nd of 38full answer under 2a Internal risks
C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

F3536th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Only marginally. A disposal programme exists but is small relative to the acquisitions now being added, so the perimeter is widening, not narrowing. The simplification under way is organisational (four regions, delayering) rather than portfolio.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Disposals: EUR 1bn to 1.5bn of non-core revenue to be divested between 2026 and 2030 (about 2.5% to 3.5% of 2025 revenue), following the TAMCO switchgear business in Malaysia (about EUR 200m of sales). The candidates and their margins are not identified in the file; the stated aim is to remove lower-margin, dilutive activities.
  • Additions in the same period: Motivair (consolidated 2025), Cognite, AiDASH, Shelly and PTC, the last alone adding about USD 2.7bn of 2026 revenue (about 5% of group revenue).
  • Organisational simplification: Multi-Hub 2.0 replaces a triple country-regional-global matrix with four regions (North America; Europe; China and East Asia; South Asia and International) from January 2026; the India minority buyout (Temasek's 35% of the Indian operating company for EUR 5.5bn) simplifies ownership, which Deutsche Bank sees as easing execution.
  • Peer contrast: ABB exited about USD 2bn of revenue (Dodge, Accelleron, Power Conversion) and sold Robotics to fund Rotork; Siemens Energy is separating Transformation of Industry; Eaton is spinning off Mobility. Schneider is the sector's net acquirer of perimeter.
  • For the model: treat disposals as a small negative scope effect of 0.5% to 1% of revenue a year from 2027 onwards with a mildly positive margin mix effect; the dominant scope effect is positive from acquisitions.
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C-5816th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Schneider is entering several new product markets at once, almost all adjacent to its installed base and data-centre position (liquid cooling, solid-state transformers, prefabricated modules, grid software, smart home), which is sensible. The one large entry that is not adjacent in the same way is discrete-manufacturing design software (PTC), where Schneider has no operating track record and must earn the trust it enjoys in electrical equipment. Geographic expansion is a deepening of existing hubs (India, North America, China), not new territory.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Liquid cooling: Motivair (75% for EUR 847m, about five times sales; remaining 25% due in 2028) is performing ahead of its business case, with production extended to Italy and India and a new coolant distribution unit launched; Schneider remains number two behind Vertiv.
  • 800V DC and solid-state transformers: an own solid-state transformer in customer testing; a pioneering 800V DC arc-flash study; the world's first fully software-defined medium-voltage switchgear (claimed threefold faster manufacture), deployed first at Equinix.
  • Prefabrication and capacity: asset-light Foxconn partnership for North America; a SoftBank-linked module plant at Dunkirk; Indian capacity expanded two to three times (Ambit).
  • Grid and home software: AiDASH (90% for USD 350m EV, satellite vegetation and wildfire analytics for utilities); Shelly (EUR 1.2bn, about 23 times estimated 2026 operating profit, revenue CAGR over 40%, 26.0% EBIT margin in H1 2026); an Apple partnership for smart thermostats.
  • Industrial software: Cognite (USD 3.1bn, 18 times EV to sales, 24 times ARR; the data contextualisation layer for AVEVA CONNECT) and PTC. Expert evidence is mixed: industrial customers resist migrating to PTC's Windchill+ on cost and security, and resellers describe PTC's SaaS products as "unfinished".
  • Sensibility test: the hardware adjacencies use Schneider's channel, installed base and hyperscaler relationships; the software adjacencies rely on cross-selling between historically disjointed go-to-market systems, which former sales directors describe as a weakness.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

B+625th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement. In H1 2026 the price rises were nominal: they lagged cost inflation and net price was negative. From Q3 2026 they are turning real, because the product price rate (5.1%) now exceeds the run-rate of raw-material and tariff inflation and management guides a positive full-year net price. Real pricing is therefore newly realised and largely a 2027 carry-over effect; it is not yet proven through a full cost cycle.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • H1 2026 bridge: gross product pricing +EUR 280m (about 3% on products, Bernstein), raw materials −EUR 330m, tariffs −EUR 104m: net price −EUR 154m. Pricing covered about 65% of input-cost inflation.
  • Acceleration: product pricing about 2% in Q1 (unchanged from the Q4 2025 exit rate), about 4% in Q2 (a 2.5 times step-up), 5.1% in Q3 (Deutsche Bank), against ABB at just above 2% and Legrand targeting about 3%. Pricing turned positive in China in Q1 2026 after a deflationary period.
  • H2 2026: management expects to be net price positive in value for the full year, implying an H2 net price tailwind of at least EUR 150m (Deutsche Bank estimates about a 2% EPS benefit).
  • Limits: Bernstein notes Schneider balances commodity headwinds "through pricing on less than half of their sales" (products); systems pricing runs through backlog discipline and indexation clauses, so the benefit arrives as old backlog runs off. Schneider occasionally prices aggressively to win large data-centre projects. In 2025 pricing was "a sore spot" and the gross margin fell 40 basis points organically.
  • Structural support: low-voltage products are a small share of total project cost (6% to 7% of construction cost per the Legrand file), customers in scarcity pay for delivery dates, and the Sector Digest's contracted-margin story says executed margins rise through 2028 as pre-2022 contracts roll off.

For the model: price contribution of about 3% to 4% of product sales in 2027 (carry-over) against input inflation that is flat to modestly positive gives a real price gain; flex it against a copper and silver scenario.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

F2537th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Acquisitions have stepped up dramatically, to roughly EUR 31bn to 32bn committed in about twenty months (close to a quarter of today's market value), almost all at top-of-market multiples and low initial returns. The strategic logic is coherent (a digital thread from design to operations, ownership of the industrial data layer, liquid cooling, full ownership of India), but discipline is weak by any return-on-capital standard, and integration risk is concentrated because three software platforms are being combined at once. This is the main internal negative in the case.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Deals and prices: India minority (35%) EUR 5.5bn at 44 times EV/EBITA, about EUR 1bn above initial expectations (Deutsche Bank), year-one ROIC about 2.5% (Redburn); Motivair EUR 847m at about five times sales; Cognite USD 3.1bn at 18 times EV/sales, "a new record in the manufacturing space"; AiDASH USD 350m; Shelly EUR 1.2bn at 23 times operating profit; PTC USD 23.7bn EV at a 42% premium, 21 times EBITA before synergies, ROCE about 4% in year one and above the cost of capital only by year five (Deutsche Bank). Earlier: the AVEVA minority at EUR 4.7bn and 40 times EV/EBIT. Redburn counts EUR 10.2bn of minority buyouts excluded from the company's ROCE definition (worth about 3 points of stated ROCE).
  • Logic: PTC is about 40% adjusted EBITA margin and adds 90 to 100 basis points to group margin; it fills the discrete PLM/CAD gap; "SaaS-pocalypse" valuations allowed a lower entry multiple than a year earlier; Redburn says excluding all synergies cuts its DCF value only 1%; Redburn models EPS accretion of 1.9% in 2028 and 5.3% by 2032; the company guides immediate low-single-digit EPS accretion.
  • Integration record: AVEVA's SaaS transition has run from the 2023 buyout to 2026; a former strategy director describes earlier software blends turning early customers into "guinea pigs"; former AVEVA R&D leadership calls unifying PTC, Cognite and AVEVA data models highly complex; software talent attrition over the last 18 months.
  • Market verdict: the shares fell about 10% on the announcement (EUR 17bn of value, Redburn) and about 16% after the deal (Jefferies); Deutsche Bank called the deal a negative surprise that "polluted" a clean organic story.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

F3236th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The balance sheet is being used heavily, but for acquisitions, not for returns to shareholders: leverage will rise to about 2.3 times EBITDA, new equity will be issued and the buyback is paused for two years. Shareholder returns are being stepped down in the 2027 and 2028 window that the 18-month model covers; only the progressive dividend continues.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Leverage: net financial debt rose from EUR 8,147m to EUR 13,721m during 2025 (India buyout, Motivair) and to EUR 15,360m at June 2026 (1.64 times EBITDA); PTC adds EUR 16bn to 17bn of debt, taking 2027 net debt to EBITDA to about 2.3 times (Deutsche Bank; Redburn 1.8 times; Jefferies below 2 times), with a commitment to an A-category credit rating and deleveraging to below 1.5 times within two years of consolidation.
  • Equity: EUR 5bn to 6bn of new equity, through an accelerated bookbuild per Deutsche Bank ("waiting for the cap raise", 19 October), roughly 4% dilution at today's price.
  • Buyback: the EUR 2.5bn to 3.5bn programme to 2030 delivers EUR 600m in 2026, pauses in 2027 and 2028, and is to accelerate in 2029 and 2030. For 2027 and 2028 the share count rises (new equity, employee plans of about 1.5 million shares a year) instead of falling.
  • Dividend: progressive; EUR 4.20 for FY2025 (16 consecutive years of increases); consensus EUR 4.69 for FY2026 and EUR 5.30 for FY2027; payout about 50%.
  • Contrast with peers: ABB runs net debt to EBITDA of 0.18 times with a USD 2bn buyback; Siemens Energy returns up to EUR 10bn by FY2028; Legrand self-funds acquisitions at about 12 times EBIT.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–64

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Several second-order positives are under way and are under-weighted against the PTC debate: the end of the AVEVA subscription-transition drag in 2026, the scaling of prefabricated capacity through partners, a pricing turn in China, design-in at the chip vendors, and the first evidence that Industrial Automation is recovering. Together they support upside to 2027 margins independent of PTC.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • AVEVA: the negative effect of the SaaS transition on organic growth ends in FY2026; recurring software revenue rose to 82% of agnostic software sales (from 78%); subscription ARR growing about 10% to 11%; Bernstein estimates completion adds about 50 basis points to group margin and about 3% to normalised EPS; AVEVA's margin target is 30% by 2028 from 17% in 2024.
  • Prefabrication and partners: capacity up about fourfold; Foxconn adds North American capacity without heavy capex; modular blueprints cut deployment lead times about 60%; first UL 2140 certification.
  • Chip-vendor design-in: NVIDIA DSX POD partner (October 2026), AMD Helios reference design, NVIDIA/HPE physical-AI agents.
  • Industrial Automation recovery: Q2 organic growth 11% with process automation back to growth; the 18% margin target for 2028 reaffirmed; Lexium robotics launched.
  • Services attach: digital service contracts can reach about 10% of data-centre capex.
  • Organisation and people: 61% employee shareholding participation; mandatory AI training for 160,000 staff; AI@scale programme for overhead scalability.
  • Plausible: an upgrade of the 2030 margin target (see C-13); cancellation of the remaining equity need if PTC is partly financed by disposals (not stated by the company; conjecture).
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

D5013th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. They have not. There is no activist and no controlling shareholder; investor frustration over the India and PTC prices has been expressed through the share price and broker commentary, not through governance. The chairman is the controlling actor in practice and is the author of the current strategy. Change in approach would therefore come only from the board itself, most plausibly if PTC integration falters.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • AlphaSense searches found no activist campaign; short interest is about 0% of free float; 55% of surveyed European long-only funds are overweight (Morgan Stanley), so the register is crowded with long-term holders rather than agitators.
  • Bylaws cap individual voting at 10% (15% with double votes), which raises the cost of any activist stake.
  • Morgan Stanley notes investor frustration with limited India accretion in late 2025 without activist intervention; the PTC deal triggered a 10% to 16% share-price fall.
  • The board's one intervention (removing Herweck) was about execution style, not strategy; the observer appointment of Julia Liuson strengthens software oversight rather than questioning the software strategy.
  • Plausible trigger: if the share price stays depressed and PTC integration slips, the precedent at Siemens Energy (Ananym Capital demanding a strategic review) shows that sector activists target capital allocation.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

B+726th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The positive corporate changes are strong, broad and well resourced in operations: a new insider team has reset cost, pricing and cash discipline, raised guidance twice and set credible, numeric mid-term targets that the first year is beating. They are offset, not cancelled, by a capital-allocation change that adds leverage, dilution and integration risk. The net internal picture is positive for earnings (the self-help is tangible and partly ahead of plan) and negative for the multiple (the market discounts management's acquisition discipline).

Reasoning.

  • Strength: margin delivery of +120 basis points in H1 2026 and +100 to 120 basis points guided for FY26, productivity running at about twice the 2025 rate, net price turning positive, record H1 cash.
  • Breadth: the changes touch every lever (pricing, procurement, support functions, organisation, capacity, software mix, R&D design-to-cost), and both divisions, although Industrial Automation lags.
  • Resourcing: capex, partners and R&D are funded; the people bench is deep in electrical operations and thin in software.
  • The offset: about EUR 31bn to 32bn of acquisitions at high multiples and low early returns, a two-year buyback pause and an incentive scheme that does not penalise any of it.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

C+558th of 38

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. There is a well-founded staircase, but its first steps are about removing negatives (the equity overhang, PTC uncertainty) and confirming margins, rather than new positives; the genuinely novel positives (PTC synergies, Industrial Automation and AVEVA margin targets, the 800V DC content gain, the buyback restart) arrive in months 6 to 36. The single most important dated step is the FY2026 results on 18 February 2027 (margin delivery, FY27 guidance, the annual backlog figure).

Within 6 months (to April 2027):

  • Equity raise completed (expected; removes an overhang; novelty low; well-founded: Deutsche Bank "waiting for the cap raise").
  • Closings: Shelly (tender offer supported by founders holding 57%), Cognite, AiDASH; PTC approvals progress (novelty low; well-founded).
  • Peer read-across: Legrand Q3 (5 November), Siemens Energy FY results with expected 2030 targets of 20% to 22% (11 November), Siemens (12 November), US bulk-power rules (24 December): confirms the sector's margin trajectory (novelty medium; well-founded).
  • FY2026 results, 18 February 2027: H2 margin at or above the 19.7% to 19.9% guide, net price positive, record free cash flow, the year-end backlog (disclosed annually, last EUR 25.4bn, +18%), FY27 organic guidance (novelty high; well-founded).
  • Q1 2027 revenue (about late April 2027): data-centre orders against tough comparisons (risk as well as opportunity).

Months 6 to 18 (April 2027 to April 2028):

  • PTC closing and first combined plan: synergy phasing, combined software ARR (about EUR 3.5bn pro forma), the first evidence of cross-selling (novelty high; well-founded on cost synergies, less so on revenue).
  • H1 2027 results (late July 2027): margin progress and Industrial Automation moving towards 18% (novelty medium).
  • AVEVA after the SaaS transition: re-acceleration of reported software growth as the transition drag ends in 2026 (novelty medium; well-founded: subscription mix 82%, ARR growth about 10% to 11%).
  • 800V DC proof points: Vertiv launch (early 2027), RWE and Heron pilot (April 2027), NVIDIA Rubin Ultra racks (2027), Schneider's own solid-state transformer pilots; evidence of content gain in the hybrid phase (novelty high; medium-founded).
  • Regulatory step: EU F-gas high-voltage step (2028) and Dutch and US grid programmes.
  • FY2027 results (February 2028): first full year of PTC-inclusive guidance.

Months 18 to 36 (2028 to 2029):

  • 2028 targets: Industrial Automation 18% margin, AVEVA 30% margin (novelty high; medium-founded).
  • Deleveraging below 1.5 times and a buyback restart in 2029 (novelty high; well-founded if cash conversion holds).
  • PTC accretion: Redburn models 1.9% EPS accretion in 2028 and 5.3% by 2032 (medium-founded).
  • Motivair: purchase of the remaining 25% in 2028.
  • Native 800V DC and NVIDIA Kyber (2028); solid-state transformer volume from 2029: a test of whether Schneider wins the new architecture (two-sided).
  • Possible upgrade of the 2030 targets (conjecture, supported by year-one delivery).

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

A754th of 38

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

A754th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Schneider operates in a supportive industry structure: concentrated, certification-gated, specification-led markets where the large competitors put margin first, prices have not fallen in downturns and customers rarely switch. It ranks number one or two in the activities that carry about 83% of revenue and almost all of the growth, and it is among the most advantaged players on the measure that now decides share in data centres (integrated, prefabricated, certified delivery at speed). Customers, the channel and regulators are all helping. The support is fragile in two identifiable, dated places rather than everywhere: the scarcity premium that capacity additions will erode from 2027 to 2029, and the standardised low end (China automation, Indian low voltage, commodity boxes), where entrants price to cost. Competitors are converging on Schneider's integrated model, which keeps prices rational now and erodes uniqueness later. Precedent says the sector rewards secular exposure, self-help and disciplined capital allocation together; Schneider has the first two and is currently failing the third, which explains why the share price is behaving like the underperformance archetype while the earnings behave like the outperformance archetype. For the model: hold price contribution high through 2027, fade it from 2028, keep Energy Management margins rising through 2028 on backlog repricing, and treat Industrial Automation as the structurally weaker segment.

Value chain dynamics

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?A783rd of 38full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

A744th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The route to market helps: about 45% of revenue goes through distributors and partners that rarely switch brands, absorb credit risk and give Schneider unmatched reach in buildings and short-cycle products, while data-centre and utility business is sold direct. The channel is currently healthy (destocking finished, no pre-buying). Fragility is low; the two watch-points are antitrust scrutiny of distributor pricing arrangements in France and the gradual shift of large projects from distributor-led to direct, system-level procurement, which Schneider is positioned to win but which reduces channel leverage in some segments.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Scale of channel: partners and indirect distributors generate about 45% of group revenue; Sonepar and Rexel distribute over 50% of B2B electrical supplies in France; Home Depot and Lowe's give residential access; over 600,000 ecosystem service providers are certified.
  • Channel power works for the incumbent: "distributors rarely switch brands unless severe shortages emerge"; "direct sales bypass remains rare as OEMs rely heavily on distributors to manage service and logistics"; distributors carry credit risk, supporting Schneider's working capital.
  • Channel health: European channel inventories normalised after pandemic hoarding; Jefferies found balanced sell-in and sell-out in Q1 2026; the short-cycle product acceleration to 17% in Q3 is consistent with end-demand rather than restocking alone (but the split between use, refill and price has not been disclosed, see the Wisdom Library's "use, refill or price" rule).
  • Risks: the French Competition Authority fined Schneider EUR 207m (and Legrand EUR 43m, Rexel EUR 124m) over vertical price-fixing in distribution ("prix dérogés"); the market is not paying for the channel in the data-centre wave (Rexel −11.8% over three months), which hints that value is migrating to manufacturers and away from intermediaries on large projects.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

C+5612th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Existing competitors are behaving rationally on price but aggressively on capability: each is buying or building the integrated data-centre and grid offer that Schneider leads. In the short run this helps (it validates the architecture and keeps prices high) and in the medium run it hurts (it erodes Schneider's uniqueness and adds capacity). Schneider's position is robust in specified, integrated power systems, moderately exposed in cooling and medium voltage, and weak in automation software scale and Chinese low-end automation.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Eaton: about USD 11bn of acquisitions in early 2026 (Boyd liquid cooling about USD 1.7bn of sales; Fibrebond; Resilient Power; Ultra PCS); NVIDIA 800V DC collaboration; medium-voltage solid-state transformer 2.0 with ten committed prototype orders; Electrical margins 26.3% (2025), projected 29.3% by 2030 (Redburn) against Schneider at 24.4%.
  • Vertiv: Q2 2026 sales +24% (18% organic), adjusted operating margin 22.6% (+410 basis points); UtilityInnovation acquisition extends upstream; leads liquid cooling; ceased disclosing backlog.
  • ABB: Q3 2026 Electrification orders +70% to USD 7.7bn, backlog USD 16bn, margin 25.6%; strong in medium voltage and DC protection; ROCE 29.9%; low leverage (0.18 times) gives USD 13bn to 20bn of acquisition capacity.
  • Legrand: white-space leader (32% of sales in data centres), seven acquisitions in H1 2026; but some operators move complex equipment to Schneider.
  • Siemens: software scale (Digital Industries ARR EUR 5.7bn) and a DC portfolio (SENTRON solid-state breaker); Smart Infrastructure orders +42% in Q3.
  • Fragility: low in core power distribution; moderate where Schneider is number two (liquid cooling, MV versus ABB); high in automation versus Siemens and Chinese local suppliers.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?A704th of 38full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

A661st of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Disruption is acting hardest on Schneider's customers (hyperscalers redesigning power trains and bypassing the grid), on adjacent suppliers (UPS-only, low-voltage-only and grid-tied players) and on software incumbents, and least on integrated system providers. Each force currently leaves Schneider's position intact or improved for the forecast horizon, because Schneider sells into every architecture (AC, hybrid, DC, grid-tied and behind-the-meter). The fragility is a slow, long-dated one in Secure Power and a valuation-level one in software.

Reasoning, by actor.

  • Demand (hyperscalers and colocation): shifting to 800V DC (from 2027 to 2028 in planning), prefabrication (20% to 60% of builds by 2030) and behind-the-meter power (22% of capacity under construction) to bypass five-to-seven-year grid queues. Effect on Schneider: more content per megawatt, more single-sourcing; positive.
  • Competition: consolidating into integrated offers; Eaton and Vertiv replicating; effect: erodes uniqueness, keeps prices rational; neutral to mildly negative.
  • Regulators and politicians: dating grid demand by law, slowing data-centre permits; effect: positive demand, timing risk.
  • Suppliers: silicon carbide becomes mandatory for 800V DC (China controls about half of supply), copper usage falls up to 45% in 800V designs (positive for input costs, negative for copper-intensive busway content), grain-oriented steel stays scarce; effect: mixed.
  • Utilities: losing load to behind-the-meter; investing USD 1.4 trillion in the US; effect: positive for Schneider's grid business either way.
  • Software users: agentic AI changing seat-based pricing; effect: valuation risk for the software segment, limited earnings risk for industrial software (deep domain data).

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B789th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. In its core activities Schneider is number one or two: global leader in low- and medium-voltage electrical distribution systems for buildings and data centres, the most specified power vendor inside data centres, and number two to Vertiv in data-centre cooling. It is a second-tier player in industrial automation hardware (behind Siemens and Rockwell, and losing ground in China to domestic suppliers) and, even after PTC, number two to Siemens in industrial software by recurring revenue. About 83% of revenue (Energy Management) therefore sits in leadership positions; the 17% in Industrial Automation does not.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Data-centre power: Schneider represents more than 20% of power spend for 64% of surveyed procurement managers, against 36% for Vertiv (Bernstein survey); the top three equipment suppliers (Schneider, Vertiv, Delta) command 80% of power and cooling share; Schneider holds the "highest customer specification share" through EcoStruxure.
  • Cooling: more than 20% of cooling spend for 60% of managers (Vertiv 42%, Johnson Controls 30%); in liquid cooling Vertiv is above 20% share and Schneider below 20% "but close behind".
  • Medium voltage and grid: ABB claims global leadership in medium voltage and remains top competitor in North American utility connections; Siemens Energy, Hitachi Energy and GE Vernova lead high voltage, where Schneider is not a major player. Schneider's grid position is in medium voltage and grid software (One Digital Grid, AiDASH).
  • Scale: EUR 40.2bn of 2025 revenue, about EUR 33.1bn in Energy Management, against ABB's USD 33.2bn group, Eaton's Electrical sector, Legrand's EUR 9.5bn and Vertiv (backlog of USD 15.0bn at end-2025); Schneider's Energy Management is the largest pure electrical-distribution franchise in the peer group.
  • Automation and software: Siemens holds 51% of China's medium-to-large PLC market; Inovance holds 37% of China's AC servo market; Siemens Digital Industries software ARR EUR 5.7bn against Schneider's pro forma about EUR 3.5bn after PTC.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C+7610th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Schneider's profitable markets are concentrated oligopolies with steep share distributions: data-centre power and cooling, data-centre electrical components and medium-voltage equipment are all above 30% for the top five, typically well above 50%, and the top five dwarf numbers six to ten. Its building-products market is a long-tailed mix of global majors and thousands of local mid-size players, but at the premium, specified end it is also concentrated. Concentration is structurally supportive of price discipline.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Data-centre power and cooling: top three 80% (Schneider, Vertiv, Delta).
  • Critical data-centre electrical components: the top five electrical players supply about 70% (ABB file).
  • Data-centre busbar: Starline, Schneider, Eaton, ABB and Siemens account for about 64% of global revenue, with a long tail (Chint, C&S, EAE, Zhejiang Tianzheng).
  • PLCs: top five 77% (Siemens 20.1%); medium-to-large PLCs in China, Siemens 51%.
  • Building low voltage: Legrand counts over 6,000 mid-size industry players, and about half of its EUR 150bn addressable market is held by local small and medium enterprises; fragmented national standards keep it local. Steepness: for wiring devices and distribution boards in each country, two or three majors (Schneider, Legrand, ABB, Siemens, Eaton or Hager locally) dominate the specified channel.
  • Gas turbines and high-voltage transformers (adjacent, not Schneider's): three players hold 85% of high-capacity turbines; transformers are constrained by few qualified suppliers. Scarcity in these adjacent nodes lengthens the whole chain's pricing window.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

B727th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Market shares in Schneider's core Western electrical markets are stable: new entrants have taken little share over three to five years because certification, specification and installed-base lock-in block them. Shares are moving in two places: among the incumbents in data centres (Eaton and Vertiv gaining in North America and liquid cooling as everyone grows fast), and in China automation and Indian low voltage, where domestic entrants have taken substantial share.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Stable: "distributors rarely switch brands unless severe shortages emerge"; vendor switching among hyperscalers is "extremely rare"; installed electrical systems last over 20 years and are maintained through about 400 Schneider service centres; replacing core PLC or SCADA systems is rare because of the cost of operational failure; UL, IEEE and IEC certifications plus one-year utility qualification tests block unproven suppliers.
  • Incumbent share shifts: Eaton's data-centre orders up about 85% in Q2 2026 and "gaining market share in North America"; ABB Electrification orders up 70% in Q3; Legrand's data-centre sales up over 30%; Schneider's own data-centre orders triple-digit. In a market growing at 15% to 30%, share shifts of a few points are normal and do not damage absolute growth.
  • Entrant share gains: Chinese domestic brands reached 59% of servos and 61% of industrial robots in China; Inovance expanded to 37% of China's AC servo market in Q2 2026 and 14.3% of small PLCs; South Korean makers took a 19% US transformer import share; Shanghai Liangxin supplies Eaton and Vertiv in North America; in India, Legrand fell from number one to number four in switches as domestic competitors matched quality.
  • Implication: the entrant threat is real in standardised boxes and in China and India; it is small in specified systems, medium voltage and data-centre integration, which is where Schneider's growth and margin come from.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

B728th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Share is consolidating towards the most advantaged players, defined now as those that can deliver integrated, prefabricated, certified systems at speed, and Schneider is among the two or three most advantaged on that definition (with Vertiv, and Eaton closing). In software, share consolidates to scale (Siemens), and Schneider is buying its way into the leading group. In Chinese automation, share consolidates to local cost leaders, and Schneider is not among them.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Data centres: pre-integrated power modules "will dominate procurement as customers seek to bypass multi-vendor integration costs"; modular builds rising from 20% to 60% of all builds by 2030 let integrated OEMs "capture bigger wallet shares and higher margins"; Bernstein argues prefabrication drives single-sourcing and "underappreciated market share gains" for Schneider; narrower portfolios at Siemens, ABB and Legrand "dilute their modular upside".
  • Gatekeepers: design-in with NVIDIA and AMD reference designs and five-year capacity agreements (Compass, USD 3bn) lock share into today's leaders.
  • Peers buying the same advantage: Eaton (Boyd, Fibrebond) and Vertiv (UtilityInnovation) are spending to replicate Schneider's integrated stack; Legrand buys white-space breadth (Girtz, Avtron). This confirms where the advantage lies.
  • Software: Siemens is the scale leader (EUR 5.7bn ARR, 64% of Digital Industries backlog in software); Schneider's PTC deal is an attempt to join the consolidators.
  • China automation: domestic substitution is the trend; Schneider defends by bundling medium voltage with low voltage (an expert says this mitigates low-voltage price competition), but Western peers "face market share challenges in the value-for-money segment".
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

A823rd of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The industry is margin-first and benign. The large competitors run return-on-capital or margin-based management systems, add capacity cautiously and defend price in downturns; industry prices have not fallen materially when volumes fell. Ironically, Schneider is described as the most growth-first of the majors, occasionally accepting hardware margin dilution to win lifecycle business, which is a source of share gain rather than of industry price weakness.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Margin-first peers: ABB's decentralised "ABB Way" ties about 50% of business-line manager bonuses to local margin and uses ROCE in short- and long-term incentives; Legrand is "bottom-line hungry", rejects price moves that risk loyalty and held margins above 20% through the 2020 disruption; Siemens and ABB are "factory-driven and product-driven organizations where defending unit margin is prioritized"; Eaton runs "capacity discipline" with high-teens margins in Electrical Americas.
  • Prices in downturns: Legrand "has never cut its average prices in the past twenty years"; in Q1 2024, sales fell 5.6% while Legrand's adjusted margin held 20.5%; the 2023 to 2025 destocking did not produce list-price cuts in Western low voltage; Chinese automation suppliers are raising prices in 2026, not cutting.
  • Capacity conduct: leaders "limit capacity convergence to keep supply tighter than demand" (ABB file); Siemens Energy refuses greenfield expansion.
  • Schneider's own stance: a market-driven organisation prioritising top-line growth that accepts temporary margin dilution on some hardware categories to capture high-margin lifecycle software and services (expert, August 2026); occasionally aggressive on large data-centre bids.
  • Exception: Indian transformer prices have historically crashed 25% to 30% when Chinese and Tier-2 capacity arrived (Edelweiss; Ambit), showing what happens in standardised, uncertified products.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

B+707th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: is there 'Michael O'Leary' instability, and would competitors launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement. The attitude has been stable and has, if anything, become more disciplined over three years as scarcity rewarded patience. No major competitor shows O'Leary-style instability. The risk of a price attack is not from a rival sensing Schneider's weakness; it is from the system as a whole when the capacity now being built (2027 to 2029) meets a slower demand growth rate, and from Chinese suppliers in standardised products. That is a cyclical-normalisation risk, not a behavioural one.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Stability: margin trajectories of all majors rose through 2023 to 2026 (Eaton Electrical +800 basis points since 2019 to 26.3%; ABB Electrification 13.4% in 2019 to 25.6% in Q3 2026; Siemens Energy Grid 19.9%); pricing increased in 2026 across Siemens, Inovance and Schneider.
  • Capacity build: Eaton about USD 1.5bn across 24 North American facilities (its Electrical Americas margin fell 440 basis points to 25.6% in Q1 2026 on ramp inefficiencies); Siemens over USD 400m; Vertiv doubling chiller capacity in Italy; Siemens Energy +50% transformer and switchgear capacity by 2030; Bernstein counts about USD 6bn ramping over 18 months, after which "equipment lead times will collapse, exposing suppliers to severe pricing deflation" (the bear view).
  • Sceptic views: a former Schneider senior vice president warns of "intense pricing pressure as supply catches up" over 18 to 24 months; Ambit warns of emerging overcapacity by 2030.
  • Counter-evidence: capacity is being added with customer commitments and capacity-reservation agreements; global advanced transformer capacity remains about 10% short of demand even after expansions (Deutsche Bank); the Industry Digest notes pricing for people and installed bases lasts longer than pricing for factory slots.
  • For the model: assume price contribution fades from about 4% to 5% on products in 2026 to 2027 towards 1% to 2% by 2029 as lead times normalise, with systems margins held up through 2028 by backlog repricing.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

A763rd of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The industry structure is highly supportive: concentrated, certified, specification-led markets with margin-first competitors, low switching and long asset lives. The support is fragile in two specific places. First, the current excess returns partly reflect scarcity, which capacity additions will erode from 2027. Second, the standardised, low-end layers (basic low-voltage boxes, servos, small PLCs) are exposed to Chinese and Indian entrants. The specified, integrated and serviced layers, where most of Schneider's profit sits, are robust.

Reasoning.

  • Supportive: C-25 (top five well above 30%), C-26 (stable shares outside China and India), C-28 (no price cuts in downturns), plus high regulatory and certification barriers and a 20-year installed base.
  • Fragile point one, scarcity: data-centre and grid pricing currently attaches to delivery dates; the Sector Digest says "capacity dates set the window and cannot be pulled forward", which also means the window will close on a known schedule (2027 to 2029).
  • Fragile point two, low-end: domestic Chinese share in servos and robots, Korean and Chinese transformer exports, and Indian switch-market share loss at Legrand show that where the product is a standard box, price follows the lowest-cost producer.
  • Schneider-specific mitigants: about 83% of revenue in Energy Management, with growth concentrated in integrated systems, software and services; the local-for-local hubs; bundling of medium and low voltage in China.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

C5812th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Substitutes are a moderate and growing long-term threat to specific product lines (AC UPS and switchgear in AI data centres, physical grid equipment, electromechanical breakers), but in the forecast horizon most substitutes are complements that Schneider also sells. The change is gradual and Schneider is building the substitutes itself.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • 800V DC with solid-state transformers and battery backup units substitutes centralised AC UPS rooms, step-down transformers, AC switchgear and rack PDUs; timing post-2028 to 2029; Secure Power is 32% of Schneider's data-centre revenue (Bernstein); Schneider has its own solid-state transformer and DC portfolio.
  • Solid-state circuit breakers (Siemens SENTRON, up to 1,000 times faster) substitute electromechanical breakers in DC environments; costs are up to ten times higher today.
  • Grid-enhancing technologies and software could unlock 23 GW of capacity (Morgan Stanley), reducing the need for behind-the-meter generation and some physical equipment; Schneider sells such software (its digital twins allowed Southern California Edison to double grid capacity without a physical substation rebuild).
  • Software-defined switchgear (Schneider's own) decouples intelligence from hardware, compressing hardware content but adding software content.
  • Agentic AI as a substitute for seat-based software: management says about 90% of its software revenue is insulated; consumption pricing at AVEVA.

4a

Business quality

A724th of 388 answers
Judgement72C-4172C-4274C-4368C-4468C-4564C-4640C-4770
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

A724th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Schneider's competitive advantage is strong and, where it matters most for the next four years, widening. Its moat is not a network effect but a combination of specification and installed-base switching costs, regional manufacturing scale, certified prefabrication capacity and the broadest integrated portfolio in the industry, which together supply the thing customers are now paying for: certainty of delivery and integration at gigawatt scale. Customer surplus utility is demonstrably not inferior and is probably leading in data-centre power; pricing power is being applied in 2026 without loss of share; and the market is large enough (about EUR 600bn by 2030, growing 6% to 7%) to support 7% to 10% organic growth without heroic share gains. The weaknesses are specific: number two in liquid cooling, long-dated exposure of Secure Power to 800V DC, a software scale gap to Siemens that PTC narrows only partly, and no practice of sharing scale economies, so loyalty rests on switching costs rather than on price trust. Because most of the improvement in outputs through 2028 is mechanical (backlog repricing, a fixed overhead base, the end of the AVEVA transition, a growing installed base), the Consumer can forecast Energy Management margins with reasonable confidence and should haircut the software synergy line.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

B+726th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Schneider's moat is a combination of specification and installed-base switching costs, delivery capability at scale (local-for-local hubs and prefabrication) and portfolio breadth, reinforced by certification barriers. It is strong and, in data centres and grid, widening, because the scarce resource customers now pay for (time to power, single-source integration) is exactly what this combination supplies. It is narrowing in three places: liquid cooling (number two), traditional UPS over the long term (800V DC), and automation software scale against Siemens. The "Digital Flywheel" label (62% of revenue) is a marketing description, not a network-effect moat; the real software moat is switching cost in the data layer.

Advantage by advantage.

  • Customer-related (strong, widening): risk-averse hyperscale designers prefer proven brands; Schneider is the most specified power vendor (more than 20% of power spend for 64% of procurement managers); installed electrical systems last over 20 years and are maintained through about 400 service centres; experts say "comprehensive service networks set Schneider apart from Siemens and ABB hardware offerings"; EcoStruxure training creates switching costs; the OSIsoft PI historian and AVEVA CONNECT make switching "without massive software disruption" impractical.
  • Production and delivery (strong, widening): about 90% of products made and sourced in-region, which insulated Schneider from tariffs better than Legrand (45% of Legrand's US costs imported); prefabricated capacity grown about fourfold; first UL 2140 certification for prefabricated data-centre power; asset-light capacity through Foxconn; Schneider and Vertiv are described as the most open partners on factory expansion.
  • Scale and breadth (strong, stable): the only player offering low and medium voltage, secure power, cooling, building and grid software and industrial software together; horizontal organisation packaging cross-business solutions, outperforming ABB's vertical divisions in data-centre bids (expert); R&D of about EUR 2.4bn a year.
  • Regulatory and standards (medium, widening near term): SF6-free leadership (80% substitution rate) ahead of the EU F-gas steps; fragmented national standards; utility pre-qualification.
  • Knowledge and culture (medium, mixed): low power distance and local empowerment, but software talent attrition and integration bugs show the knowledge moat in software is thinner than in electrical engineering.
  • Narrowing: Vertiv leads liquid cooling; Eaton and Vertiv are replicating grid-to-chip; Secure Power is 32% of data-centre revenue and exposed after 2029; China low-end automation share lost to Inovance and others.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

A744th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Schneider is the largest focused electrical-distribution and energy-management company in the world (EUR 40.2bn of 2025 revenue, about EUR 45bn in 2026), with about 160,000 employees. Scale confers four real advantages: the ability to duplicate research, factories and supply chains in four regional hubs; purchasing weight; credibility as a single source for gigawatt-scale campuses; and an R&D budget no focused peer can match. It also carries diseconomies of complexity (a matrix organisation, headcount that grew faster than peers) that the current programme is trying to remove.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Relative size: ABB USD 33.2bn (group, three segments); Siemens industrial business about EUR 76bn but a conglomerate with only about 5% of sales in data centres; Legrand EUR 9.5bn; Vertiv and Eaton's Electrical sector smaller than Schneider's Energy Management (EUR 33.1bn).
  • What it buys: duplication of active R&D, factories and suppliers across North America, Europe, China and India (a cost a smaller peer cannot carry); EUR 11bn of annual purchases across 20,000 suppliers; R&D of 5.8% of revenue (against ABB's 4.5% of non-order R&D) rising to 7%; over 600,000 certified ecosystem partners; capacity to fund prefabrication growth of about 270% in two years and to sign five-year capacity agreements.
  • Single-source credibility: large, complex campuses favour vendors who can carry delivery risk; a data-centre operator moved its complex equipment to Schneider from Legrand.
  • Diseconomies: Energy Management headcount +24% over three years; productivity fell to 0.7% of sales after 2020; Redburn notes the decentralised multi-hub setup "creates high execution complexity, requiring extraordinary leadership to drive synergies"; experts flag bureaucracy deterring software engineers.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

B+686th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Inferiority is disproved in Schneider's core offers, and in integrated data-centre power and prefabricated systems the evidence points to leadership. The surplus utility comes from delivery certainty, integration (one vendor across power, cooling and software) and lifecycle service, not from lower price. Inferiority cannot be disproved in liquid cooling (Vertiv leads) or in automation software breadth (Siemens), and acquired-software quality has been a weak point for early customers.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Customer choice evidence: specification surveys (64% versus Vertiv's 36% for power; 60% versus 42% for cooling); a global data-centre operator moved complex equipment purchases from Legrand to Schneider; Vantage uses Schneider as primary technology partner for standardised hyperscale designs; Switch and Compass signed multi-year capacity agreements; Equinix deployed the first software-defined medium-voltage switchgear; Northumbria Healthcare NHS Trust standardised on EcoStruxure; 87% of surveyed customers likely to purchase Schneider's solid-state transformer.
  • Economic utility: combined power and process installations can pay back in under six months; modular blueprints cut deployment lead times about 60%; prefabrication compresses engineering and deployment by 30% to 60%; digital services can be about 10% of data-centre capex.
  • Weak spots: liquid-cooling share under 20% against Vertiv's over 20%; "persistent software bugs and debugging cycles" in past software blends; disjointed go-to-market between hardware and acquired software (former sales directors); industrial customers resisting PTC's Windchill+.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

B+646th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Pricing power is demonstrated and is being applied in 2026, with no visible erosion of customer preference (orders and share are rising at the same time as prices). It is not unconditional: in 2025 Schneider under-priced against cost inflation, its pricing lever acts directly on less than half of sales (products), and in large data-centre tenders it sometimes buys share with price. The 2026 evidence moves pricing power from conjecture to application, but it has not yet been tested in a period of falling scarcity.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Application in 2026: product price increases from about 2% (Q1) to about 4% (Q2) to 5.1% (Q3), more than twice ABB's group pricing (just above 2%) and ahead of Legrand (about 3%); tariff costs passed through; China pricing turned positive in Q1 after a deflationary period; full-year net price positive guided, implying at least EUR 150m of H2 price-cost tailwind.
  • No erosion of utility: Q3 organic growth of 16.9% (sector-leading), triple-digit data-centre orders and rising specification share alongside the price rises.
  • Mechanisms: indexation clauses in project contracts; low-voltage content a small share of total project cost; scarcity of delivery slots; strong brand trust (Redburn: "strong pricing power over cycles, driven by leading market positions in concentrated markets").
  • Limits: 2025 was described as a pricing "transition year" with organic gross margin −40 basis points; H1 2026 net price −EUR 154m; Bernstein: an "uphill battle" balancing commodities through pricing on less than half of sales; a Vertiv expert says Schneider "occasionally competes aggressively on price to win large data center projects"; low-end transactional segments (surge protection, generic components) face high price competition in distribution.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

C4016th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Only to a limited extent. Schneider does not state or practise a "scale economies shared" model of passing productivity gains through as lower prices; it keeps productivity in margin and prices to value and scarcity. The de facto sharing it does practise is through integration and lifecycle value (lower total cost of ownership, energy savings, faster deployment) and through occasional aggressive hardware pricing on large projects to win the service tail. That is commercially sensible today but it is not the reinforcing, customer-loyalty-building moat that true scale-sharing creates.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Productivity of EUR 535m in H1 2026 went to margin (organic margin +120 basis points) and to offsetting input inflation, not to price reductions.
  • Messaging emphasises "unparalleled efficiency for its global customers" and decarbonisation outcomes (consult, digitise, decarbonise), i.e. customer value through energy efficiency rather than price.
  • An expert describes Schneider as "accepting temporary margin dilution on certain hardware categories to capture high-margin lifecycle software services", which is sharing at the point of sale with recapture later.
  • The asset-light partner model (Foxconn) and prefabrication shorten customer timelines by up to 60%, a time-value benefit rather than a price benefit.
  • Contrast: in a scarcity market, sharing scale economies through price would be value-destructive; the absence of sharing is therefore not a criticism of the current cycle, but it means customer loyalty rests on switching costs and performance rather than on a price-trust bond.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

A704th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. For 2026 to 2028, most of the improvement in Schneider's financial outputs is mechanical: backlog repricing, a fixed support-function base growing more slowly than revenue, the completion of the AVEVA subscription transition, a growing installed base feeding services, and law-dated regulatory demand. The parts that require heroics are concentrated in software (integrating PTC and Cognite and hitting EUR 800m of revenue synergies) and in Industrial Automation's margin target. The Consumer can therefore weight margin forecasts for Energy Management with high confidence and the software and synergy lines with low confidence.

Mechanical forces, each period:

  • Backlog repricing: the Sector Digest's "contracted margin" story (executed margins rise through 2028 as pre-2022 contracts run off).
  • Operating leverage on a fixed base: support-function costs were about EUR 9.4bn in 2025; holding them flat while revenue grows 10% lowers the ratio by about 2 points without any new initiative (the target is 1.5 to 2 points by 2030).
  • Software mix: recurring revenue 82% of agnostic software; the SaaS transition drag ends in 2026 (Bernstein: about 50 basis points of group margin over time).
  • Installed base: every year's record shipments enlarge a 20-year service tail.
  • Regulation: SF6 steps in 2026, 2028, 2030 and 2032 add replacement demand on a known calendar.
  • Price carry-over: 2026 increases carry into 2027.

Forces working against, also mechanical: capacity additions across the sector (2027 to 2029), lead-time normalisation, R&D stepping up to 7% of sales, and currency translation.

Heroic: PTC and Cognite integration and revenue synergies; Industrial Automation from 14% to 18% margin by 2028; AVEVA from 17% to 30% by 2028.

Management quality

Group judgement

Company - Management

D5424th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is a strong, ambitious, execution-focused team that is delivering the best operating year in the cycle: growth leadership, a pricing and productivity reset, two guidance raises and the start of a margin catch-up, on top of a resilient, well-invested multi-hub operating system. The chief executive is an enabler of execution, and the communication of the operating model is clear and quantified. The weaknesses are equally clear and concentrated: capital allocation (about EUR 31bn to 32bn of expensive acquisitions in twenty months, a net-issuer share count, a buyback paused near a valuation low), incentives that do not test returns on capital, an insider team thin in software as three software platforms are combined, and a culture that is congruent in electrical operations but not in software. For the second-set model, management quality justifies leaning towards the upper half of operating guidance and confidence in the 2026 to 2028 Energy Management margin path; it does not justify confidence in revenue synergies, in an early buyback restart, or in multiple expansion before integration is proven.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

B+786th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Ambition is high and is the most growth-oriented among the large European electrical groups. Management wants Schneider to be "the ultimate energy technology partner" at the convergence of electrification, automation and digital, to grow 7% to 10% a year to 2030, to double recurring revenue and to move from a hardware company to one where software and services are a quarter of revenue. That is a bigger and bolder vision than ABB's, Siemens' or Legrand's; only Vertiv targets faster growth, as a narrower pure play.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Words: "we must go much faster, we have unique technology"; "we will need to accelerate the growth of Schneider"; the strategy is "at halftime".
  • Targets against peers: organic growth 7% to 10% (Siemens 6% to 9%, ABB 5% to 7%, Legrand 6% to 8%, Vertiv 20% to 22%); +250 basis points of margin; Digital Flywheel above 70% of revenue.
  • Acts of ambition: PTC (USD 23.7bn EV, about 59% of Schneider's market value at the time, in the top 2% of European industrial deal sizes relative to acquirer scale); its own solid-state transformer; the world's first software-defined medium-voltage switchgear; prefabricated capacity up about fourfold; a 5 GW French AI infrastructure partnership.
  • Culture of push: experts describe Schneider as "exceptionally aggressive on commercial growth" against Legrand's "bottom-line hungry" culture, and as market-driven against factory-driven Siemens and ABB.
  • Caution: ambition has outrun discipline in capital allocation (C-64).
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C6413th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. The hardware and data-centre strategy is smart, bold and moat-widening: integrated, prefabricated, local-for-local delivery is exactly what the current environment rewards, and partnerships keep it capital-light and adaptable. The software strategy is directionally right (owning the industrial data layer and the digital thread from design to operation) but unproven in economics, and it is being executed at prices that make success necessary rather than optional. The strategy is dynamic across most plausible environments (it sells into AC and DC, grid-tied and behind-the-meter, tariffs and no tariffs), with one exception: a combined AI-capex pause and software integration stumble, which the strategy has made more damaging by adding leverage.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Moat-widening choices: multi-hub duplication insulating against tariffs and decoupling (90% local); prefabrication and UL certification; design-in with NVIDIA and AMD; asset-light capacity (Foxconn); SF6-free technology ahead of regulation; liquid cooling (Motivair, ahead of plan).
  • Growth as priority: a growth-first commercial culture and a 7% to 10% framework; "speed over perfection".
  • Range of environments: 800V DC: own solid-state transformer and sidecar roadmap aligned with NVIDIA; behind-the-meter: content rises; grid-led growth: Infrastructure "acting as an independent growth driver"; Chinese competition: medium-voltage bundling.
  • Questionable choices: three software platforms (AVEVA, Cognite, PTC) to be unified at once; revenue synergies of EUR 800m disputed (Bernstein: about EUR 300m conventional cross-sell); a strategy announced in December 2025 as organic ("acquisitions not a prerequisite") pivoted within ten months to a USD 23.7bn deal; Siemens still holds a larger software and automation hardware position, so the software bet starts from number two.
  • Test from the Wisdom Library: "linked businesses are not a conglomerate" asks whether the divisions actively improve each other's core offering; in data centres the answer is yes (power plus cooling plus software); between PTC's CAD/PLM and Schneider's electrical hardware it is not yet demonstrated.
C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

D6419th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Olivier Blum is a strong operator and an enabler of execution, judged by actions: he scaled Energy Management before his appointment and, as chief executive, has delivered record revenue, a pricing and productivity reset, two guidance raises and the start of a margin catch-up. He is not yet proven as a capital allocator or strategic redesigner; his largest strategic act (PTC) was received as a negative surprise and is financed at the expense of shareholder returns. On the evidence he is an animal-CEO-type operator with partial strategic agency, which suits the 2026 to 2028 margin story and leaves the software bet as the open question.

Actions in evidence.

  • Before: Energy Management revenue EUR 26.44bn (2022) to EUR 31.13bn (2024), margin +170 basis points to 22.1%; earlier he built the India franchise and led China strategy; five years on the AVEVA board.
  • As chief executive (from November 2024): FY2025 revenue EUR 40.2bn (+8.9% organic), free cash flow above EUR 4bn, margin +50 basis points (bottom of range); H1 2026 +14% organic, +120 basis points of margin, record free cash flow; a new pricing methodology from 1 January 2026; Multi-Hub 2.0 delayering; partnerships with Foxconn, SoftBank, NVIDIA, AMD, Apple and Deloitte; Q3 2026 +16.9% organic and a second guidance raise.
  • Capital allocation under him: India buyout (EUR 5.5bn, about EUR 1bn above expectations), Cognite, AiDASH, Shelly, PTC, buyback paused.
  • Style: "pushy executor"; high operational drive; low historical willingness to challenge board-set metrics (expert); lifelong insider (insularity risk).
  • Market verdict: Deutsche Bank upgraded on his productivity focus in 2025; Bernstein said FY2025 rebuilt credibility with long-only investors; PTC then cost 10% to 16% of the share price.
C-57b

How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

D5520th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. The team is experienced, stable and deep in electrical operations and finance, but it is built almost entirely by internal promotion, and hiring external talent, particularly in software and AI, has not visibly been a priority. For a strategy whose riskiest leg is software integration, that is the team's main gap.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Team: chief financial officer Nathan Fast (19 years, controlling, supply-chain finance, investor relations); head of investor relations Antoine Sage (20 years); executive vice presidents Frédéric Godemel (Energy Management/power systems), Manish Kumar (Secure Power and data centres), Pankaj Sharma (software and services, seen as a future leader), Melton Chang and Gwenaelle Huet; an executive committee of 15 with 33% women and 53% non-French members; Esther Finidori promoted to chief sustainability officer; regional appointments (India chief financial officer and HR head, Canada sales and marketing) all internal.
  • Succession: structured annual review; deep bench (Leadership digest rates key-person risk low).
  • Gaps: voluntary turnover up to 13.9% (2025) from 12.6%; "significant" loss of AI and software talent to start-ups over 18 months, citing pay and bureaucracy; insider-heavy promotion "risks creating a culture of insularity" (all families); no external software chief executive inside the top team as three software businesses are combined; PTC's sales-channel personnel are a named integration dependency.
  • Mitigants: board observer Julia Liuson (enterprise software); Ellyn Shook (human capital) on the board; AI training for 160,000 employees.
C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

A823rd of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Very well. The multi-hub model, with research, factories and suppliers duplicated in four regions and 90% local production, was built before the tariff shocks and is why Schneider absorbed them better than peers; prefabricated, cooling and Indian capacity have been expanded ahead of demand; R&D is rising; and the installed base is served by a large service network. The one buffer that has been thinned, deliberately, is the balance sheet.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Supply-chain resilience: duplicated R&D, factories and supply lines across North America, Europe, China and India; Singapore electronics hub; localised copper cathodes; dual sourcing; strategic safety stocks; real-time control towers.
  • Capacity ahead of demand: prefabricated capacity over one million square feet on eight sites (about 270% growth in two years); Indian capacity two to three times; liquid-cooling production in Italy and India; Foxconn and contract manufacturers for flexibility; capex lifted to 3.6% of revenue in 2026 consensus.
  • Investment in the future: R&D at 5.8% of revenue rising to 7%; own solid-state transformer; software-defined switchgear; AI training for 160,000 staff.
  • Service buffer: about 400 service centres maintaining a 20-year installed base.
  • Thinned buffer: leverage rising to about 2.3 times; talent in software thinner than the strategy needs.
  • Peer contrast: Legrand imports 45% of its US cost base; ABB under-invests in R&D (4.5%); Siemens Energy relied on state guarantees in 2023.
