C-60

How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

B+625th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Communication of the operating model is clear and unusually quantified (organic growth, organic margin in basis points, currency and scope shown separately, productivity in euros, a net-price bridge, a support-function ratio target). Communication of the earnings transmission at quarter level is weaker than at North American peers (margins only half-yearly, backlog only annually, no quarterly orders), and communication of capital allocation has been poor (a PTC deal at odds with prior "organic" messaging and revenue synergies without a credible bridge). The Consumer can rely on the company's bridges for the margin model but must rely on brokers and peers for quarterly order and backlog signals.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Helpful: the H1 2026 bridge (gross price +EUR 280m, raw materials −EUR 330m, tariffs −EUR 104m, productivity +EUR 535m, tariff refund EUR 100m); currency guidance updated each quarter (−EUR 750m to −850m, then −EUR 400m to −500m, then −EUR 300m to −400m); restructuring guided (about EUR 450m); the December 2025 framework with numeric 2030 targets.
  • Unhelpful: adjusted EBITA disclosed semi-annually (investors "must wait until February" to confirm productivity, Morgan Stanley); backlog disclosed annually only (Redburn notes quarterly channel checks are needed); revenue synergies of about EUR 800m for PTC without timing; the market's reading of PTC as a "negative surprise" (Deutsche Bank) after management said acquisitions were not a prerequisite.
  • Tone: consistently confident and disciplined ("executing our plans precisely as presented"), with management's informal commentary running ahead of the formal guide (see C-85).
  • New team: investor relations changed hands twice in 2026 (Fast to chief financial officer, Sage to head of investor relations), but continuity of disclosure is expected.
C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

C-6216th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, whatever the environment?

Judgement. The recent record is one of large delivery: 2026 is a year of beats and raises on growth, margin, productivity and cash. The mid-term record is good on growth and mixed on margin: Schneider met or beat its revenue guidance in 2023 to 2025, but landed at the bottom of its margin range in 2025, let gross margin fall, and expanded Energy Management margins far less than ABB and Eaton from 2019 to 2024. In the tougher 2020 environment it protected margins well. The pattern is a company that has always delivered top line and is now, for the first time in the cycle, delivering margin catch-up too.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • 2026: Q1 organic 11.2% (1.1 points above consensus); Q2 16.5% (about 6 points above); H1 margin 19.3% (80 basis points above consensus), productivity EUR 272m above consensus, free cash flow 17% above; Q3 16.9% (310 basis points above); guidance raised twice.
  • 2025: organic growth 8.9% within 7% to 10%; Q4 10.7% against 7.2% consensus; margin +50 basis points, the bottom of 50 to 80; gross margin −40 basis points organically; reported EPS +3% (currency).
  • 2024: organic growth tracked the 6% to 8% target; Redburn's September 2024 title notes "slight P&L miss, but 17% FCF cut".
  • Longer run: Jefferies said in 2020 that ambitious margin targets were unreachable without divestments; Energy Management margin +430 basis points 2019 to 2024 against ABB +990 and Eaton +730; revenue more than quadrupled 2003 to 2025.
  • Tough environment: 2020 revenue −7.4% with FactSet adjusted EBIT margin 14.6% against 14.8% in 2019.
C-62

To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

C-5817th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Schneider holds outlier standards in growth and speed of capacity scaling, where it is currently out-executing most European peers, but not in margins, returns or capital discipline, where ABB and Eaton set the standard. It is a high-standard operator that is catching up, not yet an outlier.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Out-executing on growth: Q3 2026 organic growth of 16.9% against ABB's 14%, Legrand's about 10.7% and Siemens Smart Infrastructure's 13%; organic growth at the 80th percentile of the sector (Jefferies); prefabricated capacity up about 270% in two years and a first UL 2140 certification.
  • Out-executing on pricing in 2026: product pricing at 5.1% against ABB's just above 2%.
  • Not outlier on margin: Energy Management at 22.3% to 22.4% against Eaton's 26.3% and ABB Electrification's 25.6%; Industrial Automation 14% against an 18% target.
  • Not outlier on returns: ROCE about 12% adjusted against ABB's 29.9%; capital intensity of acquisitions high.
  • Speed over perfection: the chief executive's philosophy raises velocity, and the Leadership digest rates it "partial adherence to long-term quality metrics".
C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

D4226th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Governance scaffolding is prudent and above French norms, but capital-allocation behaviour puts management's strategic ambition ahead of per-share returns. The board is independent, pay is performance-linked and clawbacks exist; yet the incentive design rewards EPS growth (which debt-funded acquisitions can deliver) without any return-on-capital test, the company's ROCE definition excludes the expensive buyouts, and management chose to pause buybacks and issue equity when its shares trade at the low end of their forward valuation range. The evidence shows a well-governed company whose shareholders are not first in capital allocation.

Evidence for shareholder alignment and prudence:

  • 92% board independence (French average about 60%); chairman and chief executive roles split in 2023; clawback and malus since 2023; 100% independent remuneration committee; an independent audit chair; clean joint audits (PwC, Forvis Mazars).
  • About 78% of chief-executive target pay is performance-linked; the chief executive must hold five years of salary in shares (Blum holds 65,443 shares); chairman Tricoire holds 823,872 shares; 61% employee shareholding participation; over 90% approval of Blum's pay policy; roadshows covering 44.6% of capital.
  • Voting caps of 10% (15% with double votes) protect minorities from a controlling holder.

Evidence against:

  • No return-on-capital metric in either incentive plan (ABB and Siemens use ROCE); 25% of the long-term plan on carbon targets; minority buyouts carry no economic penalty for executives (investor feedback).
  • ROCE stated as 15.1% excludes EUR 10.2bn of buyouts.
  • PTC: equity issuance of EUR 5bn to 6bn and a buyback pause while the 24-month-forward P/E sits at the 11th percentile of five years.
  • Herweck's departure indemnity drew only 68% support; no open-market purchases by executives in H1 2026; the Indian listed entity's cost-plus related-party transfers without public bidding.
C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

F2534th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement. It is not. Schneider allocates organic capital at very high returns, but its discretionary capital goes overwhelmingly to high-priced acquisitions with low early returns, and it is a net issuer, not a repurchaser, of shares: the share count rose from 551m (2019) to 562m (2025), the buyback is small and now paused, and new equity is about to be issued near a multi-year valuation low. This is the weakest element of the case and the principal reason the market applies a discount despite rising earnings.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Share count: 551.0m (2019) to 562.2m (2025); buybacks of EUR 419m (2023), EUR 136m (2024) and net issuance of EUR 39m (2025); EUR 600m in 2026 (about 0.4% of market value); none in 2027 and 2028; EUR 5bn to 6bn of new shares for PTC (about 4%).
  • Acquired returns: India year-one ROIC about 2.5%; PTC ROCE about 4% in year one, above cost of capital only by year five; Cognite 18 times sales; AVEVA minority at 40 times EV/EBIT; Redburn: buying minorities at 44 times EBITA "destroys long-term capital efficiency".
  • Defences on record: Motivair is ahead of its business case; Deutsche Bank argues India will exceed its cost of capital quickly if local double-digit growth continues; Redburn finds PTC's DCF value almost unchanged without synergies; software was bought when "SaaS-pocalypse" valuations were depressed.
  • Contrast: ABB recycled Robotics proceeds into Rotork while buying back USD 2bn; Siemens Energy returns up to EUR 10bn by FY2028; Legrand buys at 10 to 12 times EBITDA and earns post-tax ROCE of about 16% on data-centre acquisitions.

Financial profile

B+626th of 388 answers

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

B+626th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Economically, Schneider is a strong, asset-light compounder whose earnings are inflecting: EPS growth is moving from about 7% a year (2019 to 2025) to about 20% in 2026 and 2027 on low-teens revenue growth, with margins rising (adjusted EBITA 18.7% to about 19.8%) and cash conversion around 100% of adjusted net income. The organic reinvestment runway is excellent, the reducible cost base is large and partly already being harvested, and the mid-term margin plan comes from many medium-sized, mostly mechanical levers that peers have shown are achievable. Operating leverage is moderate, so the cone of outcomes for operating profit is relatively narrow; currency and price-cost matter more than volume. The weaknesses are below the operating line and in capital: true returns on capital are about 12% (not the stated 15.1%) because of expensive buyouts, the earnings base excludes recurring restructuring and acquisition amortisation, and leverage rises to about 2.3 times in 2027 without any buyback to amplify EPS. The model should therefore show strong operating profit growth, then a visible financing drag (interest, about 4% more shares, no buybacks) in 2027 and 2028; on that basis consensus FY27 EPS of EUR 12.61 looks full rather than conservative.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

A723rd of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Schneider is moving from high-single-digit EPS growth (2019 to 2025) to about 20% EPS growth in 2026 and 2027, on low-teens revenue growth, because margin expansion now adds to organic growth instead of being consumed by currency and cost inflation. The 2026 figure is near-certain; 2027 is well supported operationally but consensus (EUR 12.61) sits at the upper end of what the standalone business plus a modestly accretive PTC can deliver; 2028 and beyond are less durable because the capacity wave and data-centre normalisation arrive together.

History (FactSet, EUR):

  • Revenue: EUR 27,158m (2019) to EUR 40,152m (2025), a 6.7% reported CAGR (organic higher: 8.9% in 2025), with a 7.4% fall in 2020.
  • FactSet adjusted diluted EPS: 5.12 (2019) to 7.78 (2025), a 7.2% CAGR; on the brokers' basis 7.26 (2023), 8.32 (2024, +14.6%), 8.59 (2025, +3.2%). The 2025 stall came from currency (−EUR 482m of adjusted EBITA) and higher interest (net interest −EUR 553m against −EUR 246m in 2019), while organic adjusted EBITA grew 12.3%.

Consensus (FactSet median, brokers' basis): revenue +12.8% (2026), +12.0% (2027), +10.1% (2028), +7.4% (2029); EPS 10.30 (+19.9%), 12.61 (+22.4%), 14.49 (+14.9%), 16.11 (+11.2%). FY27 broker EPS range 10.65 (Santander) to 13.67 (Berenberg, UBS), a 28% spread that largely reflects different treatment of PTC.

Durability test (my arithmetic, for the Consumer to check):

  • 2026: guidance of 12% to 14% organic growth and a 19.7% to 19.9% margin, with nine months reported, supports EPS of about EUR 10.2 to 10.5. Note that the margin includes a EUR 100m tariff refund (about 1.5% of EPS) that will not recur.
  • 2027 standalone: organic growth of 9% to 11%, about 1% currency drag and margin +50 to 80 basis points (from an underlying base of about 19.6% excluding the refund) gives adjusted EBITA growth of about 12% to 16% and EPS of roughly EUR 11.5 to 12.0, with no buyback support.
  • 2027 PTC effect: PTC's roughly 40% EBITA margin on about USD 2.9bn of revenue, less interest on EUR 16bn to 17bn of debt and about 4% more shares, gives low-single-digit accretion at best in a first full year (Redburn models 1.9% in 2028).
  • Conclusion: a defensible FY27 range is about EUR 11.6 to 12.4; consensus EUR 12.61 requires about 12% organic growth and more than 100 basis points of margin again. The drivers of upside are real (price carry-over, productivity running ahead of plan), so this is a "full" rather than "wrong" consensus.

Quality: growth comes from organic operating profit, not from share count reduction (there are no buybacks in 2027 and 2028), which makes it higher quality than peers' buyback-assisted growth.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

A703rd of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. The organic reinvestment runway is excellent: Schneider can deploy capex, working capital and R&D into capacity, prefabrication and new products at returns well above its cost of capital for at least four to five years, because demand exceeds capacity and the business is asset-light (capex about 3% of sales). The problem is that management is also deploying very large sums in acquisitions at returns far below that. The fair multiple therefore depends on the mix: on organic deployment alone the business deserves a premium quality-compounder multiple; with continuing high-priced acquisitions it deserves a discount to that.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Organic opportunity: demand is ahead of capacity (backlogs at records; "structural growth is exceeding current revenue-conversion capability"); capex of about 2.5% to 3.5% of sales (FY2025 net capex EUR 1,496m; consensus EUR 1.6bn to 1.9bn a year); asset-light capacity through Foxconn and contract manufacturers; management's target post-tax return on new organic capital of 25% to 30%.
  • Incremental economics: organic adjusted EBITA growth of 18% to 21% on 12% to 14% organic revenue growth in 2026 implies incremental margins of about 27% to 30%; net working capital runs at 15% to 17% of revenue, so each EUR 1 of extra revenue needs roughly EUR 0.2 to 0.25 of working capital and fixed assets and earns EUR 0.20 to 0.30 of adjusted EBITA (between the average and the incremental margin), a pre-tax return on the incremental capital of roughly 80% to 120% (my arithmetic).
  • Operating spend: R&D stepping up to 7% of sales (from 5.8%) is spending into high-return product cycles (solid-state transformers, software-defined switchgear, liquid cooling), though it will hold back reported margin.
  • Inorganic deployment: PTC's year-one ROCE about 4%, India's about 2.5%; Cognite at 18 times sales; these lower group returns for years.
  • Fair multiple implication: high organic RONIC plus 7% to 10% growth for four to five years supports a premium multiple; the 24-month-forward P/E of 16.33x (11th percentile of five years) prices the acquisition dilution and macro, not the organic runway.
C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

C6013th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Returns are good on the organic business, mediocre on the group as reported once acquisitions are counted properly, and will fall further after PTC. Stated ROCE of 15.1% (2025) flatters the true figure by about 3 points because EUR 10.2bn of minority buyouts are excluded; on a like-for-like basis Schneider earns about 12%, below ABB (22.9% in 2024, 29.9% in Q3 2026) and Siemens (19.1%), level with Legrand (12.3%). Returns on tangible capital and on new organic capital are high; the gap between the two is the measure of what acquisitions have cost.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Stated: ROCE 15.1% in 2025, crossing the 15% ambition; target 15% to 20%; post-tax return on new organic capital 25% to 30% targeted.
  • Adjusted: Redburn says excluding EUR 10.2bn of AVEVA and India minority buyouts inflates ROCE by about 3 points; the company described those deals as "ROCE neutral".
  • Peers: ABB ROCE 29.9% (Q3 2026); Vertiv ROIC 32.2%; Siemens Energy Grid ROIC 52%; Legrand 12.3% post-tax.
  • New investment: PTC about 4% ROCE in year one, above cost of capital only by year five (Deutsche Bank); India about 2.5% in year one (Redburn).
  • Tangible capital: not stated in the files; the balance sheet is goodwill-heavy after Invensys, AVEVA, OSIsoft, India and the pending deals, so return on tangible capital is high (asset-light model, net working capital about 15% of sales, capex about 3%). The organic business clearly earns well above 25% on tangible capital.
  • Incentives: the long-term incentive plan has no return-on-capital metric, unlike ABB and Siemens.
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

B+745th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Conversion is high and dependable at about 100% of adjusted net income through the cycle (106% in 2025), with a seasonal first-half dip and a structural working-capital drag from fast growth in systems. The FactSet history understates it because its free-cash-flow line is after dividends (see the preliminary notes). The risks to conversion in 2026 to 2027 are identifiable: loss of factoring, rising contract assets on long projects, EUR 450m of restructuring cash, and the working-capital needs of 13% growth.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Company definition: FY2025 free cash flow EUR 4,635m (record, +10%), 106% conversion; H1 2026 EUR 1,631m (+244%, 17% above consensus), 66% of H1 net income (seasonal); target about 100% on average to 2030.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

C4513th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. The earnings base is moderately clean. The tax rate is stable and there is no hidden leakage to minorities, but adjusted earnings exclude costs that recur every year (restructuring and the amortisation of acquisitions that are central to the strategy), capitalised development flatters margins against US peers, and the 2026 margin includes a one-off tariff refund. The adjusted-to-reported gap (about 14% to 16%) will widen after PTC, so the Consumer should model both bases and value on a basis that charges restructuring.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Gap: FY2025 adjusted net income EUR 4,829m (+4%) against reported EUR 4,163m (−2%); brokers'-basis EPS 8.59 against reported basic 7.41 (a 16% gap). The FY2025 gap includes a non-cash EUR 388m impairment of an associate (genuinely one-off).
  • Recurring exclusions: restructuring guided at about EUR 450m in 2026 ("elevated", for the Next programme), plus EUR 147m of restructuring and M&A charges in H1 2026 other operating expenses; amortisation of purchased intangibles (EUR 206m in H1 2026, falling as old deals amortise, but set to rise sharply with PTC, Cognite and Shelly purchase-price allocation).
  • One-offs inside adjusted EBITA: a EUR 100m tariff refund in Q2 2026; Deutsche Bank and the H1 review argue the H1 margin beat leaned on the refund and on "aggressive, potentially unsustainable" administrative cost cuts.
  • Capitalised costs: EUR 367m of development costs capitalised in FY2025, 15% of EUR 2,380m innovation spend; Eaton expenses all R&D a EUR 128m impairment of capitalised development hit H1 2026, which shows the capitalised balance carries write-down risk.
  • Tax: 23.1% (2024), 23.5% (2025), 24% (H1 2026); stable; FactSet reported tax rate 22.9% (2025). No sign of below-the-line leakage beyond rising interest.
  • Minorities and associates: the India buyout removed most minorities (EUR 273m on the balance sheet); associates EUR 762m.
  • Other: percentage-of-completion accounting on long systems contracts (estimate risk); the French fine of EUR 207m ran through non-trade working capital; the company's ROCE definition excludes EUR 10.2bn of buyouts.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

D4224th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Financial leverage is moving from comfortable (1.6 times EBITDA) to stretched-but-manageable (about 2.3 times in 2027) because of PTC. It does not amplify EPS much, because it is used to buy low-return assets rather than to buy back shares; its main effect is to add roughly EUR 0.5bn to 0.6bn a year of interest and to remove the buyback for two years. The risk it creates is timing: peak leverage in 2027 coincides with the most likely window for data-centre normalisation and the capacity wave.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Path (my reconstruction, for the Consumer to check): net financial debt EUR 13,721m at end-2025 and EUR 15,360m at June 2026 (1.64 times EBITDA, after EUR 2.4bn of dividends and EUR 0.6bn of buybacks); add Cognite (USD 3.1bn), Shelly (EUR 1.2bn) and AiDASH (USD 0.35bn) on closing, and second-half free cash flow; then PTC's roughly EUR 21bn enterprise value less EUR 5bn to 6bn of new equity. That gives net debt near EUR 30bn at the PTC peak and about 2.3 times EBITDA in 2027 (Deutsche Bank), deleveraging below 1.5 times within about two years of consolidation. Consensus total debt (EUR 25.6bn in 2027) does not yet fully reflect this.
  • Interest: FactSet net interest expense EUR 553m in 2025 (from EUR 246m in 2019); EUR 16bn to 17bn of new debt at an A-category cost adds about EUR 0.5bn to 0.6bn a year; interest cover remains high (EBIT about ten times interest).
  • Commitments: an A-category credit rating; a progressive dividend; buyback paused 2027 and 2028, then accelerated to complete EUR 2.5bn to 3.5bn by 2030.
  • Peer context (FactSet latest): ABB 0.3 times (0.18 times at Q3), Legrand 2.2 times, Siemens 2.9 times, Siemens Energy net cash; Eaton about 2.2 times after its acquisitions. Redburn says post-PTC leverage "places the group at higher financial risk than its peers".
  • Sensitivities: a downturn at peak leverage would slow deleveraging and could delay the buyback restart; equity investors would see the combination of leverage and software integration as a fragility (the Fitness for Fighting Fragility row is at Double Underweight).

Economic leverage

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

Memo topic 5, : the Rater's score

5a

Pricing power

3 answers
C-1762C-4564P-0460
C-17To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?B+625th of 38full answer under 2b Internal corporate change forces
C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?B+646th of 38full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

A602nd of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Price will contribute about 2.5% to 3% of revenue growth in 2026, about 2% to 2.5% in 2027 (mostly carry-over), about 1.3% in 2028 and about 1% a year in 2029 and 2030. In the margin, price-cost was roughly neutral for 2026 (H1 −EUR 154m, H2 at least +EUR 150m). 2027 is the one year of clearly positive net price, at about +EUR 350m to 450m, or +0.7 to +0.9 points of gross margin before wage inflation and mix. The contribution then fades to roughly neutral from 2028 as competitor capacity arrives and lead times normalise. Two structural features protect the downside. List prices on products are sticky: neither Schneider nor its peers gave back list prices in 2009, 2016, 2020 or the 2023 to 2025 destocking. And product pricing sits on specification and distributor switching costs, not on scarcity. The skew is therefore favourable on metals: if copper and silver fall and list prices hold, margin rises (about +45bp per 10% fall). It is unfavourable on data-centre systems, where scarcity pricing, not brand, is doing part of the work and will be renegotiated after 2028.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • The 2026 acceleration was mostly cost recovery, so the "real" gain is 2027's. Product price realisation was 1.7% in Q1, 3.7% in Q2 and 5.1% in Q3 2026 (Deutsche Bank, 19 October). The H1 bridge was gross pricing +EUR 280m against raw materials −EUR 330m and tariffs −EUR 104m. Pricing covered about 65% of input inflation. The full year is guided net price positive "in value", so FY2026 is roughly neutral. In 2027, the Q2 to Q4 2026 increases annualise, giving H1 2027 product price of about 4% to 5% year on year. Input costs annualise more slowly: hedging covers about 20% of the top three commodities with a 12-month taper, and pass-through lags 3 to 6 months. That gap is the 2027 margin tailwind, and it is front-loaded into H1 2027.
  • Mechanism and coverage. Direct list pricing touches less than half of sales (Bernstein): products sold through distributors (about 45% of revenue goes through partners). Systems and data-centre contracts reprice through backlog indexation (CFO, April 2025: contracts "increasingly integrate the ability to reprice backlog") and through the run-off of pre-2022 contracts. That is the Sector Digest's "contracted margin" story, rising through 2028. Price on products is fast and sticky. Price on systems is slow but persists while backlog turns over. That explains why the systems margin should keep rising into 2028 even as list-price momentum fades.
  • Why the fade starts in late 2027.
  • Benchmark US switchgear lead times have fallen from 60 to 40 weeks (Bernstein, 14 September 2026), and medium-voltage transformer lead times to about 35 weeks. High-voltage transformers remain at about 138 weeks, but Schneider is not a high-voltage player.
  • Eaton is investing about USD 1.5bn across 24 North American sites (200 projects). Bernstein counts about USD 6bn of capacity ramping over 18 months. LS Electric and Shanghai Liangxin are entering North American data-centre switchgear with UL-certified products.
  • Morgan Stanley's distributor survey shows the share of distributors receiving supplier price increases fell from 68% in Q2 to 54% in Q3 2026. That is the first observable sign of cooling.
  • Former Schneider and Siemens Energy executives expect "increased pricing competition after 2028" and hyperscaler renegotiation of framework pricing once scarcity ends.
  • Why the fade should not become give-back in products. Legrand has never cut average prices in twenty years. In 2009 Schneider held a 12.9% margin through a 15.7% organic decline, and distributors (Rexel) held gross margin. Specification share is high (64% of data-centre procurement managers put more than 20% of power spend with Schneider), and certification gates (UL 2140, IEC, utility pre-qualification) block cheap entrants. The precedent for real give-back is narrow: China in 2025 (product pricing slightly negative) and standardised, uncertified products (Indian transformers fell 25% to 30% when capacity arrived). China pricing turned positive in Q1 2026, and Chinese automation suppliers are now raising prices (Inovance's fourth increase of 2026, 8% to 35% on HMIs; Xinje 8% to 20%). Chinese price discipline currently helps Industrial Automation rather than hurting it.
  • Large tenders are the weak point. A former Vertiv executive says hyperscalers "aggressively leverage competitive bids". A former ABB data-centre leader says large cloud operators rarely buy complete packages from one supplier. Deutsche Bank notes hyperscalers select from three or four qualified suppliers per category. Schneider "occasionally competes aggressively on price" to win the lifecycle tail. Line-jumping premiums (for example USD 250,000 to cut a USD 3m switchgear delivery from 45 to 25 weeks) and capacity-reservation agreements (Compass USD 3bn over five years; Switch USD 1.9bn) are scarcity rents. They will be competed away from 2028. Reservation agreements often carry no binding minimum volumes.
  • Peers corroborate a 2% to 3% world. ABB's group pricing was just above 2% in Q3 2026, weighted to Q4. Legrand targets about 3% a year to support a 21% to 22% margin over 2027 to 2030. Siemens Digital Industries raised list prices 5% to 20% from 1 November 2026, above its usual 2% to 15%. Schneider's 5.1% on products is at the top of the peer range, so it has the most to fade.
  • What I put in the model.
  • Price inside organic growth: 2027 10.5% = about 2.2 points of price plus 8.3 of volume and mix. 2028 8.0% = 1.3 + 6.7. 2029 and 2030 7.5% = 1.0 + 6.5.
  • Net price-cost in adjusted EBITA: 2026 about zero; 2027 about +EUR 400m (credited at about +50bp net of wage inflation and systems mix); 2028 about +EUR 100m; 2029 and 2030 about zero.
  • Sensitivity: copper and silver are about half the raw-material bill, about 4.5% of revenue. A 10% fall with list prices held adds about EUR 230m (+45bp). A 10% rise unrecovered for two halves costs about the same.

Cone and skew over 18 months. The base is as above. The upside is metals falling in 2027 while list prices hold (+30bp to +50bp in FY2027). The downside is a renewed copper and silver spike plus a wider US tariff (11% of employees are in Mexico), which in H1 2026 cost about EUR 430m gross in one half: −50bp to −100bp for one or two halves before recovery. Skew is mildly positive for FY2027 and turns mildly negative for 2028 to 2029, when the capacity wave lands on data-centre systems pricing.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7562P-0166
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

B628th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Operating leverage is moderate, not high. Incremental margins on growth run at about 25% to 30% against an average margin of about 20%, and historically Schneider has protected margins well in downturns (decremental margins close to the average margin). This narrows the cone of outcomes: a five-point swing in organic growth moves adjusted EBITA by roughly EUR 450m (about 5% of EPS), so margin forecasts are robust to volume error, and EPS is more sensitive to currency and price-cost than to volume.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Sensitivities stated by brokers: 1 point of organic sales growth changes adjusted EBITA by about EUR 90m, about 1% of EPS (Bernstein, via Deutsche Bank); 2 points of product volume change EBITA by about EUR 130m, about 1.5% of EPS (Deutsche Bank); 0.5 point of margin is about 2.6% of EPS (Redburn). On a EUR 45bn base, EUR 90m per point is a drop-through of about 20%, i.e. close to the average margin.
  • Observed on the way up: 2026 organic adjusted EBITA +18% to +21% on organic revenue +12% to +14% implies incremental margins of about 27% to 30%; H1 2026 organic EBITA +22.1% on +14% revenue.
  • Observed on the way down: in 2020 revenue fell 7.4% and FactSet's adjusted EBIT margin moved only from 14.8% to 14.6%; Legrand (similar model) held 20% in 2020.
  • Cost structure: purchases of about EUR 11bn of EUR 21bn to 23bn cost of goods sold are variable; support-function costs of about EUR 9.4bn (23% of sales) are semi-fixed and are where the leverage lives; a large distributor share (45%) keeps the sales cost base partly variable.
  • Currency is the bigger lever: a 10% US-dollar move changes adjusted EBITA by EUR 427m (28% drop-through on EUR 1,505m of revenue).
  • Range for the model: FY27 organic growth between 6% and 13% maps to adjusted EBITA margins of roughly 19.9% to 20.9% on the standalone business; the range widens mainly through price-cost (±EUR 150m to 300m) and currency.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

B+664th of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Schneider's operating leverage is moderate in size and asymmetric in shape. On the way up it works through a semi-fixed support-function base (EUR 9.4bn, 23% of sales) that grows at roughly half the rate of revenue. On the way down it is cushioned, because about 45% of revenue is purchased materials (variable) and support costs have historically been flexed quickly. Over the next four years it will keep adding margin, but at a decaying rate that follows organic growth. Standalone organic margin expansion (adjusted EBITA) should be about +75bp in 2027, +40bp in 2028, +35bp in 2029 and +30bp in 2030, after +100bp to +120bp in 2026. That compares with about +45bp a year from 2019 to 2025. The critical insight is that most of the 2026 support-function gain was leverage on a base that kept growing in absolute terms, not net cost removal. Margin progression is therefore more growth-dependent than the self-help narrative implies. If growth falls to mid-single digits, the margin story stalls before costs are actually cut.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • The cost structure (FY2025, company and AlphaSense T-P1). Cost of sales was EUR 23.3bn, of which purchases were EUR 18bn (78% of cost of sales, about 45% of revenue, variable) and factory labour and overhead EUR 5bn (about 12% of revenue, semi-variable). Support functions were EUR 9.4bn (23.3% of revenue, semi-fixed). R&D was EUR 2.4bn (5.9%, fixed and rising by plan). The semi-fixed and fixed base is about 35% to 40% of revenue. Peers carry leaner overheads: Eaton's SG&A is 15.7% of sales, Vertiv 15.8%, ABB 19.8%. The overhead gap to Eaton (about 7.5 points) is the reservoir that operating leverage drains.
  • What the leverage was in 2026, decomposed. Organic adjusted EBITA growth of +18% to +21% on +12% to +14% organic revenue implies an incremental margin of 27% to 30%. But the H1 2026 support-function bridge shows gross savings of EUR 182m (headcount and simplification) against EUR 160m of reinvestment (R&D and digital) and EUR 129m of inflation. Absolute support costs rose by about EUR 107m. The ratio fell 1.1 points only because revenue rose 9.8%. Morgan Stanley's "headcount-driven" reading (8 September 2026) is correct in substance: the gain came from a hiring freeze on a base that had grown 24% in three years, not from structural removal. The harvesting opportunity is real (Redburn: Schneider can grow without adding staff because it hired ahead), but it pays out only while revenue grows.
  • Broker sensitivities agree with this picture. Bernstein puts one point of organic growth at about EUR 90m of adjusted EBITA (about 20% drop-through, close to the average margin). Deutsche Bank puts two points of product volume at about EUR 130m. The high incremental margins of 2026 came from price, productivity and a frozen overhead together, not from volume alone.
  • The down-cycle record limits the cone. In 2009 organic sales fell 15.7% and the margin fell only 310bp to 12.9%, with support costs cut by EUR 541m. In 2016 organic sales fell 0.9% and the organic margin rose 70bp, with EUR 377m of supply-chain savings. In 2020 organic sales fell 4.7% and the organic margin rose 20bp. Legrand held a 17.6% margin through a 14.9% sales fall in 2009. Decremental margins have historically been at or below the average margin, so the downside cone on margin is narrower than on revenue. The exception to watch is capacity under-absorption. Eaton's ramp of 2 million square feet cost about 100bp in Electrical Americas. Schneider, with prefabricated capacity up fourfold to 1.35 million square feet on eight sites, carries the same risk if data-centre orders pause.
  • How I translate it into the line items.
  • Support-function ratio: 23.3% in FY2025 (H1 2026 was a seasonally high 23.2%), to about 22.3% (FY2026), 21.6% (2027), 21.1% (2028) and about 20.3% (2030). That is −2.0 points from 2026 and −3.0 points from 2025, against the target of −1.5 to −2 points by 2030. It requires support costs to grow 5.5% to 7% a year against organic revenue growth of 7.5% to 10.5%.
  • R&D: from 5.9% towards 6.6% of sales by 2030, giving back about 0.7 points.
  • Gross margin: broadly flat from 2028 as price fades and systems and prefabricated mix grows.
  • Net effect: adjusted EBITA margin for the group excluding PTC rises from 19.9% (2026, including a EUR 100m tariff refund worth about 20bp) to 20.3% (2027), 20.7% (2028), 21.0% (2029) and 21.3% (2030). The core business, excluding the loss-making Cognite and AiDASH, is about 15bp higher: about 20.4% in 2027, which is +75bp on an underlying 2026 base of 19.7%. PTC lifts the group to 20.5% (2027), 21.7% (2028), 22.3% (2029) and 22.7% (2030).
  • Against history. FactSet's adjusted EBIT margin rose from 14.8% (2019) to 17.6% (2025), and the company's adjusted EBITA from about 15.3% to 18.7%: roughly +45bp to +55bp a year on about 8% average organic growth. Energy Management gained only +430bp from 2019 to 2024, against ABB's +990bp and Eaton's +730bp. The next four years are a reversion to that historical pace after an exceptional 2026, not a new regime. The first set's "half the five-year plan in year one" reading is correct for 2026. It should not be extrapolated, because the volume that delivered it decelerates.
  • The cone over 18 months. Each five-point swing in FY2027 organic growth moves adjusted EBITA by about EUR 450m to EUR 650m, or 5% to 7% of EPS. The upper figure applies on the downside if prefabricated capacity is under-absorbed. My FY2027 standalone margin range is 19.9% to 21.0%, base 20.4%. Price-cost (±EUR 150m to 300m) and currency (EUR 427m per 10% US-dollar move) widen it more than volume does.

Peer read-across. ABB's decentralised margin model (Electrification from 13.4% in 2019 to 25.6% in Q3 2026) shows what accountable overhead can achieve. Eaton shows that leverage is lost temporarily during capacity ramps. Legrand shows the margin-first ceiling (about +70bp since 2019). Schneider is between ABB and Legrand: real catch-up potential, but delivered through growth rather than through ABB-style restructuring of accountability.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7642P-0238
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?D4224th of 38full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

F3834th of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Financial leverage is a drag on EPS throughout the forecast period, not an amplifier. The balance sheet goes from about 0.9x net debt to EBITDA at end-2026 (temporarily flattered by the EUR 5.5bn equity raise held as cash) to a peak of about 2.3x at end-2027, when PTC closes. It then falls to about 1.8x (2028), 1.4x (2029) and 1.1x (2030). The debt buys a low-return asset rather than shares, so it adds cost without leverage benefit: net financial items roughly double from −EUR 715m (2025) to −EUR 1,360m (2028). Combined with 4% more shares, this makes 2027 the trough of PTC's EPS effect, at roughly −2.5% to −3% against a no-deal path. Accretion arrives only from 2028, at about +1.5% to +2%. The balance-sheet risk is timing, not solvency. Peak leverage in 2027 to 2028 coincides with the window in which data-centre growth normalises and competitor capacity arrives. A downturn then would push the buyback restart beyond 2029 rather than threaten the A-category rating.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Net financial debt path (company definition, excluding leases; my build).
  • End-2025: EUR 13.7bn.
  • June 2026: EUR 15.4bn (1.64x).
  • End-2026: about EUR 9.7bn, after H2 free cash flow of about EUR 3.1bn, Cognite (EUR 2.65bn) and AiDASH (EUR 0.27bn) closing in Q4, and +EUR 5.44bn of net equity proceeds.
  • End-2027: about EUR 28.8bn, adding Shelly (EUR 1.2bn, Q1 2027), PTC (about EUR 21.0bn including fees and refinanced PTC debt) and the dividend, less EUR 5.5bn of free cash flow. That is 2.27x pro forma EBITDA (Deutsche Bank 2.3x; Redburn's 1.8x uses a cash-only definition and a higher EBITDA).
  • End-2028: about EUR 24.9bn (1.8x). End-2029: about EUR 21.7bn (1.4x), after a EUR 1.1bn buyback. End-2030: about EUR 18.2bn (1.1x).
  • The stated target of below 1.5x within two years of consolidation is met in my base case at end-2029, but only just. It does not survive a downside year.
  • Interest.
  • New debt of EUR 16bn to 17bn. Deutsche Bank prices it at about 3.5% (+EUR 578m a year). With the US 10-year near 5.10% and a December Fed hike priced above 90%, a mixed euro and dollar issue is more likely to cost 3.75% to 4%. The July 2026 EMTN priced a 7-year fixed tranche at 3.375% before the latest rise in rates. I use 3.75% (EUR 620m a year).
  • Net financial items: −EUR 715m (2025), −EUR 820m (2026, higher average debt after the India buyout), −EUR 1,020m (2027, a cheap first three quarters plus one quarter of PTC debt and pre-funding carry), −EUR 1,360m (2028), −EUR 1,240m (2029), −EUR 1,130m (2030).
  • Each 100bp on the new debt is about EUR 165m before tax, or about 1.5% of EPS. This is the most under-discussed sensitivity in the case, because consensus anchors on Deutsche Bank's 3.5%.
  • The PTC EPS arithmetic, year by year (my estimates).
  • 2027: shares +4.1% for the whole year (about −EUR 0.47 of EPS). PTC's Q4 adjusted EBITA of EUR 260m, less Q4 interest of EUR 155m and pre-funding costs of about EUR 80m, gives almost nothing after tax. Interest earned on the raised cash before closing offsets a little. Net effect about −2.5% to −3%.
  • 2028: PTC adjusted EBITA of about EUR 1.19bn (40% margin on about EUR 2.8bn, plus EUR 70m of synergies), less about EUR 620m of interest, less 25% tax, gives about +EUR 430m of net income (+5.5%) against +4% shares. Net accretion about +1.5%. Redburn's +1.9% agrees.
  • 2030: with EUR 250m of cost synergies, accretion of about +3% to +4%.
  • The deal is roughly EPS-neutral over three years. Its economic case rests entirely on PTC's growth and synergies after 2029.
  • Return on capital. PTC's ROCE is about 4% in year one (Deutsche Bank) and 3.6% before synergies, rising to 8.3% by 2032 with all synergies (Redburn, against an 8% WACC). Group ROCE on the company's definition (15.1% in 2025) falls to roughly 11% to 12% in 2028 once PTC's EUR 21bn of capital is included. On a like-for-like basis that includes the EUR 10.2bn of excluded minority buyouts, it falls towards 9% to 10% (my arithmetic). The balance sheet becomes goodwill-dominated. Purchase-price amortisation rises from about EUR 420m (2026) to about EUR 1.25bn (2028), on my estimate of about EUR 8.5bn of identifiable PTC intangibles amortised over 12 years. That widens the gap between reported and adjusted EPS from about 14% to about 18%.
  • Rating and flexibility. S&P (A/A-1 stable) and Moody's (A2 stable) reaffirmed on 6 October 2026. The thresholds are not disclosed. In my base case the A rating is safe. In a case where data-centre orders pause in 2027, 2028 EBITDA is about 10% lower, leverage stays above 2x at end-2028, and the 2029 buyback restart is the first casualty.
  • Peer contrast. ABB has net cash (−0.5x in 2026) and a recurring USD 1.5bn to 2bn buyback. Eaton is at about 2.2x after Boyd and Fibrebond. Schneider moves from the middle of the peer group to its most leveraged quartile, at the moment when the sector's multiple is compressing on financing fears.
5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7770P-0364
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

A702nd of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. There is substantial reducible cost, concentrated in support functions and in product cost through design and procurement. Support-function costs (about EUR 9.4bn, 23% of sales) can fall by 1.5 to 2 points of sales by 2030 and are already down 1.1 points in six months; industrial productivity of EUR 2bn to 2.5bn over 2026 to 2030 is running at roughly twice the planned pace. Raw materials are not cuttable but are passable; R&D is deliberately rising; regional duplication is a strategic cost the group has chosen to keep. Peer and own-history precedents say the plan is achievable, and probably conservative.

By cost block (FY2025, EUR):

  • Cost of sales, about 23.3bn: purchases of about 11bn (raw materials about 9% of revenue, half copper and silver) are pass-through, not cuttable; conversion and logistics costs are reducible through "cost by design" with engineering in India and China, centralised procurement, and productivity of EUR 2bn to 2.5bn over five years (EUR 535m in H1 2026 alone, against EUR 596m for all of 2025). Reducible: about 2% of cost of sales a year.
  • Support functions, about 9.4bn: the clearest cuttable block. Energy Management headcount grew 24% in three years; Multi-Hub 2.0 removes the triple country-region-global layer; central functions fell to 1.7% of revenue from 1.9%; AI@scale targets overhead scalability. A 1.5 to 2 point reduction on 2030 revenue is worth roughly EUR 0.8bn to 1.2bn a year (my arithmetic on consensus revenue).
  • R&D, about 2.4bn (5.8% of revenue): not to be cut; stepping up to 7% of sales, a headwind that partly offsets support-function savings.
  • Restructuring: about EUR 450m in 2026 is the cost of the savings.
  • Not reducible by choice: duplicated R&D, factories and suppliers across four hubs (insurance against tariffs and decoupling); Redburn warns this duplication can be a long-term profitability drag.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

A642nd of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. The gross scope is large, but most of it does not reach margin, because it funds inflation and reinvestment. Of about EUR 2bn to 2.5bn of planned industrial productivity (2026 to 2030) and a 1.5- to 2-point cut in the support-function ratio, I expect a net improvement in standalone adjusted EBITA margin of about +1.8 points from 2027 to 2030, on top of 2026's +100bp to +120bp. It is front-loaded: about +75bp in 2027 (helped by a price-cost swing), then +30bp to +40bp a year. PTC's EUR 250m of cost synergies reach adjusted EBITA at about EUR 70m (2028), EUR 170m (2029) and EUR 250m (2030), worth about 40bp of group margin by 2030. The productivity target will very likely be beaten in gross terms. What matters for the model is how much survives inflation, the R&D step-up and price fade.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Industrial productivity reaches gross margin only net of input inflation. H1 2026 productivity was EUR 535m (against EUR 596m for all of 2025), yet the organic gross margin rose only 10bp, to 42.5%. Raw materials (−EUR 330m) and tariffs (−EUR 104m) net of pricing (+EUR 280m) consumed most of it. Productivity was strong partly because the base was low (management's own comment). Rule for the model: industrial productivity of EUR 400m to 500m a year from 2027 is spent on wage and material inflation. It adds margin only in years when net price-cost is non-negative. 2027 is such a year (carry-over pricing against plateauing metals). 2028 may not be.
  • Support-function savings reach margin through leverage. H1 2026 gross savings were EUR 182m, against EUR 160m of reinvestment and EUR 129m of inflation (see P-01). The ratio fell from 24.3% to 23.2%. Multi-Hub 2.0 removes the triple country-region-global matrix, central and digital costs fell to 1.7% of revenue from 1.9%, and AI@scale targets overhead. I expect absolute support costs to grow about 5.5% to 7% a year against organic revenue growth of 7.5% to 10.5%. That lowers the ratio by about 0.7 points in 2027, 0.5 in 2028 and 0.4 in each of 2029 and 2030: about 2 points from FY2026's roughly 22.3%, and 3 points from FY2025's 23.3%, against the target of 1.5 to 2 points. Against it:
  • R&D rises from 5.9% to about 6.6% of sales (−0.7 points).
  • Wage inflation in factories and price fade on systems from 2028 (−0.3 to −0.5 points a year in 2028 and 2029) offset part of the gain.
  • Net to adjusted EBITA: about +0.4 points a year on average from 2027 to 2030.
  • Segment levers, with a haircut.
  • Industrial Automation: from 14.0% (H1 2026) to the 18% target by 2028. I model 15.3% (2027), 16.3% (2028) and 17.5% (2030) excluding PTC. Discrete recovery is real, but the H1 miss of 60bp to 70bp, the EUR 128m impairment on range simplification and Chinese competition argue against full delivery.
  • AVEVA: from 17% (2024) to the 30% target by 2028, worth about 40bp to 50bp of group margin. I credit about two-thirds by 2028, because the subscription transition ends in 2026 and ARR is growing about 10%.
  • The cost of the savings. Restructuring of EUR 450m (2026, up from EUR 287m) then normalises to EUR 250m, EUR 200m and EUR 175m in my base. It is deducted in brokers' adjusted EPS. The fall from EUR 450m to EUR 250m adds about EUR 0.26 of EPS in 2027 (about 2.5 points of growth), a tailwind the narrative rarely mentions. PTC's EUR 250m of implementation costs (Redburn: EUR 125m in each of 2028 and 2029) are excluded from adjusted EPS but are real cash.
  • Large cuts are not on the agenda, and should not be. Regional duplication of R&D, factories and suppliers across four hubs (90% local-for-local) is a deliberate cost. It insured the group against tariffs (net tariff impact of −EUR 104m in H1 2026, less severe than Legrand's 45% import dependence in the US implies). I do not model its removal. This is the right choice strategically, but it caps the margin gap that can be closed to Eaton's 26% to 27% in North American electrical.
  • PTC cost synergies. EUR 250m by year three is about 10% of PTC's sales (Redburn: "demanding"). Precedents: Siemens and Mentor Graphics about 5% of target sales; Siemens and Altair 23% targeted; AVEVA and OSIsoft GBP 20m (small, with heavy management and sales turnover). Delisting and procurement make about half of the target near-certain. I phase it at 30%, 70% and 100% over 2028 to 2030.
  • When it reaches the lines. Most of the 2027 benefit falls in H1. Price carry-over and a frozen overhead against H1 2026's negative price-cost (−EUR 154m) and the tariff refund (+EUR 100m) give an H1 2027 adjusted EBITA margin of about 20.1% (+80bp year on year). H2 2027 is about 20.9% (+45bp, including about 30bp of PTC mix). 2028 to 2030 add +30bp to +40bp a year before PTC mix.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set treated the support-function improvement as cost discipline that is "partly the harvesting of fat". I agree on the fat. But the mechanism is a freeze, not a cut: the fat is not being removed, it is being outgrown. That is fine while growth is 10%. It means a growth shock exposes the 24% headcount increase again, and real cuts (with restructuring costs) would then be needed.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4770P-0560
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?A704th of 38full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

B+603rd of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Reinforcing loops dominate through 2027 and are visible in orders and backlog already booked. Balancing loops are building and should dominate the rate of change from H2 2027 to 2029, though not the direction. Net, the loops produce a growth and margin path that decelerates but does not reverse: organic growth of about 14% (2026), 10.5% (2027), 8% (2028) and 7.5% (2029 and 2030), and margin expansion decaying from about 100bp to about 30bp a year. The one balancing loop that could convert deceleration into decline is financing: debt-funded hyperscaler and neocloud capital spending meeting wider credit spreads. It acts on orders first and on revenue 12 to 24 months later. It is the loop the model cannot see in reported numbers until it is too late, so it must be watched in leading indicators.

The reinforcing loops, ranked by financial weight.

  1. Prefabrication, single-sourcing, share and capacity (strong, running). Factory-built modules cut on-site installation by about 80% (Compass and Schneider white paper, 21 October 2026) and compress deployment 30% to 60%. That raises customers' willingness to single-source (Bernstein survey, 14 September 2026), which lifts Schneider's share of wallet. Prefabricated electrical rooms lift content from about USD 1.8m to 2.8m per megawatt. Schneider then adds capacity (fourfold since 2025, 1.35 million square feet on eight sites plus 12 partner sites), which shortens lead times and wins more single-source awards. Modular builds rise from about 20% to 60% of builds by 2030. Financial effect: data-centre revenue growing mid-to-high teens in 2027 and low teens in 2028, with share gain on top of market growth. It stalls if site readiness (grid, permits, labour) lags factory output. An expert notes 30% to 40% of backlogs can be held up by site readiness. It reverses only if hyperscalers standardise designs enough to commoditise the modules.
  2. Volume leverage on a frozen overhead (strong while growth lasts). Growth lowers the support-function ratio, which funds prefabrication and R&D, which supports growth. This loop is mechanical, but it runs only as fast as revenue grows (see P-01). It is the loop that turns a growth shock into a margin shock.
  3. Contracted-margin run-off (moderate, dated to 2028). Backlog priced before 2022, and before the 2026 price increases, ships out and is replaced by orders at today's prices. Systems margins rise through 2028 even as list-price momentum fades.
  4. Reference-design gatekeeping (moderate, early). NVIDIA GB300 and Vera Rubin RapidScale blueprints, AMD Helios and NVIDIA DSX POD put Schneider in specifications before bids open. That raises specification share and content per megawatt, and reinforces loop 1.
  5. Installed base, services and software (slow, durable). Digital services are worth up to about 10% of data-centre electrical capital spending (about USD 0.5bn per gigawatt). AVEVA's subscription transition ends in 2026 (ARR +10%; margin from 17% to a 30% target), and PTC adds EUR 2.4bn of ARR at 96% recurring. This adds about 40bp to 50bp of group margin over the period, but it is too slow to offset loops 1 and 2 if they turn.

The balancing loops, ranked by financial weight.

  1. Scarcity pricing invites capacity, which normalises lead times and fades price (building; bites H2 2027 to 2029). The evidence is in P-04. Financial effect: price contribution from about 2.5% to 1%, and net price-cost from +EUR 400m (2027) to about zero. Under-absorption is a risk if Schneider's own fourfold prefabricated capacity outruns orders.
  2. Debt-financed demand invites wider credit spreads, which slow orders (latent; the swing factor). Neocloud and hyperscaler spreads have widened (Fitness for Fighting). Deutsche Bank (21 October 2026) warns of an "abrupt slowdown in data-centre deployment by 2028". Only 13% of the US data-centre pipeline to 2032 is under construction (Allianz). An expert reports no developer financing crunch yet, because tier-1 players are well capitalised. Financial effect if triggered in mid-2027: data-centre orders −30% to −50%, 2028 organic growth cut by 5 to 8 points to about 1% to 3%, and margin flat to −50bp because loop 2 runs in reverse. Leverage stays above 2x and the buyback is deferred.
  3. Load growth invites grid queues, local opposition and permit brakes (active; mostly timing). Texas paused data-centre environmental permits on 21 September 2026 pending an audit of 474 GW of interconnection requests, but at least 16 permits were still approved. Dominion's queue is 70 GW. Virginia SB 843 proposes no new rezonings or permits from July 2027 to July 2030. Spain proposes hourly 80% renewable matching. This loop is partly self-cancelling for Schneider: delay pushes developers behind the meter (22% of capacity under construction, 40% of the pipeline), which raises electrical content per megawatt. Financial effect: revenue timing slips of a few percent of data-centre revenue, not lost demand.
  4. High margins invite entrants in standard products (moderate, contained). Korean makers hold 19% of US transformer imports, LS Electric has a USD 125m US hyperscaler switchgear contract, and Liangxin targets RMB 300m of US data-centre revenue in 2027. The US national-emergency declaration on bulk-power security restricts Chinese grid equipment. Effect: pressure on low-voltage standard products and UPS, not on certified systems.
  5. Acquisitions invite leverage, a buyback pause and a lower multiple (active, financial). This does not feed back into operations, except through talent: AI and software attrition, with voluntary turnover at 13.9%, slows PTC, Cognite and AVEVA integration and puts revenue synergies at risk (P-08 and P-10).

How long the reinforcing loops run before the balancing loops dominate. Data-centre orders grew at triple digits in Q2 and Q3 2026 and convert in 12 to 24 months, so 2027 revenue is largely booked. Management claims visibility "into 2028". My judgement: the reinforcing loops dominate the level of growth through 2027, and the balancing loops dominate the rate of change from Q4 2027, when price comparisons toughen, US capacity ramps and data-centre order comparisons are hardest. 2028 is the year the debate turns from "how much higher" to "how flat". That is the Industry Digest's point about the plateau after 2028, arriving about a year earlier in the rate of change.

The earliest observable signals that the balance is shifting:

  • Q1 2027 data-centre orders against triple-digit comparisons (late April 2027).
  • The year-end 2026 backlog (disclosed only annually, on 18 February 2027; EUR 25.4bn, +18%, at end-2025).
  • Medium-voltage switchgear lead times below about 35 to 40 weeks.
  • Distributor surveys showing supplier price increases below 50%.
  • Neocloud high-yield spreads, and the Texas audit outcome and Virginia SB 843 vote.
  • Eaton's and Vertiv's order commentary, since Vertiv no longer discloses backlog.

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

A722nd of 38

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. Current guidance is conservative for the next one to two years and about fair for years three and four. In 2024 and 2025, with weaker input forces (destocking, flat residential, no data-centre order surge), Schneider grew 8.4% to 8.9% organically; with today's stronger forces (triple-digit data-centre orders, a short-cycle recovery, a record backlog, rising grid capex) a 7% to 10% framework looks low for 2027, and +250 basis points over five years looks low after +100 to 120 in year one. Only for 2029 and 2030, when capacity arrives and data-centre growth normalises, does the framework look appropriately prudent.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Near term: the FY26 guide implies Q4 at about 8% after 15% in the first nine months; backlog phasing and product momentum argue for more.
  • Next two years: data centres (about 30% of sales) guided at a 12% to 14% CAGR, which alone adds about 4 points; grid and non-residential mid-single-digit; products accelerating; price carry-over; that sums to roughly 9% to 11% organic in 2027 before any short-cycle surprise.
  • Margins: a 250 basis-point five-year target, against 100 to 120 basis points delivered in 2026 and productivity running about twice the plan, leaves about 35 basis points a year needed for 2027 to 2030 (C-13).
  • Offsets that justify some prudence: R&D rising to 7% of sales; the non-recurring EUR 100m tariff refund in 2026; competitor capacity from 2027; integration costs and purchase-price amortisation on the new acquisitions, which sit outside adjusted EBITA but reduce reported profit (PTC itself is margin-accretive).

Case simplicity

Group judgement

Risks

A683rd of 38

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The third set's question is whether the financial forecasts for the next 18 months can be trusted, and the answer is a clear yes for the operating lines and a qualified yes for earnings per share. The two earlier sets are congruent on what matters, and the October evidence resolved their differences in the second set's favour: the equity raise is done (25m shares at EUR 240), the first EUR 6bn of debt is priced (4.3%), PTC will contribute one quarter of 2027 against a full year of new shares, and the support-function ratio is falling because revenue is outgrowing a cost base that is still rising, not because costs are being cut. The FY2027 forecast rests on strengths the business has demonstrated in the last four quarters: converting a record backlog, realising price on specified products without losing share, and holding overhead growth below revenue growth. It does not need the strengths the business has yet to show (overhead removal, an 18% Industrial Automation margin, revenue synergies from three software platforms); those are excluded, haircut or dated beyond the window. The drivers are concentrated in a small number of visible, measurable things, of which the dominant one (data-centre conversion) is largely booked and the financing ones (shares, interest, closing date) have moved from estimate to fact. The cone for FY2027 adjusted EPS is about EUR 10.7 to 12.4 around a base of about EUR 11.75, a moderate ±8%, slightly negatively skewed, with consensus (EUR 12.61) at or above the bull edge; the operating cone is narrower, and the cone doubles in 2028 when normalisation, the capacity wave, full PTC consolidation and the interest on EUR 16bn arrive together. The company's disclosure confirms delivery twice a year with the best margin bridge in the peer group and gives nothing on orders, backlog or financing between those points, so the two drivers that could turn the case must be watched through peers, hyperscaler capex and credit markets. The internal transmission traces cleanly to adjusted EBITA and then stops; the company's "immediately accretive" statement on PTC will need reconciling to a FY2027 in which EPS grows about five points slower than operating profit. Finally, the multiple is half to two-thirds industry-driven, set by the sector's financing fear, with a company-specific discount that opened in October and that delivery can address. The steer for the memo: the probability that FY2027 revenue and adjusted EBITA land within 5% of the base is about 75% and 70%; for adjusted EPS about 60%; the single risk that would break the forecast is a financing-led pause in data-centre orders during 2027, which would hit FY2028 rather than FY2027; and the forecast's most likely failure mode against the market is not an operating miss but an EPS consensus that is 6% to 7% too high for reasons of share count and interest that are already knowable.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

F3032nd of 38

Judgement. The perimeter is wide (more than 100 countries, two segments, four end markets, many product lines from wiring devices to PLM software) and is widening through acquisitions. Width helps revenue predictability through diversification (regional divergences have offset each other) but hurts analytical predictability: organic growth, segment margins and the new software model are harder to forecast, margins are disclosed only half-yearly and the backlog only annually. PTC adds a different business model with its own cycle (discrete-manufacturing R&D budgets) and its own subscription transition.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Geography (2025 and 2026): North America about 39% of revenue; Western Europe, China and East Asia, and South Asia and International the rest; US dollar revenue EUR 15.1bn (from EUR 11.2bn in 2023); euro about 19%; China EUR 4.3bn.   • Lines: low and medium voltage, secure power, cooling, building management, grid software, home energy (Shelly, Apple), discrete, process and hybrid automation, robotics, industrial software (AVEVA, OSIsoft, ETAP, Cognite, then PTC).
