D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

A802nd of 38

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The change expected over the next 18 months is plausible on strengths the business has already demonstrated, and the model is built so that it does not need the strengths the business has yet to show. About four-fifths of the FY2027 earnings change against FY2025 comes from three things Schneider has visibly done in 2026: converting a record backlog at 15% to 17% organic growth, realising product price at 5% while holding share, and growing revenue faster than a near-frozen overhead. The three strengths it would need to acquire (removing overhead rather than outgrowing it, lifting Industrial Automation from 14% to 18%, and integrating three software platforms at once) are either excluded from the forecast (PTC revenue synergies at zero), haircut (Industrial Automation at 15.3% in 2027 and 16.3% in 2028 against the 18% target; AVEVA at two-thirds of its 30% target) or dated beyond the 18-month window (cost synergies from 2028). The plausibility of the FY2027 forecast is therefore high; the plausibility of the 2028 to 2030 progression is medium, because from 2028 the margin story depends on the un-demonstrated strengths and the growth-dependent lever weakens as growth normalises.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

Demonstrated strengths, with the record that proves them.

  • Backlog conversion and delivery at scale. Organic growth of 10.7%, 11.2%, 16.5% and 16.9% over four quarters, each above consensus (by 3.5, 1.1, 6 and 3.1 points); revenue of EUR 11,460m in Q3 2026; prefabricated capacity grown fourfold to 1.35 million square feet on eight sites with the first UL 2140 certification; Foxconn as asset-light North American capacity. The precedent that this scaling need not break margins is Eaton: Electrical Americas rose from 22.5% (2022) to 26.5% (2023) despite a 190 basis-point drag from growth investment, then absorbed a 270 basis-point ramp hit in Q1 2026 and recovered 190 basis points sequentially by Q2. Schneider's own 2009 (organic −15.7%, margin −310 basis points, EUR 541m of support cost removed) and 2020 (organic −4.7%, margin +20 basis points) show the downside is cushioned.
  • Pricing on specified products. Product price realisation of 1.7%, 3.7% and 5.1% (or 5.5%) over the three quarters of 2026 with share rising; indexation clauses on systems; a June 2026 exit rate that covers raw materials and tariffs; net price guided positive for the full year. The precedents for holding list prices through normalisation are strong (Legrand has never cut average prices in twenty years; no Western low-voltage list-price cuts in 2009, 2016, 2020 or the 2023 to 2025 destocking); the precedents for give-back are confined to standardised, uncertified products (Indian transformers −25% to −30% when Tier-2 capacity arrived) and to large tenders (hyperscalers "squeeze hardware margins to single digits", an expert; Schneider's UK subsidiary withdrew from fixed-price tenders in 2026 because prices were too low). The model's price contribution of 2.2 points in 2027 fading to 1.0 by 2029 sits inside what has been demonstrated.
  • Volume leverage on a semi-fixed overhead. The ratio fell 1.1 points in H1 2026 and 0.8 points in FY2025, in both cases because revenue outgrew a rising cost base (note 5). This is a demonstrated mechanism, but it is a mechanism with a condition attached: it only works above about 5% revenue growth. The model's 2027 (10.5% organic) is well inside the condition; 2029 and 2030 (7.5%) are inside it with less margin.
  • Margin catch-up room is real. Energy Management at 22.3% to 22.4% against Eaton Electrical 26.3% and ABB Electrification 25.6%; Energy Management gained 430 basis points in 2019 to 2024 against ABB's 990 and Eaton's 730. The gap is the reservoir; ABB's decentralised model and Eaton's volume leverage show it can be drained. Redburn models Energy Management at 23.7% (2027) and 24.3% (2028); the second set has 23.2% and 23.5%, which is comfortably inside the demonstrated precedent.

Strengths not yet demonstrated, and how the model treats them.

  • Removing overhead rather than outgrowing it. Support costs rose as a share of sales in 2023 and 2024 despite targets; gross savings in FY2025 (EUR 349m) were more than offset by inflation and reinvestment (EUR 748m). Multi-Hub 2.0 (four regions from January 2026), AI@scale (Morgan Stanley's EUR 400m of annual savings in 2025 and 2026) and EUR 800m to 950m of restructuring over three years are the tools; the proof that they remove cost rather than slow its growth does not yet exist. The model does not need it before 2028, because it assumes support costs keep growing at 5.5% to 7% a year. The precedents that it can be done (ABB's White Collar Productivity programme, USD 1.3bn of run-rate savings with headquarters headcount halved; Rockwell's USD 435m of savings with a 12% workforce cut) all involved a downturn or a change of operating model, not a growth phase.
  • Industrial Automation from 14.0% to 18% by 2028. Standalone organic growth of 8% in Q3 2026 with process weakness in China; the H1 2026 margin miss of 60 to 70 basis points; the EUR 128m impairment on range simplification; Chinese domestic share gains (Inovance at record highs in servos and PLCs). Against that, Rockwell's segment margins of 23.5% by 2028 show the hardware ceiling is far higher, and Chinese competitors are raising prices (Inovance's fourth increase of 2026, 8% to 35% on HMIs; Xinje 8% to 20%), which is a tailwind to Western pricing in China. The model's 16.3% in 2028 is a haircut of about 40% of the targeted improvement.
  • AVEVA from 17% to 30% by 2028. AVEVA's own margin was 29.5% in 2021 before the SaaS transition cut it to 19.5% in 2022, so 30% is a recovery, not a new level; Siemens' software margin path (12% in 2025 to 28% by 2030) is the sector precedent; ARR grew 10% in Q3 2026. But AVEVA's margin fell year on year in H1 2025 on weak organic sales and renewals in the Gulf were lumpy in Q3 2026. The model credits two-thirds.
  • Integrating PTC, Cognite and AVEVA at once. The record is mixed. Invensys (2014): 90% of synergy initiatives executed in the first phase and double-digit first-year EPS accretion with EUR 500m of tax synergies. AVEVA and OSIsoft (2021): cost synergies raised above target, but sales-force turnover and a "disjointed" integration that created client confusion (expert); AVEVA's integration and SaaS transition weighed on Industrial Automation margins (Morgan Stanley, 2024). New-product software "plagued by bugs" with early customers as "guinea pigs" (former director, March 2026); "significant" loss of AI and software talent over 18 months; the unified data model across PTC, Cognite and AVEVA described as highly complex (former AVEVA R&D head). Sector precedents: Altair's integration cost Siemens 250 basis points of Digital Industries margin in a quarter and 120 basis points for the year, and consensus mis-modelled EUR 150m of integration cost; Synopsys raised its Ansys restructuring budget from USD 300m to 350m to USD 380m to 410m with a 10% headcount cut. Cost synergies of EUR 250m (10% of PTC sales, below the 13% precedent average) are plausible; revenue synergies of EUR 800m (33% of PTC sales) have no precedent at that scale. The model carries cost synergies phased 30%, 70%, 100% over 2028 to 2030 and no revenue synergies.

The arithmetic of reliance, FY2025 to FY2027. Adjusted EBITA rises from EUR 7,508m to about EUR 10,477m (+EUR 2,970m). Of that, about EUR 2,500m is volume and mix on the standalone business (demonstrated), about EUR 400m is net price-cost in 2027 (demonstrated in H2 2026 but not through a full cost cycle), about EUR 260m is PTC's own fourth-quarter profit (an acquired asset, not a synergy), and roughly EUR 100m is Industrial Automation and AVEVA improvement (partly undemonstrated). Nothing in FY2027 requires revenue synergies, overhead removal or a full Industrial Automation recovery. From FY2028 the composition shifts: PTC's full year (about EUR 1.1bn of EBITA, acquired), cost synergies (new strength), Industrial Automation and AVEVA (new strengths) and organic expansion of only +30 to +40 basis points a year (the demonstrated lever, weakening).

Where the plausibility could fail inside 18 months. Three tests. First, under-absorption of the prefabricated capacity if data-centre orders pause (Eaton's Q1 2026 shows a 270 basis-point hit is possible for a quarter). Second, hyperscaler tender pricing eroding systems margins before backlog repricing lands (the expert evidence on single-digit hardware margins in negotiations). Third, a renewed metals spike: Morgan Stanley's January 2026 estimate put the silver and copper spike at a potential EUR 743m year-on-year headwind requiring 1.8% of price just to break even, and the H1 2026 outcome (gross pricing +EUR 280m against −EUR 434m of raw materials and tariffs) shows the lag is real.

D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?B608th of 38full answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

B+625th of 38

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The cone for the operating lines over the next 18 months is narrow; the cone for earnings per share is moderately narrow; and against consensus the skew is clearly negative because consensus sits at the top of our cone. On my revised base, FY2027 adjusted EPS of about EUR 11.75 carries a plausible range of about EUR 10.7 to 12.4 (about −9% to +6%, a cone of about ±8%), with roughly 20% weight on each tail, and a probability-weighted outcome near EUR 11.65. Consensus at EUR 12.61 (median) or 12.44 (mean) lies at or above the bull case. The broker range of EUR 10.65 (Santander) to 13.67 (Berenberg, UBS), a 28% spread, is not the cone of outcomes: it is the dispersion of modelling choices on share count, debt cost and PTC consolidation, and it will narrow mechanically as those choices are forced by facts. Beyond 18 months the cone widens sharply, because 2028 is the year in which data-centre normalisation, the capacity wave, a full year of PTC and the interest on EUR 16bn all land together. The case is predictable over 18 months on operations, less predictable on EPS, and conditional on one trigger (data-centre financing) that sits outside the company's disclosure.

The cone for FY2027, with the drivers of each edge (my arithmetic).

  • Bear, about 20%: EPS about EUR 10.7. A financing-led pause in data-centre orders from early 2027 (neocloud credit stress spreading to colocation; the Heatmap data via Morgan Stanley show USD 260bn of planned US data-centre investment cancelled in 2026, USD 130bn of it in Q3; Bernstein counts only 35% of the announced US pipeline as credible; New York's one-year moratorium on new hyperscale data centres from July 2026; Texas's permit pause), short-cycle products rolling over, a 2% currency drag and under-absorption of prefabricated capacity: organic growth about 6.5%, standalone margin +20 basis points, the remaining debt priced in dollars near 5.5%. FY2028 in this case is about EUR 11.2, because the order pause reaches revenue with a 12-to-24-month lag and margin runs in reverse (the frozen overhead is exposed), with leverage staying above 2x and the 2029 buyback deferred.
  • Base, about 60%: EPS about EUR 11.75. Organic growth 10.5%, margin 20.5% (+75 basis points underlying, front-loaded into H1 2027 on price carry-over), currency neutral, PTC from 1 October 2027, debt at 4.6%, 589m shares.
  • Bull, about 20%: EPS about EUR 12.4. Backlog converts faster (Morgan Stanley's 12% organic for 2027; Redburn's EUR 52.3bn of sales, 5% above consensus), Europe recovers early, metals ease (each 10% fall in copper and silver with list prices held is about +45 basis points), the remaining debt prices in euros near 4.3%, PTC closes 1 July 2027. FY2028 about EUR 14.8.
  • Shape. Slightly negatively skewed around my base (the bear tail is deeper than the bull tail is high, because under-absorption and leverage compound on the downside and the trigger has the least visibility); clearly negatively skewed around consensus.

Why the operating cone is narrower than the EPS cone. Nine months of 2026 are reported. The H1 2027 margin is supported by two near-mechanical effects (the annualisation of Q2 to Q4 2026 price increases against a slower cost annualisation, and the comparison against H1 2026's net price of −EUR 154m), and restructuring falls by EUR 200m. A five-point swing in FY2027 organic growth moves EBITA by about EUR 450m to 650m (5% to 7% of EPS); the operating cone for revenue is roughly ±4% and for adjusted EBITA roughly ±6%. The EPS cone adds the financing variables: a quarter of PTC timing (1% to 1.5%), 100 basis points on the new debt (1.5%), a 10% dollar move (5%), and tax within the 23% to 25% range (2.6% between the ends).

What history says about dispersion for this kind of company. Morgan Stanley's series: sector consensus EPS was cut about 40% in 2002 to 2003 and 2008 to 2009, 15% to 20% in the milder 2011 to 2012 and 2015 to 2016 slowdowns, and the sector multiple de-rated 29% in the 2012 to 2013 crisis and 33% at the start of the pandemic before estimates moved. Schneider's own FY2025 consensus fell 12% from its peak (EUR 9.77 to 8.59) on currency and interest while organic delivery beat. The lesson is that the deep cuts came with capital-expenditure freezes, not with normalisations, and that the multiple moves before the estimates. The current case has a structural buffer the historical cases lacked (a record backlog described by an expert as seven to eight months of cushion, two-to-three-year hyperscaler commitments, 18 to 24 months of order-book visibility) and a structural fragility they also lacked (debt-financed demand at the margin; the four large hyperscalers' aggregate free cash flow fell from about USD 60bn in late 2025 to about USD 7bn in mid-2026 on Jefferies' count, so even the strong customers are now financing capex from debt and equity, as Meta's USD 95bn raise shows).

Data-centre order surges and reversals. The precedents asked for in the dispersion report are thin but consistent: Marvell's data-centre spending spiked above trend in 2017 to 2018 and corrected over several quarters; Vertiv's orders went from flat in Q4 2024 to +13% in Q1 2025 and now above +100%; Eaton's data-centre orders rose about 200% in late 2025. Pauses within 18 months of a surge are common; declines in revenue within 18 months are not, because of the lag. That is why the bear case hits FY2028 harder than FY2027.

How wide is the cone on the lines the Consumer will model?

  • FY2026: revenue within ±1% (nine months reported), adjusted EBITA margin 19.7% to 19.9% guided, mine at 19.9%; EPS EUR 10.2 to 10.5.
  • H1 2027: revenue growth about 12% to 16% reported; margin about 19.8% to 20.4%.
  • FY2027: revenue EUR 49.5bn to 52.3bn; adjusted EBITA EUR 9.9bn to 10.9bn; EPS EUR 10.7 to 12.4.
  • FY2028: EPS EUR 11.2 to 14.8, a cone of about ±13%, roughly double FY2027's.

Predictability verdict. Over 18 months, high on revenue and adjusted EBITA, moderate on EPS, and with a known calendar on which the uncertainty resolves: the FY2026 results and FY2027 guide on 18 February 2027 (backlog, net price, support-function ratio), the remaining debt tranches, the PTC vote, and the Q1 2027 revenue release in late April 2027 (the first data-centre comparison against a triple-digit base).

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

C-5216th of 38

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The disclosure is helpful for the margin model and unhelpful for the thing that most needs watching in the next six months. Schneider gives the Consumer quarterly organic growth by segment and by four regions, a product-against-systems split, qualitative data-centre commentary, quarterly currency guidance and, twice a year, a complete margin bridge (gross price, raw materials, tariffs, productivity, the support-function ratio, net price, restructuring) that peers do not match. It does not give quarterly orders, book-to-bill, backlog by region or customer type, data-centre backlog, regional support costs, or a precise PTC closing date, and it discloses margin and backlog only at the half-year and full-year. Over the six months to the end of April 2027 there are exactly two company data points (the FY2026 results on 18 February 2027 and the Q1 2027 revenue release in late April), so the Consumer will have to monitor the two drivers that matter most, data-centre order momentum and its financing, through peers and third parties. The disclosure is good for confirming delivery after the fact and poor for anticipating a turn.

What the company will disclose, and when.

  • 18 February 2027, FY2026 results. The H2 2026 and FY2026 adjusted EBITA margin (guided 19.7% to 19.9% including the EUR 100m tariff refund); the H2 net price bridge (management has committed to positive net price for the year, implying at least +EUR 150m in H2 against −EUR 154m in H1); productivity (EUR 535m in H1); the support-function ratio (23.2% in H1; mine about 22.3% for the year); free cash flow (record H1 of EUR 1,631m; conversion target about 100%); restructuring (about EUR 450m); the year-end backlog (EUR 25.4bn, +18%, at end-2025; disclosed only annually, this is the single most useful number for the 2027 forecast); the FY2027 guidance (organic growth and organic margin, with PTC separated); net debt and the buyback completed; Industrial Automation's margin against 14.0% in H1 and the 18% target. The 2026 universal registration document (March 2027) will add headcount by region, factoring, contract assets and liabilities, the capitalised R&D balance and the PPA policy.
  • Late April 2027, Q1 2027 revenue. Organic growth by segment and region, products against systems, qualitative data-centre commentary against a comparison of +11.2% (Q1 2026) and, for data centres, against the first of the triple-digit order quarters. No margin, no orders, no backlog.
  • Transaction disclosures. The PTC proxy statement (background, bridge terms), the PTC shareholder vote, HSR and CFIUS progress; the launch of the remaining roughly EUR 10bn of debt (currency, tenor, coupon); PTC's own Q4 FY2026 results on 4 November 2026 and its FY2027 guidance (the standalone baseline for ARR growth of about 10%).

What the company does not disclose, and what replaces it.

  • Orders and backlog quarterly. ABB (Electrification orders +70%, book-to-bill 1.24 to 1.41, backlog USD 16bn, reported 20 October 2026, next in early February 2027), Eaton (data-centre orders, backlog, next in early February 2027), Vertiv (orders above +100%, no backlog since it stopped disclosing, next in February 2027), Siemens Smart Infrastructure (orders +42%, results 12 November 2026), Legrand (Q3 on 5 November 2026, data-centre growth guided 25% to 30% for 2026). These are the proxies; the risk is that Schneider's own order mix (hyperscale and colocation, North America and China) differs from each peer's.
  • Demand financing. Hyperscaler quarterly capex guidance (Microsoft, Alphabet, Meta and Amazon report again in late January and early February 2027); CoreWeave and other neocloud bond yields and credit default swaps; data-centre asset-backed securities spreads; the Texas audit outcome and Virginia SB 843; Morgan Stanley's distributor survey (pricing breadth and lead times); Jefferies' satellite-based pipeline tracking.
  • Price and cost. Copper, silver and steel; Morgan Stanley's distributor survey on supplier price increases; benchmark switchgear and medium-voltage transformer lead times; competitor capacity milestones (Eaton's remaining six of 24 projects; ABB's USD 110m Virginia and North Carolina expansion; LS Electric and Liangxin in US switchgear).
  • The overhead mechanism. No quarterly support-cost figure exists; the Consumer sees it twice a year, and headcount once a year. The mechanism is growth-dependent, so quarterly organic growth is the real-time proxy.

How to read the February 2027 release. Three readings decide the 18-month case. First, the year-end backlog: growth above about 15% confirms 2027 conversion at or above the second set's 10.5% organic; growth below 10% says the triple-digit order quarters were pulled forward and that 2027 is a conversion year with a thinner 2028. Second, the H2 net price line: at least +EUR 150m confirms the H1 2027 price carry-over; a figure near zero says pricing is fading earlier than assumed and the 2027 margin gain is closer to +40 basis points than +75. Third, the FY2027 guidance basis: the company guides organically and will almost certainly separate PTC; the risk is a reported-growth comparison against a consensus that already includes acquisitions, producing a headline "miss" on the day.

Peer contrast. ABB, Eaton and Vertiv disclose orders quarterly and margins quarterly; Legrand and Siemens disclose margins quarterly. Schneider's semi-annual margin and annual backlog put it at the bottom of the peer group on frequency and near the top on bridge quality. Redburn's and Morgan Stanley's complaints (investors "must wait until February" to confirm productivity; quarterly channel checks are needed for backlog) are fair.

Score reasoning. The question asks how helpful the disclosure is for monitoring over about six months. It is adequate for confirming the margin and cash thesis at one point (February), and it leaves the order and financing thesis to be monitored externally. Redburn's observation that the company, unlike its major peers, discloses neither regional data-centre backlog nor quarterly book-to-bill is the decisive weakness for this window.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

C+5210th of 38

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The internal transmission is clear and quantified from guidance to adjusted EBITA, and then it goes dark. Schneider's targets are expressed in the lines it reports (organic growth by segment and region, organic margin in basis points, productivity in euros, the support-function ratio, net price, restructuring, currency, tax within 23% to 25%, capex intensity, cash conversion, a data-centre growth rate to 2030), and each can be checked against a disclosed number twice a year. Below adjusted EBITA the company guides nothing: not interest, not the share count, not adjusted EPS, and its one statement on EPS (PTC "immediately accretive to adjusted EPS") is true only on a full-year pro forma basis and false for FY2027 as it will be reported. The external transmission is also clear in mechanism and sensitivity, with lags that are known in direction and roughly in length: hyperscaler capex to data-centre orders to systems revenue over 12 to 24 months; metals to product margin over three to six months; competitor capacity to lead times to price over 18 to 24 months; the dollar to translation immediately. The chain is traceable; the two links that are not are the ones where the surprises will come, namely the financing arithmetic and order financing.

The internal chain, link by link.

  1. Company target to operating driver. FY2026: organic growth 12% to 14%, organic adjusted EBITA growth +18% to +21%, margin 19.7% to 19.9%, currency −EUR 300m to 400m on revenue, restructuring about EUR 450m, buyback EUR 600m. 2030 framework: organic growth 7% to 10% a year, +250 basis points of cumulative organic margin, productivity EUR 2bn to 2.5bn, support-function ratio −1.5 to −2 points, R&D to 7% of sales, disposals EUR 1bn to 1.5bn, Industrial Automation 18% by 2028, AVEVA 30% by 2028, data centres 12% to 14% CAGR, free cash flow conversion about 100%, ROCE 15% to 20%, buybacks EUR 2.5bn to 3.5bn. Every operating target names its driver.
  2. Operating driver to reported line. The half-year margin bridge is complete: gross product price (+EUR 280m in H1 2026), raw materials (−EUR 330m), tariffs (−EUR 104m), net price (−EUR 154m), industrial productivity (+EUR 535m), the support-function bridge (gross savings EUR 182m, reinvestment EUR 160m, inflation EUR 129m), the tariff refund (EUR 100m), and currency and scope shown separately. Segment margins are reported (Energy Management 22.4%, Industrial Automation 14.0% in H1 2026), with central costs. This is model-ready and better than any peer's bridge.
  3. Reported line to adjusted EPS. Here the company stops. Brokers' adjusted EPS is struck after restructuring (so the EUR 450m falling to EUR 250m is a visible +2.5 points of 2027 EPS growth that the company never mentions), before purchase-price amortisation (which rises from about EUR 420m to about EUR 600m in 2027 and somewhere between EUR 0.6bn and 1.25bn from 2028 depending on the PTC allocation; the Altair precedent allocated 80% to goodwill, Ansys 67%, so my own EUR 1.25bn estimate is at the high end) and before other operating items. Net financial items of about −EUR 715m in 2025 are far larger than FactSet's net interest line of −EUR 553m, because lease and pension interest, high-inflation currency losses and put-liability unwinds sit there. Interest on the new debt, the share count and the tax rate are not guided; the PTC accretion claim is on a basis the company has not defined. The Consumer must build this link from the prospectus (25m shares), the bond press release (4.3%), the company's 23% to 25% tax range and the brokers' basis.
  4. Adjusted EPS to the measures the market uses. Reported EPS will be about EUR 1 below adjusted in 2026 and about EUR 2 below from 2028; the company's ROCE excludes EUR 10.2bn of minority buyouts and will need a stated treatment of PTC's EUR 21bn; "about 100% cash conversion" is of reported net income, not adjusted. Each of these invites a basis dispute on results day.

The external chain, link by link, with the sensitivities the Consumer should carry.

  • Hyperscaler and colocation capex to orders to revenue. Capex guidance (Alphabet USD 195bn to 205bn for 2026; Meta USD 135bn to 145bn; Microsoft accelerating into H2 FY2027 after delayed third-party capacity) leads orders by one to two quarters; orders lead revenue by 12 to 24 months for systems (prefabrication compresses this: switchgear lead times from 88 to 34 weeks in a modular build, per Bernstein); 1 point of group organic growth is about EUR 90m of adjusted EBITA (about 1% of EPS). The link that is not visible is between a customer's financing and its orders: credit widens first (neocloud and asset-backed spreads), then projects are deferred (Oracle's force majeure at Project Jupiter; SoftBank's transformer deferral to 2029, which was regulatory rather than financial), then orders slow. Behind-the-meter generation (22% of capacity under construction) partly offsets by raising content per megawatt.
  • Metals and tariffs to gross margin to EPS. Copper and silver are about half of a raw-material bill that is about 9% of revenue; hedging covers about 20% of the top three commodities with a twelve-month taper; list-price pass-through runs three to six months on products and through backlog indexation on systems. A 10% move in copper and silver is about EUR 230m or 45 basis points of margin (about 2.5% of EPS) if unrecovered for a year.
  • Competitor capacity to lead times to price. About USD 6bn of capacity ramping over 18 months (Bernstein); Eaton's USD 1.5bn programme 75% complete; benchmark US switchgear lead times 40 weeks from 60; the share of distributors receiving supplier price increases 54% from 68%. The transmission to Schneider's price is through products first (list prices, fast but sticky) and systems later (tender pricing, slow); each point of price on products is roughly EUR 200m of revenue at near-full drop-through.
  • Currency. 10% in the US dollar is EUR 1,505m of revenue and EUR 427m of adjusted EBITA (about 5% of EPS), unhedged; the yuan is EUR 434m and EUR 64m.
  • Rates to the financing line. 100 basis points on the new debt is about EUR 160m before tax (1.5% of EPS); the EUR 750m floating tranche and any commercial paper add a small direct rate sensitivity; the ECB is expected to raise its deposit rate to 2.75% in December 2026 and the US ten-year is near 5%.
  • Regulation to timing. Permit pauses and moratoria (Texas, New York, Virginia SB 843) move revenue between quarters rather than remove it; the SF6 steps (2026, 2028, 2030, 2032) add dated replacement demand in medium voltage.

Where the trace is weakest, and what it costs. First, the PTC accretion statement, which will have to be reconciled on 18 February 2027 to a FY2027 in which adjusted EPS grows about 13% to 14% against operating profit growth of about 19%; the risk is a credibility event on a basis question rather than an operating one. Second, the growth-dependence of the support-function ratio, which the company presents as a cost programme and which behaves as leverage; a growth shock would show up as a margin miss that the bridge would then "explain" by inflation and reinvestment. Third, no order or backlog series, so the first external link is monitored through peers. Fourth, the Industrial Automation margin path to 18% has no interim milestones.

Milestones and measures to carry in the memo, in the order they will be reported. FY2026 organic growth at or above 13.5% and margin at or above 19.8% (18 February 2027); year-end backlog growth above about 15%; H2 2026 net price at or above +EUR 150m; support-function ratio for FY2026 at or below about 22.5%; FY2027 organic guidance midpoint at or above 9% and margin guide at or above +50 basis points on an organic basis; the coupon and currency of the remaining EUR 10bn; Q1 2027 organic growth above 10% with data centres still growing double digits against the Q1 2026 base; the PTC shareholder vote and regulatory clearances by mid-2027; H1 2027 margin at or above 20.0% (late July 2027); PTC closing by 1 October 2027 with a stated PPA and integration-cost phasing.

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

Judgement. Sentiment risk is high and already partly realised. Schneider is a crowded, widely owned AI-infrastructure supplier in a risk-off, rising-rate regime, with a pending equity raise and a fresh capital-allocation controversy; it can fall on any AI-financing headline, 800V DC narrative, peer miss or rate move regardless of its own delivery. The share price has already fallen about 26% from its 52-week high while estimates rose, so part of this risk is in the price.

Risks.

  • AI-financing contagion: credit widening in neoclouds and hyperscalers; Goldman's risk-appetite indicator negative; technology net selling heaviest since January 2025.

  • Software de-rating: "SaaS-pocalypse" sentiment on any software holding; PTC adds software weight to Schneider's multiple.
  • Peer events: Siemens Energy's 2030 targets (11 November), Siemens (12 November), Legrand Q3 (5 November), Vertiv or Eaton misses on project delays; ABB's Q4 order comparison.
  • Macro and country: US 10-year near 5.10%, oil at USD 111, French sovereign spreads at decade highs (country FR Double Underweight), risk-off with Momentum at Underweight.

C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

B728th of 38

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. Under Blum, the pattern is to set an initial range that the business can beat, hold it at Q1, and raise it at the half-year and again at Q3 as visibility allows, on both growth and margin. It is disciplined rather than precise: the company delivered the bottom of its 2025 margin range (currency-driven), so it does not always beat. The upgrade rhythm is tied to the half-yearly margin disclosure.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • 2026: growth 7% to 10% (February), reaffirmed at Q1 despite an 11.2% print (Deutsche Bank called it "highly conservative"), raised to 10% to 13% (July), raised to 12% to 14% (October); margin +50 to 80, then +70 to 100, then +100 to 120 basis points.
  • 2025: growth 7% to 10% delivered at 8.9%; margin +50 to 80 basis points delivered at +50 (bottom), attributed to currency rather than execution.
  • 2024: organic growth tracked the 6% to 8% target closely; free-cash-flow expectations were cut (Redburn, September 2024).
  • Mid-term: the CMD extended the 7% to 10% growth framework to 2030 rather than raising it; Deutsche Bank expects the EUR 2bn to 2.5bn productivity objective to be revised upward.
  • Behaviour of the chief executive: confident framing ("halftime", "executing precisely as presented"), but formal ranges kept prudent; consistent with a new team protecting credibility after the 2025 margin outcome.
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

C-5617th of 38

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement. Over one year the record is one of consistent beats against both guidance and consensus on growth, margin and cash. Over three years it is a beat on organic growth but a miss on reported earnings versus earlier expectations, because currency, price-cost lag and interest absorbed operating gains (FY2025 EPS consensus once reached EUR 9.77 and the outcome was EUR 8.59). Over five years and through the 2020 shock, margins were protected well. The lesson for the model: lean towards beats on organic growth and margin; do not lean on reported EPS beats when the euro is strengthening.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Last twelve months: Q4 2025 organic +10.7% against 7.2% consensus; Q1 2026 +11.2% against 10.1%; Q2 2026 +16.5% against 10.6%; H1 adjusted EBITA EUR 4,093m against EUR 3,822m (+7%), margin 19.3% against 18.5%, free cash flow EUR 1,631m against EUR 1,396m (+17%), productivity EUR 272m above; Q3 2026 revenue EUR 11,460m against EUR 11,127m (+3%), organic +310 basis points. The one miss: Industrial Automation margin (14.0% against 14.6%) and Q1 Industrial Automation growth (4.4% against 5.5%).
  • Three years: FY2024 "slight P&L miss" and a free-cash-flow cut (Redburn, September 2024); FY2025 organic growth inside guidance but margin at the bottom; FY2025 reported EPS +3% after a EUR 482m currency hit to EBITA; the FactSet EPS series shows FY2025 consensus ranging from EUR 9.77 down to the EUR 8.59 outcome, and FY2026 from EUR 11.10 down to 9.75 before recovering to EUR 10.33.
  • Comparable conditions: in 2020 (revenue −7.4%) the margin held; in the 2023 to 2025 destocking cycle, Industrial Automation lagged and gross margin slipped, which is the condition to watch if short-cycle demand rolls over again.
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

A623rd of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. The forecasts deserve high weight for FY2026 and the operating lines of FY2027, moderate weight for FY2027 EPS and FY2028, and low weight for FY2029 and FY2030. The lean depends on the line:

  • Organic growth and adjusted EBITA margin over the next 18 months: lean upward. The new team's record since late 2025 is consistent beats, and guidance is set prudently and raised at the half-year and Q3.
  • EPS and free cash flow: lean neutral to down. Over three to five years, reported EPS has repeatedly landed below earlier expectations because of currency, interest, price-cost lags and capital-allocation choices, even when organic delivery beat.
  • Software synergies, the Industrial Automation margin target and anything that depends on a buyback restart: lean down.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • The recent record. Organic growth beat consensus in Q4 2025 (10.7% against 7.2%), Q1 2026 (11.2% against 10.1%), Q2 2026 (16.5% against 10.6%) and Q3 2026 (16.9%, 310bp above). The H1 2026 adjusted EBITA margin of 19.3% was 80bp above consensus, productivity EUR 272m above, and free cash flow 17% above. Guidance was raised twice. For revenue and adjusted EBITA in FY2026 and H1 2027, lean to the top of the guided range. I sit at 13.9% organic and 19.9% margin for FY2026.
  • The longer record. FY2025 EPS consensus once stood at EUR 9.77, and the outcome was EUR 8.59 (−12%). FY2026 consensus fell from EUR 11.10 to EUR 9.75 before recovering to EUR 10.33. The FY2025 margin came in at the bottom of +50bp to +80bp (currency). FY2024 brought a "slight P&L miss" and a free-cash-flow cut (Redburn, September 2024). Energy Management's margin expansion from 2019 to 2024 (+430bp) was well below ABB's and Eaton's. The pattern is consistent: the organic engine delivers, and what sits between operating profit and EPS (currency, interest, acquisitions, share count) has repeatedly taken it away. In 2027 and 2028 the below-the-line items are again adverse: a 4% share increase, interest roughly doubling, and the tax rate rising.
  • Capital-allocation delivery is the weakest strand. Management said "M&A was not a prerequisite" in December 2025 and announced a USD 23.7bn acquisition ten months later. The India minority cost about EUR 1bn more than expected. Earlier software integrations (AVEVA and OSIsoft) saw sales-force turnover. Lean down on PTC revenue synergies (EUR 800m targeted; Bernstein finds about EUR 300m of conventional cross-sell), and give only partial credit for cost synergies. I model EUR 250m of cost synergies phased to 2030, and no revenue synergies beyond PTC's standalone 10% growth.
  • Comparable conditions. In the 2020 downturn the margin held. In the 2023 to 2025 short-cycle destocking, Industrial Automation lagged and gross margin slipped. If short-cycle demand rolls over again, lean the Industrial Automation line down first.
  • Weights I would apply (confidence that the outcome falls within ±5% of my base):
  • FY2026 revenue and adjusted EBITA: about 90%. Nine months are reported.
  • FY2027 revenue: about 75%. Adjusted EBITA about 70%.
  • FY2027 EPS: about 60%. PTC closing date, funding cost and currency all bear on it.
  • FY2028: about 50%. Data-centre normalisation and the capacity wave both land in this year.
  • FY2029 and FY2030: about 35% to 40%, essentially scenario-dependent.

Direction against consensus, line by line.

  • FY2026: in line, with an upward lean on margin.
  • FY2027: revenue and EBITDA in line. EPS about 6% below consensus, from share count and interest.
  • FY2028: revenue and EBITDA above consensus because of a full year of PTC. EPS about 5% below, for the same below-the-line reasons.
  • The Consumer should treat "beat on operations, miss on EPS" as the most likely pattern for FY2027, which is the same pattern as FY2025.

Group judgement

Financial forecasts

B609th of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts are strong at operating level and ordinary at the per-share level for the next two years. Revenue grows about 10% a year to 2030 against 6.7% historically. The adjusted EBITA margin rises about 400bp (from 18.7% to 22.7%) against about 55bp a year historically. Free cash flow doubles. These rest mostly on mechanical forces: booked data-centre orders converting over 12 to 24 months, a semi-fixed overhead outgrown by revenue, price carry-over in 2027, and contracted backlog repricing to 2028.

Three judgements should steer the third and fourth sets:

  • The margin path is more growth-dependent than the self-help story implies. The 2026 support-function gain came from freezing a cost base that had grown 24%, not from cutting it. A growth shock would expose it.
  • Below the operating line, the PTC transaction turns 2027 into a dilution year. It brings about 4% more shares for the full year, one quarter of PTC profit, and interest that roughly doubles by 2028. That is why my FY2027 and FY2028 EPS sit 5% to 6% below consensus, while my revenue, EBITDA and net income sit in line with or slightly above it.
  • The forecasts are concentrated in a few drivers: data-centre conversion and its financing; the price fade as competitor capacity lands from late 2027; and the funding cost and timing of PTC. That concentration makes the 18-month case predictable on operations and less predictable on EPS.

For an investment case, the forecasts support strong earnings growth (EPS CAGR of about 15% from 2025 to 2030, of high quality because it is organic). They do not support near-term upgrades to EPS consensus, which should drift down on share count even as operating estimates rise. And they do not support capital returns before 2029.

C-49What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?A723rd of 38full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

F3429th of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Below the operating line, the next three years reverse the 2026 tailwinds and add new drags.

  • In 2026, two hidden tailwinds help EPS growth: removing the India minority (+EUR 0.23 of EPS) and an equity raise too late in the year to dilute much.
  • In 2027 and 2028, EPS growth runs about 5 points (2027) and 3 points (2028) below growth in operating profit after restructuring (+19% in each year). The causes are a 4.1% larger share count with no buyback, net financial items nearly doubling (−EUR 715m to −EUR 1,360m), and the tax rate rising from 23.5% to about 24.8% on PTC's US profit mix.
  • From 2029, deleveraging and a resumed buyback (about EUR 1.1bn in 2029 and EUR 1.3bn in 2030) turn the below-the-line items into a tailwind of about 2 points a year.

The results:

  • Adjusted EPS: EUR 8.59 (2025), 10.36 (2026), 11.85 (2027), 13.73 (2028), 15.37 (2029) and 17.11 (2030). That is a 14.8% CAGR, double the 7.2% of 2019 to 2025, but on a path where 2027 growth (+14%) is the weakest of 2026 to 2028.
  • Free cash flow after leases: EUR 4.48bn, 5.50bn, 6.81bn, 7.71bn and 8.66bn. On the company's own definition, before lease payments: EUR 4.91bn, 5.96bn, 7.30bn, 8.23bn and 9.21bn.
  • The adjusted-to-reported gap widens from about 14% to about 18%, because purchase-price amortisation roughly triples.

Reasoning and critical evidence, item by item.

  • Interest and other financial items (see P-02 for the debt path). From 2019 to 2025, FactSet net interest rose from −EUR 246m to −EUR 553m, and it was a leading reason EPS growth (7.2% CAGR) lagged organic operating-profit growth. The same happens again in 2027 and 2028, at a larger scale. My base is −EUR 820m, −1,020m, −1,360m, −1,240m and −1,130m. The swing factor is the coupon on EUR 16bn to 17bn of new debt (each 100bp is about 1.5% of EPS) and whether it is raised in dollars.
  • Tax. The rate was 23.1% (2024), 23.5% (2025) and 24.0% (H1 2026), against a guide of 23% to 25%. PTC's US earnings and the global minimum tax push it towards the top. I use 24.0%, 24.3% and then 24.8% from 2028. Each point is about 1.3% of EPS.
  • Minorities and associates. Minorities were EUR 188m in 2025, mostly the Indian operating company. Buying Temasek's 35% (EUR 5.5bn, 18 December 2025) removes about EUR 130m to 150m of leakage from 2026. That is worth about 2.5 points of 2026 EPS growth, before the roughly EUR 125m after-tax interest on the EUR 5.5bn used to fund it, so the net gain is about +1 point. The remaining 25% of Motivair is already a financial liability (EUR 278m at acquisition), with a put/call in 2028 that I model as about −EUR 350m of cash. Residual minorities less associates come to about −EUR 20m to −30m a year.
  • Restructuring. EUR 450m (2026) falls to EUR 250m (2027), EUR 200m (2028) and EUR 175m thereafter. It is deducted in adjusted EPS, so the fall adds about EUR 0.26 of EPS growth in 2027. Integration and M&A costs (PTC implementation of EUR 250m; Cognite and Shelly deal costs) are excluded from adjusted EPS but cost about EUR 150m to 250m a year in cash from 2026 to 2029.
  • Purchase-price amortisation (excluded from adjusted EPS, included in reported). About EUR 420m in 2026, about EUR 600m in 2027 (Cognite, Shelly, one quarter of PTC) and about EUR 1.25bn from 2028 (my estimate of PTC's identifiable intangibles; the opening balance sheet will be audited only in early 2028). Reported EPS will be about EUR 2 below adjusted EPS from 2028, which matters for any reader using statutory P/E or payout on reported earnings.
  • Shares.
  • End-2025: 562.2m.
  • 2026: the EUR 600m buyback removes about 2.2m shares; employee plans add about 2.4m gross (about 1.4m through the employee share plan, for about EUR 270m of cash, and about 1.0m performance shares); the equity raise adds about 22.9m from late October. End-2026: about 585m; average 566.7m.
  • 2027 to 2028: no buyback; employee plans add 2.4m a year. Averages 586.5m and 588.9m.
  • 2029 to 2030: buybacks of EUR 1.1bn and EUR 1.3bn retire about 3.8m and 4.1m shares. Averages 589.4m and 587.9m. That completes EUR 3.0bn of the EUR 2.5bn to 3.5bn programme.
  • Contrast with history. From 2019 to 2025 the count rose from 551m to 562m (+2%) despite buybacks. From 2025 to 2030 it rises about 4.6%. Schneider remains a net issuer.
  • Dividend. Progressive, at about a 45% to 46% payout of adjusted EPS: EUR 4.75, 5.40, 6.30, 7.10 and 7.90. Consensus is EUR 4.69, 5.30, 6.10 and 6.73. Cash dividends rise from EUR 2.4bn to about EUR 4.2bn by 2030. That is affordable, but it is a larger call on free cash flow during the deleveraging years than the buyback ever was.
  • Free cash flow.
  • Conversion of adjusted net income on the company's definition: 84% (2026), 86% (2027), then 90% to 92%. It is held down by lost factoring (debtor days +4, worth about EUR 0.5bn of working capital), contract assets on long projects, working capital of about 14.8% on organic growth, and integration cash.
  • Measured against reported net income, which carries the non-cash purchase-price amortisation, conversion is about 95% to 100%. That is consistent with the company's "about 100%" target.
  • Free cash flow roughly doubles from 2025 to 2030. Through 2028 it is absorbed by debt reduction and the dividend, not by returns.
  • Versus the last four to six years. From 2019 to 2025, below-the-line items and the share count cost about 2.5 points a year of EPS growth: FactSet adjusted EBIT grew at a 9.8% CAGR against 7.2% for EPS, as interest more than doubled and the share count rose (currency hit both). In 2027 and 2028 they cost about 5 and 3 points. From 2029 they add about 2 points. The quality of EPS growth stays high (organic, not buyback-assisted). Its quantity in 2027 is lower than headline operating momentum suggests, and lower than consensus EPS, which has not fully absorbed the new share count.

What would change the base case materially.

  • PTC closing slipping into 2028. This is EPS-negative in 2027 (shares without the profit), but it lowers peak leverage.

  • The equity raise is done, not pending.

  • Restructuring normalises faster.

  • Support-function leverage is growth-dependent.

I agree with its central reading: a strong, mechanical operating engine with weak capital allocation, where earnings delivery, not the multiple, is the question. For the predictability work, the FY2027 forecast rests on two drivers: data-centre conversion and the below-the-line PTC arithmetic. It is not diffuse.

C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

A761st of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. The scope is large (a further 250 to 350 basis points of group margin by 2030, plus higher cash returns as acquisitions season), it comes from many medium-sized levers rather than one big bet, peer precedents show more than this has been achieved, and it is fully congruent with the stated strategy. The only big single lever, PTC's margin, is mechanical on consolidation; its synergies are the heroic part.

Levers and plausible size (group margin, by 2030):

  • Price, net of cost: about 1 point of revenue a year gross historically, net positive from H2 2026; carry-over supports 2027.
  • Software and services mix: software and services to 25% of revenue, recurring revenue doubled; AVEVA margin from 17% (2024) to 30% (2028); completion of the SaaS transition worth about 50 basis points of group margin (Bernstein).
  • Industrial Automation: from 14% to 18% by 2028 on about 17% of sales, worth about 70 basis points of group margin.
  • PTC: about 40% adjusted EBITA margin; adds 90 to 100 basis points mechanically before integration costs; EUR 250m of cost synergies by year three.
  • Data-centre mix: liquid-cooling components carry 40% to 50% gross margins; digital services up to 10% of data-centre capex; prefabrication capacity utilisation.
  • Portfolio: disposal of EUR 1bn to 1.5bn of dilutive revenue.

Peer precedents: ABB Electrification +990 basis points (2019 to 2024) through decentralisation and selectivity; Eaton Electrical +730 to +800 basis points on volume leverage and pricing; Siemens Energy's Grid margin to 19.9%. Legrand (+70 basis points since 2019) shows that a margin-first, low-growth model plateaus. Schneider's own Energy Management rose 430 basis points 2019 to 2024 and Redburn forecasts Schneider at 24.4% by 2030 against the company's implied about 21% to 22%.

C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

A862nd of 38

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. The core growth engine has not decelerated; it has accelerated sharply over the last twelve months and is running well above the company's normal rate. The input force (data-centre and grid investment) is also still accelerating in orders. The deceleration risk is forward-looking: the guide implies a slower Q4, data-centre order comparisons become very tough through 2027, and the financing of the input force is under strain. Expect the growth rate to fall in 2027 from an exceptional to a still-above-normal level.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Normal rate: the prior-cycle framework was 5% to 8% organic growth; delivered growth was in line with 6% to 8% in 2024 and 8.9% in 2025; the new framework is 7% to 10%.
  • Last twelve months: Q4 2025 10.7%, Q1 2026 11.2%, Q2 16.5%, Q3 16.9% (an average of about 14%), with Energy Management +18.8% and products +17% in Q3.
  • Input force: triple-digit data-centre orders in Q2 and Q3 2026; US hyperscaler capex +45% in 2026; US utility capex at least USD 1.4 trillion over five years; peer orders confirm (ABB Electrification +70% in Q3, Eaton data-centre orders about +85% in Q2, Siemens Smart Infrastructure +42%).
  • Forward deceleration signals: guidance implies about 8% in Q4 at the midpoint; Jefferies expects data-centre growth to normalise to 15% to 20%; ABB's chief financial officer warns against expecting triple-digit data-centre orders every quarter; Bernstein notes 33% comparisons for ABB in Q4.
  • Input-force fragility: the Fitness for Fighting digest says the AI theme "is cracking at its financing, not its demand", with neocloud and hyperscaler spreads wider; Wisdom Library: a capital-intensive theme cracks at its financing before its demand.
C-67

How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

D4421st of 38

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement. Consensus forecasts a step-change from history on every line: revenue growth of 10% to 13% a year against about 6% to 7% reported historically, EPS growth of 15% to 22% against about 7%, and margin expansion of roughly 100 basis points a year against roughly 40 historically. The gap is credible for FY2026 (largely reported) and mostly credible for FY2027 operationally, because the change forces (order surge, pricing turn, self-help) are real and stronger than in the past. But consensus totals mix organic growth with acquisitions, and the granular segment and regional lines show optimism in two places: Energy Management China and East Asia (+22% in FY27) and the margin and gross-margin path in FY28, which coincides with the industry capacity wave.

History against consensus (FactSet; brokers' basis for consensus):

  • Revenue growth (reported): 2019 +5.6%, 2020 −7.4%, 2021 +14.9%, 2022 +18.2%, 2023 +5.1%, 2024 +6.3%, 2025 +5.2% (CAGR 6.7%); consensus +12.8% (2026), +12.0% (2027), +10.1% (2028), +7.4% (2029, seven brokers).
  • Gross margin: 38.6% (2019) to 41.1% (2025), about +40 basis points a year; consensus 42.4% (2026), 43.0% (2027), 44.1% (2028, seven brokers), about +100 basis points a year, partly PTC's software mix.
  • EBITDA: FactSet operating EBITDA CAGR 9.0% (2019 to 2025); consensus growth +15.9%, +19.3%, +13.8%, +9.1%.
  • EBIT margin (brokers' basis): 18.8% (2026), 20.1% (2027), 21.2% (2028), 21.4% (2029).
  • EPS: FactSet adjusted diluted CAGR 7.2% (2019 to 2025); consensus +19.9%, +22.4%, +14.9%, +11.2%.
  • Cash and returns: consensus free cash flow EUR 5.1bn, 6.5bn, 7.7bn, 9.0bn (company FY2025 EUR 4.6bn); capex EUR 1.6bn to 2.2bn; dividend per share +12%, +13%, +15%.

Granular lines (FactSet segment consensus, FY26, FY27, FY28):

  • Energy Management: EUR 37.7bn, 42.2bn, 47.1bn (+13.7%, +12.1%, +11.6%); North America +15% and +12%; Western Europe +8% and +6%; China and East Asia +22% then +10%; South Asia and International +11% and +7%.
  • Industrial Automation: EUR 7.5bn, 8.5bn, 10.2bn (+6.2%, +14.0%, +20.1%), with North America +34% in FY27 and +43% in FY28: this is PTC (US-heavy discrete software) appearing in some models, confirming the blend.

Credibility, given the foundations:

  • FY2026: high (nine months reported, guide raised twice).
  • FY2027: Energy Management +12% reported is credible only with organic growth of about 13% (currency is a small headwind), above my 9% to 11% base; the China line is the most exposed (the Change Forces report flags that Q3 2026 growth of +26.1% may partly reflect unsustainable project pull-forward). EPS EUR 12.61 is "full" (C-49).
  • FY2028: a 21.2% EBIT margin and a 44% gross margin require PTC consolidated, price held, and no margin give-back as competitor capacity arrives; medium credibility.
  • FY2029: thin coverage (six or seven brokers); treat as indicative.

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

B627th of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Against 2019 to 2025, the next five years show three breaks from history:

  • revenue growth about 50% faster;
  • margin expansion about twice as fast in total, though only because of 2026 and PTC; the organic pace from 2027 reverts to history;
  • EPS growth about twice as fast.

The path is front-loaded and decelerating, and below the operating line it is weaker than operations in 2027 and 2028. The net impact on the line items is strongly positive for revenue, adjusted EBITA and free cash flow, and moderately positive for EPS. It carries a visible "air pocket" in FY2027 EPS growth (+14%, against +21% in 2026 and +16% in 2028) that consensus has not priced. The balance sheet worsens sharply for two years and then repairs.

The comparison (company basis for margins; brokers' basis for EPS).

Reasoning: how the items net out.

  • Revenue. The external forces (C-01, C-08) and the reinforcing loops (P-05) give growth well above the 2019 to 2025 trend through 2027. The balancing loops pull it back to the top half of the 7% to 10% framework from 2028. Disposals of EUR 1bn to 1.5bn by 2030 remove about 0.3 points a year. The main departure from consensus is timing, not level: consensus FY2028 revenue (EUR 55.9bn) looks to include PTC for less than a full year or at a lower currency rate, and mine (EUR 56.8bn) includes a full year.
  • Margin. The composition of expansion changes. In 2026 it is support-function leverage plus productivity; in 2027 price carry-over; from 2028 Industrial Automation, AVEVA and PTC mix. Organic expansion from 2028 (+30bp to +40bp) is similar to the historical +45bp to +55bp a year, because price fade and the R&D step-up offset the overhead leverage. The 2030 group margin of about 22.7% includes PTC. Excluding PTC it is about 21.3%, in line with the company's 18.7% + 250bp framework (about 21.2%) and Redburn's 21.7%.
  • EPS. This is where the set's answers bite. Operating profit after restructuring grows about 19% in each of 2027 and 2028. EPS grows 14.4% and 15.9%, because shares rise 4.1% in 2027, interest steps up and tax rises (P-02, P-08). From 2029, deleveraging and buybacks reverse the drag. The 2026 to 2030 EPS CAGR of 13.4% is below the 2025 to 2030 figure (14.8%), because 2026 itself is a +21% year, helped by the removal of the India minority and a late-year raise.
  • Cash and balance sheet. Free cash flow roughly doubles, but through 2028 it is committed to deleveraging and to a dividend that grows from EUR 2.4bn to about EUR 3.2bn. Shareholders receive no buyback until 2029. Against history, this is the first period in which the balance sheet, not the operating cycle, sets the pace of returns.

Against consensus (FactSet median; my figures on the same brokers' basis).

The pattern is clean: at operating level I am at or slightly above consensus; at EPS I am 5% to 6% below for FY2027 to FY2029, and the share count explains most of the gap (see the preliminary notes). The broker range confirms that the PTC and share-count treatment is not uniform. FY2027 EPS runs from EUR 10.65 (Santander) and 11.10 (AlphaValue) to 13.55 to 13.67 (Bernstein, Berenberg and UBS).

Memo topic 7, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

C+529th of 38

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The fourth set asks how much of our assessment is already in guidance, in the sell-side numbers and in the narrative, and the answer splits cleanly by line. On operations our assessment is ahead of all three: guidance is set as a floor and will be raised during 2027 (about 65% probability), consensus revenue and EBITDA sit slightly below our base and at the bottom of the company's own margin range, and the narrative under-weights the restructuring fall, the H1 2027 price carry-over and the dated backlog repricing. On earnings per share our assessment is behind all three: consensus FY2027 EPS of EUR 12.61 is about 7% above our EUR 11.75 because most models predate the 25m-share placement of 21 October and the 4.3% coupon of 28 October and because several consolidate PTC earlier than the company will; the company's "immediately accretive" language has yet to meet a FY2027 in which adjusted EPS grows about five points slower than operating profit; and the narrative treats the dilution as a known fact without having put it in the numbers. The sequence is therefore unfavourable before it is favourable: EPS consensus should drift down 3% to 5% in the December 2026 and February 2027 collections while EBITDA consensus edges up, the dashboard's sell-side momentum letter is at risk of slipping from B, and the first positive EPS revisions arrive with the H1 2027 results in late July 2027. The multiple, half to two-thirds industry-driven and set by the sector's financing fear, will not reward the operating beats until that fear eases or the PTC discount closes, and on our EPS the forward multiple is about 17.7x rather than the 16.3x consensus implies, so the stock is cheap against its range but not at the extreme the consensus multiple suggests. The model's return at the guideline fair multiple of 15.34x (+6.5% to December 2027, +25.6% to June 2029) says the same thing in another form: over 18 months the return is dividends and modest earnings growth per share, and over three years it is earnings. For the memo: the case is a strong, mechanical, largely booked operating story whose per-share consensus is 6% to 7% too high for reasons already on the public record; the gap is favourable on what the company will report and unfavourable on what the market is counting, and the two reconcile in 2027 with the EPS drift coming first (December to February) and the operating upgrades second (February and July). For 18 months to three years out, the gap turns neutral to favourable: the 2030 framework is more likely to be beaten than met, the buyback restarts in 2029 in the base case, and consensus for 2029 will be rebuilt on the right share count by then.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

A702nd of 38

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement. Over the next 18 months the pattern is most likely to repeat the pattern of 2026: FY2026 guidance met at the top of its ranges in February 2027; a prudent initial FY2027 guide set below what the business can do, which the market may read as a disappointment against a consensus that includes acquisitions; a raise at the half-year in July 2027 when the price carry-over peaks; and a second, smaller raise or a reaffirmation at Q3 2027 alongside the PTC closing. I put the probability of at least one raise to FY2027 organic guidance during 2027 at about 65%, of guidance being met without a raise at about 20% and of a reduction at about 15%, with the reduction case driven almost entirely by a financing-led pause in data-centre orders. For the 2030 framework, the +250 basis points of margin and the 12% to 14% data-centre growth rate are more likely to be beaten than met; the 15% to 20% ROCE ambition will be missed in substance once PTC's capital is counted and is likely to be preserved only by definition; and the EUR 2.5bn to 3.5bn buyback by 2030 is the mid-term commitment most at risk, because it depends on deleveraging that a 2027 or 2028 downturn would delay. Against our assessment, guidance is conservative at every horizon on operations and silent on the per-share arithmetic, which makes the gap favourable on the operating side and neutral below the line.

The calendar of guidance events, with probabilities and drivers (my estimates).

Event Date What is guided or likely to be guided Raised Met Reduced What drives each
FY2026 results 18 Feb 2027 Organic growth 12% to 14%; margin 19.7% to 19.9%; organic adjusted EBITA +18% to +21% Above the top: 10% Within range, top half: 75%; bottom half: 10% Below range: 5% The guide implies about 8% organic growth in Q4 at the midpoint against 16.9% in Q3; nine months are at about 14.9%, so the full year is at or above 13% on almost any Q4. Deutsche Bank notes a 2 to 4 point tougher Q4 comparison and "little incentive to push" with bonus targets already exceeded; Microsoft's capex slipped on delayed third-party capacity. Mine is 13.9% and 19.9%; a Q4 of about 10% gives 13.6% and 19.8%

FY2027 results and FY2028 guidance Feb 2028 FY2028 organic guide, first full-year PTC guidance, PPA and integration-cost phasing Not applicable Set at or near the framework: 60% Set below: 25% (if orders paused in 2027) 2028 is the normalisation year; the guide will be prudent

Why the met-at-the-top and raise-later pattern is the base case.

  • The record. Initial annual guidance has been a floor in each of the last three years: 2024 tracked 6% to 8%; 2025 delivered 8.9% in a 7% to 10% range (margin at the bottom, on currency); 2026 was set at 7% to 10% and raised twice to 12% to 14%, with the margin guide raised from +50 to 80 to +100 to 120 basis points. Peers behave the same way (ABB's backlog phasing implied 17% H2 growth against low-double-digit guidance; Siemens guided Smart Infrastructure 6% to 9% for FY2025 and delivered strong double digits), which Deutsche Bank and DNB Carnegie describe as a sector habit.
  • The run-rate. Redburn's H2 2026 margin of 20.2% annualises slightly below the FY2027 consensus margin of 20.3%, so the FY2027 consensus needs further leverage; mine (20.5% including PTC mix, about 20.4% standalone) needs about +40 basis points of underlying progress from the H2 2026 exit, which the price carry-over alone supplies in H1 2027.
  • The visible backlog. A record backlog at end-Q3 2026, two-to-three-year hyperscaler commitments and the company's own "visibility into 2028" make an organic FY2027 above the 7% to 10% framework the central case; the guide will nevertheless be set inside or just above it.

What would produce a reduction, and how early it would show. A reduction inside 18 months needs orders to fall during H1 2027. The triggers are external: neocloud credit stress spreading to colocation financing (CoreWeave's 2032 bonds near 15%, data-centre asset-backed spreads at multi-year highs, lenders moving to fully amortising structures); hyperscaler capex guidance cut in January and February 2027 (the aggregate hyperscaler free cash flow has fallen to about USD 7bn, so a debt-market closure would bite quickly); permitting freezes spreading beyond New York and Texas; a renewed copper and silver spike with wider US tariffs (the H1 2026 gross headwind was about EUR 430m in one half). The company's own guidance would catch up a quarter or two after peers' order prints, because Schneider does not report orders.

The secondary question: 18 months to three years out.

  • Organic growth 7% to 10% a year to 2030: likely met or beaten through 2027 and 2028 (mine averages about 9.5% over 2026 to 2030, front-loaded); the risk of it being lowered is confined to a financing-led downturn. Raised: 30%; met: 55%; reduced: 15%.
  • +250 basis points of cumulative organic margin 2026 to 2030: 100 to 120 are delivered in year one; mine reaches +260 excluding PTC. The company is more likely to beat than to raise, because the second leg (overhead removal, Industrial Automation, AVEVA) is harder and absolute support costs are still rising; a raise would most plausibly come with FY2027 results if the 2027 margin lands above 20.4%. Raised by end-2028: 35%; met or beaten without a raise: 50%; reduced: 15%.
  • Data centres 12% to 14% CAGR to 2030: 2026 alone is about +25% to +30%; Redburn models a 24% pure data-centre CAGR to 2030. Beaten in substance: 70%.
  • Industrial Automation 18% by 2028 and AVEVA 30% by 2028: the H1 2026 miss, China process weakness and the integration load make these the targets most likely to slip in time rather than be cut; mine has 16.3% in 2028. Met: 30%; missed by one to two years: 55%; formally lowered: 15%.
  • ROCE 15% to 20%: on the company's definition (which excludes the EUR 10.2bn of minority buyouts) PTC's EUR 21bn of capital would take the group to about 11% to 12% in 2028. The company is likely either to exclude PTC in the same way or to restate the target; the substance will be missed.
  • Free cash flow conversion about 100%: met on reported net income (mine 95% to 100%), short of it on adjusted; the factoring unwind is a base effect, contract liabilities exceed contract assets, and committed lines are intact.
  • Buyback EUR 2.5bn to 3.5bn by 2030, net debt below 1.5x within two years of consolidation, A-category rating: mine delivers EUR 3.0bn, 1.4x at end-2029 and the rating intact in the base case; a bear 2027 would leave leverage above 2x at end-2028 and push the restart beyond 2029. The buyback is the commitment with the least slack.
  • PTC "immediately accretive" and EUR 800m of revenue synergies: the first will be qualified to a full-year pro forma basis when FY2027 is reported; the second is more likely to be re-dated or re-based (five years; Bernstein's bottom-up EUR 292m) than delivered.

Peer read-across in the next six weeks. Legrand's Q3 (5 November), Siemens Energy's 2030 targets (11 November; Morgan Stanley expects a 20% to 22% 2030 margin), Siemens (12 November) and PTC's own FY2027 guide (4 November) will set the market's expectation for Schneider's February guidance; the sector's habit of raising mid-term targets within a year of setting them (Legrand's September 2026 upgrade to 6% to 8% growth and 21% to 22% margin) is the precedent for Schneider raising its own framework in 2027 or 2028.

Score reasoning. The gap between our assessment and guidance is favourable on operations (guidance is a floor; the first year of the plan has beaten; the FY2027 initial guide will almost certainly sit below our 10.5% and +75 basis points) and neutral on the per-share line (the company does not guide EPS, so there is nothing to raise or cut, but its accretion language will have to be reconciled). The reduction case is real but external and unlikely inside 18 months.

P-09After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?B627th of 38full answer under 7b Margins/growth vs. past
I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

C-3817th of 38

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement. The two consensus lines will move in opposite directions over the next six months, and the Consumer should expect the operating lines to be upgraded and the per-share lines to be cut. Revenue and EBITDA estimates for FY2027 and FY2028 should rise by 1% to 3% on the February 2027 results and FY2027 guide and again on the H1 2027 results, because consensus sits at the bottom of the FY2026 margin guide and slightly below our operating base. FY2027 and FY2028 EPS estimates should fall by 3% to 5% between now and mid-2027 as the brokers whose models predate 21 October 2026 adopt the 25m-share count, the 4.3% coupon on the first EUR 6bn, the cost of the remaining EUR 10bn and a fourth-quarter PTC consolidation; the H1 2027 beat will then claw part of that back. The net result by the end of 2027 is FY2027 EPS consensus of about EUR 12.0 to 12.2 against EUR 12.61 today and our EUR 11.75, and FY2028 of about EUR 13.8 to 14.0 against EUR 14.49 and our EUR 13.5. The breadth of EPS revisions, which has been positive on operations since July 2026, turns mixed in the December 2026 and February 2027 consensus collections and positive again after July 2027. For 18 months to three years out, FY2029 consensus (EUR 16.11, six brokers) is more likely to be cut for share count and interest than raised, by about 4% to 6%, unless the data-centre growth rate is held at Redburn's 24% rather than the company's 12% to 14%. Against our assessment, the gap is unfavourable on EPS (consensus too high for knowable reasons) and favourable on the operating lines (consensus slightly too low), which nets to a modestly unfavourable setup for the headline revision series over the next two quarters.

Where consensus stands, and why it is too high on EPS.

  • The arithmetic. FactSet median EPS EUR 12.61 (FY2027) on net income of EUR 6,995m implies about 555m shares; the broker-sheet mean of EUR 12.44 on EUR 7,218m implies about 580m; the post-placement count is about 589m for FY2027. Our net income (about EUR 6,935m) is within 1% of consensus; our EPS is 6.7% below it. The share count explains about two-thirds of the gap and interest the rest.
  • Who has updated. Of the 21 FY2027 EPS estimates FactSet holds, almost all are dated 16 to 20 October 2026, before the placement was sized (21 October) and before the bond was priced (28 October). Bernstein (21 October, EUR 13.39) carries a flat basic share count of 577.1m for 2026 and 2027 and net interest of EUR 466m in 2027 and EUR 343m in 2028, about EUR 1bn below our financing line in 2028. Redburn (6 October) models EUR 16.5bn of debt at 3.5% from a 1 July 2027 close. Deutsche Bank (19 October, EUR 12.38) is the one model built on a Q4 2027 consolidation with the dilution in, and its FY2027 estimate is the closest to ours among the larger houses. Jefferies (26 October) raised FY2027 and FY2028 by 3% to 4% on operations after including the raise. Deutsche Bank itself notes consensus "updates are highly staggered" and that the aggregate "remains overstated"; the next institutional consensus collection in December 2026 is when the lag clears.
  • The pattern. This is the FY2025 pattern (consensus EUR 9.77 at the peak, outcome EUR 8.59, organic delivery beating throughout) and the FY2026 pattern (EUR 11.10 falling to EUR 9.75 before recovering to EUR 10.33). Below-the-line items have taken EPS consensus down twice in two years while operations beat; the third time is now set up.

The sequence of revisions I expect, event by event.

  1. December 2026 collection, no company news: FY2027 EPS −2% to −3% (share count and coupon), EBITDA unchanged; net breadth of EPS revisions negative for the first time since early 2026; the dashboard's sell-side momentum letter (B today, with EPS +2% over one month and +7% over three) is at risk of slipping to C.
  2. 18 February 2027, FY2026 results and FY2027 guide: FY2026 EPS about in line (consensus EUR 10.30 against ours 10.36); FY2027 EBITDA +1% to +3% (margin at the top of the FY2026 guide, a backlog above expectations, an organic guide at or above the framework); FY2027 EPS roughly flat net (operations up, the remaining debt cost and a reiterated Q4 consolidation down); FY2028 EPS −1% to −3% as the interest line is rebuilt. Breadth mixed: most houses up on EBITDA, split on EPS.
  3. Late April 2027, Q1 revenue: small upgrades to sales if organic growth exceeds 10%; EPS unchanged.
  4. Late July 2027, H1 results: the largest positive revision of the period: FY2027 EBITDA +2% to +4% on an H1 margin near 20.1% and a guidance raise; FY2027 EPS +2% to +3%; FY2028 EPS +1% to +2%. Breadth clearly positive.
  5. October 2027, Q3 and PTC closing: FY2028 EPS rebased on the audited PTC opening balance sheet, the final interest line and the integration-cost phasing; direction uncertain, most likely −1% to −2% as EUR 250m of implementation costs and the full interest charge are modelled explicitly (the Altair precedent is that consensus mis-modelled EUR 150m of integration costs at Siemens).

Where consensus is too low on operations, and the size of the upgrade. FY2026 EBITDA consensus (brokers' basis) EUR 9,717m against our EUR 9,872m (+1.6%); FY2027 EUR 11,597m against 11,647m (+0.4%); FY2028 EUR 13,202m against 13,710m (+3.8%, mainly a full year of PTC that some models do not yet carry); revenue +0.1%, +0.6%, +1.7%. The consensus FY2026 EBIT margin of 18.8% is the bottom of the guided range once amortisation is added back. The operating upgrade potential is therefore modest for 2027 (1% to 3%) and larger for 2028 (3% to 5%), with the latter partly a scope reclassification rather than a change of view.

Where the Consumer should be careful about reading the revision series. The dashboard's figures are net percentage changes in consensus, not counts of brokers; a 2% fall in FY2027 EPS caused by five houses adopting the share count would read as negative momentum even if fifteen houses raised EBITDA. The memo should separate the EBITDA revision series (which should stay positive) from the EPS series (which should dip and recover), and should say so before the December collection does it for the market.

18 months to three years out.

  • FY2029 EPS consensus EUR 16.11 (six brokers) against our EUR 15.19. The gap is again shares and interest; cut by 4% to 6% as coverage of the year deepens and the share count propagates: 60%. Raised: 25%, if the February 2027 backlog and FY2027 guide confirm Redburn's and Morgan Stanley's growth path (Redburn's standalone EPS of EUR 13.86 for 2027 and EUR 20.63 for 2030 is the top of the range) or if the 2030 margin framework is raised. Held: 15%.
  • Revenue and EBITDA for 2028 and 2029: more likely to be raised than cut (full-year PTC, data centres above the 12% to 14% framework), but with thin coverage (seven brokers for revenue, none for EBITDA in FY2029) the series will be noisy; treat FY2029 as indicative.
  • The structural question for 2028 to 2029 consensus is whether brokers hold price. Consensus gross margin of 44.1% in FY2028 requires PTC consolidated, product price held through the capacity wave and systems margins rising on backlog repricing; the distributor survey (price increases less widespread), Eaton's and ABB's capacity completions and expert expectations of "increased pricing competition after 2028" argue for a modest fade that consensus does not carry. This is the cut that would come from operations rather than financing, and it belongs in 2028 estimates, not 2027.

Score reasoning. The question asks how favourable the gap is between our assessment and what the sell-side reflects. On EPS consensus is 6% to 7% above us for reasons that are already in the public record and will be absorbed within two consensus collections, which is unfavourable: the headline revision series will weaken before operations can lift it. On revenue and EBITDA consensus is slightly below us, which is favourable but small in 2027. The breadth of revisions will turn mixed, then recover. A modestly unfavourable net.

11d

Current multiple
