D-0744
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

D4422nd of 38

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. About half to two-thirds of Schneider's daily price variance is explained by its sector basket, and the share that is explained has been rising for two years until the PTC announcement broke the pattern in October. On the latest six-month window (6 May to 28 October 2026) the correlation with the equal-weighted basket of 16 dashboard peers is 0.71, beta 1.05 and R-squared 51%; across the six windows ending April to September 2026 the R-squared was 66% to 69%, the highest in the 24-month series, and the beta 1.07 to 1.14. The fall to 51% in the October window is the PTC shock (about −10% on announcement, about −16% in the weeks after) adding company-specific variance; before it, Schneider was trading as the sector's high-beta expression of the electrification theme. The multiple is therefore mainly industry-driven: its level has been set by the sector's de-rating on AI-financing fears, rates and risk-off rotation (every one of the 16 stocks in the sector carries technical momentum D or F), and the company-specific overlay is a discount for capital allocation that opened in October. For predictability this cuts both ways: delivery against the forecast will not by itself move the multiple while the sector trades on financing fears, but the company-specific discount is the part that company delivery can close.

The evidence, in order of weight.

  • The co-movement series. Correlation with the basket 0.66 to 0.83 over 24 month-end windows, R-squared 43% to 69%, beta 0.95 to 1.18. The pattern has three phases: late 2024 to early 2025 (R-squared 43% to 56%, a mixed market); the twelve months to September 2026 (R-squared rising to 61% to 69% from March 2026, when the AI-infrastructure trade became the sector's single factor); and October 2026 (R-squared 51%, idiosyncratic share 49%), when PTC gave Schneider its own story.
  • Which peers it moves with. ABB is closest (correlation 0.75, beta 0.98, R-squared 56%), then Siemens Energy, Nexans and Prysmian (0.68 each), Legrand and Siemens (0.65), NKT (0.55). The grid and data-centre equipment names explain Schneider; the small electric-vehicle charging and metering names (Alfen, Fastned, Kempower, Landis+Gyr, Arteche) do not (correlations 0.22 to 0.38). Schneider's factor is "AI-and-grid electrification large caps", not the sector as the dashboard defines it.
  • Returns. Six months: Schneider −8.4% against the basket −11.3%; so even with the PTC fall, Schneider has not underperformed the basket over six months; it has outperformed it. Twelve months: Schneider −8.2% against ABB +17.6%, Siemens Energy +14.8%, Prysmian +11.0%, Siemens +7.7%, Legrand −15.4%. The twelve-month underperformance against ABB and Siemens Energy is the company-specific part: a margin and capital-allocation discount that predates PTC (the shares returned 4.3% in the twelve months to March 2026 against ABB's 35.1%, the first set noted).
  • The multiple. On FactSet's 24-month-forward P/E Schneider is at 16.33x, the 11th percentile of five years and the lowest point of two years; on my EPS the forward multiple is about 17.7x, the 25th percentile. The dashboard's current P/E of 21.3x at the 77th percentile of ten years is a trailing reading that the earnings inflection makes stale. Against peers on the dashboard's current P/E, Schneider (21.3x, 77th percentile) sits with ABB (19.7x, 73rd) and Siemens (19.1x, 98th), above Legrand (17.1x, 8th) and Siemens Energy (14.4x, 32nd). The de-rating is sector-wide: Legrand at the 8th percentile on the 800-volt worry, Siemens Energy at the 32nd, Prysmian at the 41st. Morgan Stanley (16 October 2026) argues that exposed electrical stocks now price a slowdown in data-centre spending, some "at zero revenue growth in 2028", and models a 37% downside for ABB in a severe data-centre decline. The sector's multiple is pricing the financing fear, and Schneider's sits inside it with an extra discount.
  • The company-specific overlay since October. Three sources: the PTC price and leverage (Deutsche Bank: "polluted" a clean organic story; Bernstein cut its target to EUR 340 on a higher cost of capital); the equity supply (removed on 21 October; the stock has a 90-day lock-up on further equity-linked issuance); and a software weighting that imports the "SaaS-pocalypse" discount (software multiples fell about 20% in early 2026 on AI-disruption fears; Dassault reinstated at Underperform by Jefferies; PTC itself bought during that de-rating). Buy ratings fell seven points in a month to 65% while estimates rose; the mean target of EUR 328.68 implies 42% upside, so the sell-side is not the source of the discount.

Implications for the forecast and the multiple.

  • Over the next six months, the share price will track hyperscaler capex guidance, neocloud credit, ABB's and Vertiv's order prints and rates more closely than Schneider's own February results, unless those results change the PTC story (the first consolidated plan, the debt coupons, the FY2027 guide).
  • The industry-driven half is the part the Consumer cannot forecast from the company's fundamentals; the idiosyncratic half (now about 49%) is the part that delivery can address. The precedent that applies is Legrand's: it de-rated to the 8th percentile on a technology narrative despite rising estimates, and the de-rating persisted until the company reframed the exposure (its September 2026 Capital Markets Day). Schneider's equivalent reframing is the February 2027 FY2027 guidance and the PTC closing plan.
  • A fair-multiple discussion for the memo (topic #11) should therefore start from the sector's range, not Schneider's own history alone: ABB re-rated to 28 to 33 times forward earnings in its peak delivery phase; the sector's electrical leaders currently trade at 14x to 20x on the dashboard's measure; Schneider at 17x to 18x forward on our numbers is in the middle of the sector and the lower quartile of its own range.

Score reasoning. A multiple that is half to two-thirds industry-driven, in a sector whose multiple is being set by an external financing fear, makes the return less predictable than the earnings. The idiosyncratic share has risen for a reason the company can address (PTC), and Schneider has outperformed its basket over six months, which argues that the company-specific discount is already largely in the price. The score is below 50 because the dominant driver of the multiple over 18 months sits outside the company.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

B+606th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share-price, financial and operational outperformance and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement. In this sector, outperformance has come from three things combined: exposure to a secular electrical demand driver, visible self-help margin expansion, and disciplined capital allocation that compounds return on capital. Underperformance has come from expensive, poorly integrated acquisitions, from a margin gap to peers, and from de-ratings when a demand pulse peaks or a technology threat is priced. Schneider currently fits the first two outperformance patterns strongly and fits the main underperformance pattern (expensive acquisitions) at the same time. The share price is following the underperformance pattern while the earnings follow the outperformance pattern.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Outperformance precedents: ABB lifted Electrification margins from 13.4% (2019) to about 25% through decentralisation and project selectivity, with ROCE near 30%, and re-rated to 28 to 33 times forward earnings; Eaton re-rated from a "capacity expansion story to a powerful earnings revision story" on data-centre exposure and margins exiting about 32% in Electrical Americas; Siemens Energy rose 47% in twelve months as margins turned. Since early 2026 high-exposure electrical suppliers outperformed standard machinery by about 30 percentage points, half from upgrades and half from multiple (Bernstein).
  • Underperformance precedents: Schneider itself: Jefferies warned in 2020 of "value-eroding acquisition strategies"; Energy Management margin rose only 430 basis points 2019 to 2024 against ABB's 990 and Eaton's 730; ROCE of 14.8% in 2024 lagged ABB's 22.9% and Siemens' 19.1%; the shares returned only 4.3% in the twelve months to March 2026 against ABB's 35.1%. Legrand de-rated to 15.6 times 2027 EV/EBITA on the 800V DC worry despite rising estimates. Siemens Energy lost EUR 7bn of value in one day in June 2023 on quality defects.
  • Rules of thumb in the Wisdom Library and digests that apply: "Sector tailwind plus a self-improving company" (Schneider fits); "a price plateau at a high level is two to three more years of rising margin" (fits through 2028); "pay for conversion once the orders are in; the first half of a one-to-two-year holding is the cleaner half" (fits for 2027); "a technology threat priced years before it can bite is a terminal-value question" (800V DC); "a capital-intensive theme cracks at its financing before its demand" (the current market fear); "linked businesses are not a conglomerate" and the cockroach pattern at M&A integration (the PTC test); "in a bear market the cleanest case in the sector wins" (Schneider's case is not currently the cleanest: Fitness for Fighting's Fragility row is Double Underweight).
  • Fit: strong on demand and self-help; weak on capital allocation and case simplicity. The pattern implies that the re-rating requires either integration proof or a return to buybacks, not just earnings beats.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

C+5512th of 38

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. On a multiple-only basis, about 20% to 25% (to roughly EUR 175 to 180); with a moderate earnings cut as well, about 30% (to roughly EUR 160). A severe data-centre pause would take the trough to about EUR 145. Importantly, the shares already trade near the stressed multiples that bears and peer bear cases use, so most of the remaining downside requires earnings cuts, not just de-rating; the reward to a return to the median multiple is about +25%.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • Today: EUR 231.40; 24-month-forward P/E 16.33x on implied 24-month-forward EPS of about EUR 14.2; 18.4 times FY27 consensus EPS (EUR 12.61) and 16.0 times FY28 (EUR 14.49); EV of EUR 154.3bn, about 15 times FY27 consensus EBIT before PTC; 52-week range EUR 217 to 312.
  • History: five-year 24-month-forward P/E range 13.79x to 24.99x, median 20.41x; ten-year bands used by the Selector: 24.6x "mania", 21.0x "defendable", 17.6x "prudent" (median), 15.9x "conservative", 12.6x "crash".
  • Bear references: Morgan Stanley's bear value of EUR 220 to 230 (growth 6% in 2027, flat margins), already roughly reached; peer bear cases at about 15 times EV/EBITA in a severe downturn (Legrand), which Schneider already trades near on FY27 consensus.
  • Scenarios (my arithmetic): crash multiple 12.6x on unchanged 24-month EPS (14.2): about EUR 179 (−23%); crash multiple with EPS 10% lower: about EUR 161 (−30%); conservative 15.9x with EPS 10% lower: about EUR 203 (−12%); severe (EPS 20% lower, 12.6x): about EUR 143 (−38%).
  • Upside reference: five-year median 20.4x on EUR 14.2: about EUR 289 (+25%); consensus mean target EUR 328.68 (+42%).

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

B556th of 38

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative today is "great operator, bad deal": the market credits the data-centre and margin story but has de-rated the shares on PTC, financing, AI-capex fears and the macro regime. The staircase ahead is well-founded and dated, but its near-term steps are mostly the removal of negatives (completing the equity raise, closing the deals, proving the FY26 margin and setting FY27 guidance on 18 February 2027) rather than new positives; the novel positives (PTC synergies, Industrial Automation and AVEVA margin targets, the buyback restart, the 800V DC content gain) sit in months 6 to 36. That shape argues that the narrative can improve on delivery rather than on surprise, and that the first catalyst capable of shifting it is the February 2027 results.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–76

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The two sets are highly congruent on what drives operating profit and on where the risk sits, and the places where they jar are the places where the second set corrected the first, in each case in the direction the new evidence now confirms. There are two residual tensions worth the Consumer's attention: the first set's reading that the 2030 margin framework is conservative and likely to be raised against the second set's reading that 2026 was exceptional and the organic pace reverts to history from 2027; and the first set's treatment of the 24-month-forward multiple as sitting at a five-year low, which holds on consensus earnings but not on ours. Neither tension undermines the 18-month forecast; both matter for how the memo describes the 18-month-to-three-year case. The congruence is strongest exactly where the forecast is most reliable (booked data-centre conversion, pricing carry-over, overhead leverage) and weakest where it is least reliable (software integration, capital allocation), which is itself a sign that the analysis is sound rather than forced.

Where the sets align, and the new evidence confirms them.

  • The operating engine. Both sets describe a mechanical, order-led acceleration: data centres about 30% of sales with triple-digit orders converting over 12 to 24 months, short-cycle products recovering, pricing turning real in H2 2026, overhead outgrown by revenue. The Q3 2026 regional detail (North America +19.9% organic, China and East Asia +26.1%, Europe +8.6%, South Asia and International +13.2%; "another record backlog at quarter-end") and the peer prints since (ABB Electrification orders +70%, backlog USD 16bn, book-to-bill 1.24 to 1.41; Vertiv sales +61% organic with orders above 100%; Eaton with 18 of 24 capacity projects complete) support the picture without qualification.
  • Where the risk sits. Both sets put the forecast risk below the operating line (shares, interest, PTC timing) and in 2028 rather than 2027 (data-centre normalisation, the capacity wave, full PTC consolidation). The first set wrote "beat on operations, miss on EPS" as the lesson of FY2025; the second set made it the FY2027 base case; the new evidence (25m shares, 4.3% coupon, Deutsche Bank consolidating PTC only from Q4 2027, Bernstein modelling a flat 577m share count) shows consensus has not yet absorbed it.
  • Capital allocation as the weak strand. The first set's scores of 30 to 38 on capital allocation, buybacks and leverage, and the second set's 40 to 42 on financial leverage and below-the-line items, say the same thing in two languages. The PTC deep dives (ROCE 3.6% to 4% in year one, above the cost of capital only in year five or six; revenue synergies of EUR 800m "demanding", Bernstein's bottom-up cross-sell of EUR 292m; Deutsche Bank excluding revenue synergies altogether) are consistent with both.
  • Cash conversion. The first set's "about 100% through the cycle, with identifiable 2026 to 2027 risks" and the second set's 84% to 92% of adjusted net income (95% to 100% of reported) are the same judgement on two bases. The factoring report resolves the one open question in the first set's favour: factored receivables were a stable EUR 343m to 345m at end-2024 and end-2025, the H1 2026 unwind was a treasury decision with EUR 3,525m of committed lines unused and no covenants, and contract liabilities (EUR 3,542m) still exceed contract assets (EUR 2,740m). The working-capital drag is a base effect, not a structural loss.

Where the second set corrected the first, and the evidence sides with the second set.

  • Equity raise pending (first set) against done (second set): done, and larger (note 1).
  • PTC "low-single-digit accretion at best" in 2027 (first set) against a 2.5% to 3% dilution year (second set): the second set is right; the one-quarter consolidation against a full year of new shares is now in Deutsche Bank's and Bernstein's models, and the financing precedents (Eaton and Cooper 2012, Siemens and Altair 2025 with a 2% first-year EPS drag and 250 basis points of Digital Industries margin dilution) all show front-loaded dilution and back-loaded synergy.
  • Restructuring of EUR 300m to 450m a year (first set) against EUR 250m falling to 175m (second set): the company's framework (EUR 500m incremental over 2025 to 2027 above a EUR 100m to 150m norm) and Redburn's EUR 800m to 950m over three years, of which EUR 737m is already spent in 2025 and 2026, support the second set.
  • "Self-help cost discipline" (first set) against "a freeze on a base that grew 24%" (second set): the absolute cost series (note 5) settles it for the second set.

The two residual tensions, and how I resolve them.

  • Is the 2030 framework conservative? The first set (C-13, C-71) argued that 100 to 120 basis points delivered in year one leaves only 35 basis points a year to reach +250 and makes an upgrade likely. The second set argued the 2026 gain was growth-dependent leverage that decays with growth. I side with the second set for 2027 to 2028: Redburn expects the initial FY2027 margin guide to be about +50 basis points; Morgan Stanley's view that the first phase was "headcount-driven" and the next requires genuine operating efficiency is the company's own framing of a harder second leg; and absolute support costs are still rising. An upgrade of the 2030 ambition is possible after FY2027 if the data-centre cycle holds, but it is not a base-case input. The first set's score of 80 on C-13 reads about ten points too high on this evidence.
  • Is the multiple at a five-year low? The first set's C-83 and C-07 used the FactSet 16.33x. On our EPS the forward multiple is about 17.7x (note 8). The stock is cheap against its own range, but not at the extreme the first set described; this moves the trough estimate in C-83 down by about 5% (a multiple-only trough near EUR 170 rather than EUR 175 to 180) and reduces the "reward to a return to the median multiple" from +25% to about +15%.

Minor inconsistencies to be aware of. Product pricing in Q3 2026 is 5.1% (Deutsche Bank) or 5.5% (Morgan Stanley); data-centre growth to 2030 is 12% to 14% (company), about 15% (second set) or 24% (Redburn's pure data-centre CAGR); 2027 leverage is 1.8x (Redburn, cash-only definition), 2.3x (second set, Deutsche Bank) or 2.5x (Deutsche Bank pro forma); Jefferies' EUR 10.19 FY2027 EPS in one report is on a different basis from its EUR 11.80 in FactSet. None of these changes a conclusion, but the Consumer should quote the basis with each figure.

For the predictability question. Congruence on the operating lines, with the residual tensions sitting in the 18-month-to-three-year window and in the multiple rather than in the FY2027 forecast, is positive for predictability over 18 months.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

C4814th of 38

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. The sell-side narrative on Schneider is complete on the big external stories and incomplete on the arithmetic that will decide FY2027 earnings per share. Everything that is sector-wide and dramatic is well known and prominently discussed: the financing of the AI build-out and neocloud credit, the pricing fade as competitor capacity lands from 2028, the buyback pause, the doubted EUR 800m of PTC revenue synergies, the "great operator, bad deal" verdict. The dynamics that are company-specific and mechanical are known to one or two houses and absent from the consensus numbers: that FY2027 carries a full year of 25m new shares against one quarter of PTC profit; that the first EUR 6bn of debt cost 4.3% against the 3.5% in the models and that the next EUR 10bn may cost more in dollars; that the support-function margin gain is volume leverage on a still-rising cost base and therefore reverses with growth; that restructuring falling from EUR 450m to 250m adds about 2.5 points to 2027 EPS growth; and that the hyperscalers' own free cash flow has fallen to a few billion dollars, which moves the financing risk from the marginal customers to the core ones. Investor commentary beyond the sell-side is not in the evidence base, and the narrative report credited only five houses (Deutsche Bank, Bernstein, Jefferies, Rothschild and Co Redburn, Morgan Stanley) out of the roughly twenty that FactSet lists; the Consumer should read the "well known" judgements below as "well known among the leading European capital-goods houses" and should assume the wider holder base knows less. The under-articulated dynamics are, on balance, negative for FY2027 EPS and positive for FY2027 operations, so the gap between our assessment and the narrative is modestly unfavourable for the next 18 months and roughly neutral for 18 months to three years out.

Chinese automation price increases Deutsche Bank China automation tracker (21 October 2026: Inovance's fourth increase of 2026, 8% to 35% on HMIs; Xinje 8% to 20%); Bernstein (10 August: Inovance share at record highs); CCB International Discussed in the China automation coverage, not linked to Schneider's Industrial Automation by the Schneider analysts Known in another coverage universe; not in Schneider's narrative
Buyback pause 2027 and 2028, restart 2029 Company prospectus (22 October); Bernstein (21 October: present value of the pause about −EUR 500m, a 6% cut in cash returns); Redburn (leverage to 1.3x by 2029); Morgan Stanley (27 October 2025: EUR 4bn of excess capital possible by 2027) Universally known; the view that it is a reallocation rather than a loss is Bernstein's Fully known
PTC revenue synergies of EUR 800m Deutsche Bank (19 October: "unrealistic", excluded); Redburn (6 October: "demanding", 33% of PTC sales); Bernstein (21 October: EUR 292m of conventional cross-sell; break-even at a 40% haircut); Jefferies (5 October: significant execution risk) The most discussed company-specific item since 5 October; universally doubted Fully known, and discounted
Restructuring falling from EUR 450m to EUR 250m No house frames it as a 2027 EPS tailwind; Redburn and Deutsche Bank record the EUR 500m incremental programme Absent Not known

The peers' narratives, as the frame investors apply. ABB is the disciplined allocator (net cash, a USD 1.5bn to 2bn buyback, Rotork funded from Robotics proceeds, ROCE near 30%) and is the yardstick against which Schneider's PTC and India deals are judged. Eaton is the capacity-expansion and data-centre story whose margin dip and recovery during its ramp is the template for Schneider's prefabrication risk. Legrand is the stock de-rated on the 800-volt worry despite rising estimates, and the precedent that a technology narrative can hold a multiple down for a year. Siemens is software priced as an AI victim, the frame that PTC imports into Schneider's multiple; Dassault's 5% growth and Jefferies' Underperform are the same frame. Vertiv is the pure play whose 61% growth and 25.2% margin set the bar for data-centre execution. The sector's investors are focused on three things: whether AI capex is financed, whether electrical pricing fades in 2028, and who allocates capital well. They are not focused on the per-share arithmetic of a mid-year acquisition.

What is missing from the narrative, and in which direction it will surprise.

  • Negative for FY2027 EPS: the full-year share count against a quarter of PTC (Deutsche Bank only); the 4.3% coupon and the dollar mix (Jefferies only); the growth-dependence of the support-function ratio (Morgan Stanley's point, modelled by none); the hyperscalers' own cash-flow squeeze (Jefferies only). These will surface between the December 2026 consensus collection and the February 2027 results.
  • Positive for FY2027 operations: restructuring falling by EUR 200m (nobody); the front-loading of price carry-over into H1 2027 (Deutsche Bank and Redburn note net price positive, nobody dates the peak); the backlog repricing to 2028 (framed as resilience, not as a dated tailwind); the end of the AVEVA transition drag in 2026 (Bernstein). These will surface at the H1 2027 results.
  • Neutral to positive for 18 months to three years: the 2029 buyback restart and deleveraging to 1.4x are known; the possibility of a raised 2030 framework is conjecture in two houses; the PPA size and the reported-to-adjusted gap are unknown and will widen the statutory P/E, which some holders use.

Investor commentary. The evidence base holds no Twitter, forum or buy-side letter material; the first set's indicators are that 55% of surveyed European long-only funds were overweight before PTC, that short interest is near zero, that the shares fell 10% to 16% on the deal and that 44.6% of the capital was covered by roadshows. The absence of investor-level evidence is a gap for the memo's topic #8, and I request it below.

Score reasoning. The question asks how favourable the gap is between our assessment and what the narrative reflects. The narrative is right on the sector risks and on the deal's quality, and it is ahead of the consensus numbers on dilution; it is behind on the 2027 EPS mechanics in a way that will produce negative headlines before positive ones; and it under-weights two positives that arrive later. Modestly unfavourable for the 18-month window, neutral beyond.

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 25 October 2026, 19:52 to 20:17 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Set 1 answers: the foundational components of the investment case

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Schneider Electric SE (SU-FR), written 2026-10-25, price EUR 231.40 (23-Oct-26)

Preliminary notes for the Consumer: data traps to clear before modelling

These notes are not answers; they are the reading rules I applied, recorded so that the second-set model is built on the same footing.

  1. The latest Earnings Review is current. File 09 covers the H1 2026 results for the six months to 30 June 2026, reported 30 July 2026, which matches the last period in the FactSet block (06/2026). It does not contain the Q3 2026 revenue release of 16 October 2026. The Q3 figures I use (revenue EUR 11,460m, +16.9% organic, +17.9% reported; FY26 guidance raised to 12% to 14% organic growth and 100 to 120 basis points of organic adjusted EBITA margin expansion, implying a 19.7% to 19.9% margin) come from the Change Forces report of 25 October 2026. File 10 (labelled the Q4 2025 review) is a hybrid that also cites Q1 2026, PTC and Q3 events; I treat its non-FY2025 statements as secondary.
  2. Margin bases. The company's headline measure is adjusted EBITA: before amortisation of purchased intangibles (EUR 206m in H1 2026, so roughly EUR 0.4bn a year), before restructuring (about EUR 450m guided for 2026) and before other operating items. FactSet history is "EBIT (adjusted)" and "EBITDA (operating)"; consensus EBIT is the brokers' basis. Consensus FY26 EBIT of EUR 8,516m on revenue of EUR 45,304m is 18.8%; adding back about EUR 0.4bn of amortisation gives about EUR 8.9bn, or about 19.7% adjusted EBITA. Consensus therefore sits at the bottom of the guided 19.7% to 19.9% range. Model adjusted EBITA, amortisation and restructuring as three separate lines.
  3. FactSet's historical "free cash flow" is after dividends. It reconciles exactly as operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex less dividends paid (FY2025: 6,131 − 1,072 − 2,191 = 2,868; FY2024: 5,580 − 950 − 1,963 = 2,667). The 0.6x to 0.8x "free cash flow / net income" in the peer table is therefore an artefact of that definition. On a pre-dividend basis (operating cash flow less all capex including capitalised development) FY2025 conversion was about 110% of reported net income and about 95% of adjusted net income, consistent with the company's own FY2025 free cash flow of EUR 4,635m and 106% conversion. The consensus free cash flow line (EUR 5,076m for FY26) is pre-dividend; never compare it with the history line.
  4. Consensus is a blend of pre-PTC and post-PTC models. The PTC deal was announced on 5 October 2026 and most estimates are dated 16 to 22 October. Consensus FY27 total debt rises by EUR 10.7bn to EUR 25.6bn, while the deal needs EUR 16bn to 17bn of new debt; FY27 consensus EBITDA growth of +19.3% and revenue growth of +12.0% look partly inclusive of PTC; consensus net income differs between the block's sections 3 and 4 (FY26 EUR 5,565m against a broker median of EUR 5,923m), so implied share counts cannot be derived. Rebuild FY27 onwards in three layers: organic standalone; acquisitions on their closing dates (Motivair is already consolidated; Cognite, AiDASH, Shelly and PTC are not); and financing (debt cost, equity issued, buyback pause).
  5. PTC figures vary by source. Equity value USD 22.6bn (USD 205 per share, a 42% premium); enterprise value USD 23.7bn, or about EUR 21bn to EUR 21.1bn; funding EUR 16bn to 17bn of debt plus EUR 5bn to 6bn of equity; 2027 net debt to EBITDA of 2.3x (Deutsche Bank), 1.8x (Redburn) or "below 2x" (Jefferies). I use Deutsche Bank's 2.3x as the prudent case.
  6. Two valuation readings conflict. The FactSet 24-month-forward P/E is 16.33x, the 11th percentile of five years and the lowest point of the last two years (two-year median 21.48x). The dashboard's "current P/E" of 21.3x sits at the 77th percentile of ten years and drives the pinned valuation letter D. With consensus EPS growth of +20% in FY26 and +22% in FY27, the forward measure is the relevant one for an 18-month case.
  7. Pinned dashboard letters (ratings snapshot of 24-Oct-26): technical momentum D, sell-side earnings momentum B, valuation D.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Overall steer for the Consumer (second set)

For an 18-month to four-year model, the foundations support the following shape. Revenue: organic growth of about 13% in FY2026 (guide 12% to 14%), slowing to about 9% to 11% in FY2027 and towards the 7% to 10% framework in FY2028 and FY2029, with data centres (about 30% of sales) growing mid-teens in 2027 and grid and non-residential mid-single-digit; a currency translation headwind (US dollar 38% of revenue); acquisitions added as separate layers on their closing dates. Margins: adjusted EBITA about 19.7% to 19.9% in FY2026 (including a non-recurring EUR 100m tariff refund), then +50 to 80 basis points a year organically on price carry-over, support-function savings and productivity, partly offset by R&D rising to 7% of sales; PTC adds about 90 to 100 basis points mechanically on consolidation; fade price contribution from 2028 as competitor capacity arrives. Below the line: restructuring of about EUR 300m to 450m a year; rising purchase-price amortisation; interest up by about EUR 0.5bn to 0.6bn a year with PTC debt; tax about 24%; minorities small; shares up about 4% from the equity raise with no buybacks in 2027 and 2028. Cash: conversion of 90% to 100% of adjusted net income; net debt to EBITDA peaking at about 2.3 times in 2027. Judgement on consensus: FY2026 close to right; FY2027 EPS of EUR 12.61 is full on a standalone basis; FY2028 depends on PTC and on holding price through the capacity wave. Judgement on the case: strong, mechanical operating momentum and a genuine competitive advantage, offset by weak capital allocation and a market regime that is compressing multiples; the share price already sits near stressed multiples, so the forecast question is earnings delivery, and the predictability question is concentrated in data-centre financing in 2027 and software integration.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Schneider Electric SE (SU-FR): global energy-management and industrial-automation group; segments are Energy Management (low- and medium-voltage electrical distribution, secure power and data-centre systems, liquid cooling, building and grid software; about 83% of revenue) and Industrial Automation (discrete, process and hybrid automation hardware plus industrial software including AVEVA, with Cognite and PTC acquisitions pending; about 17% of revenue).

Request: T-P1

Peers: ABB (ABBN-CH), the closest European electrification and automation peer and the reference case for decentralised margin expansion; Eaton (ETN-US), the electrical and data-centre power peer with the highest electrical margins and a large capacity build; Legrand (LR-FR), the low-voltage building and data-centre white-space peer with a long record of holding margins through downturns; Siemens (SIE-DE), the automation, industrial software and Smart Infrastructure peer; Vertiv (VRT-US), the data-centre power and cooling pure play; Rockwell Automation (ROK-US), the industrial automation and software peer; Hager Group and Socomec (private), European low-voltage and power-switching competitors. Inputs: What share of Schneider Electric's cost base is fixed versus variable, given support function costs of EUR 9,375m and cost of sales of EUR 23,257m in FY2025 (2025 Universal Registration Document)? How did Schneider's adjusted EBITA margin move with revenue in 2009, 2015 to 2016, 2020 and the 2023 to 2025 short-cycle destocking, and how did ABB, Eaton and Legrand margins move in the same periods? How much of the H1 2026 margin step (adjusted EBITA margin 19.3%, +120 basis points organic; industrial productivity EUR 535m; support function costs down 1.1 points of sales; a EUR 100m tariff refund; company H1 2026 results, 30 July 2026) was structural efficiency and how much was headcount restraint or one-off, given Morgan Stanley's September 2026 view that it was headcount-driven? Is the Energy Management headcount increase of 24% over three years against 1% to 7% at peers (Rothschild & Co Redburn) reversible, and at what cost? What did Schneider's earlier cost programmes promise and deliver, and over what time? How feasible are the December 2025 Capital Markets Day targets (support-function-to-sales ratio down 1.5 to 2 points by 2030; EUR 2bn to 2.5bn of industrial productivity over 2026 to 2030; +250 basis points of organic adjusted EBITA margin over 2026 to 2030; Industrial Automation margin of 18% by 2028; AVEVA margin of 30% by 2028)? How much will the planned R&D step-up towards 7% of sales, the capitalisation of development costs (EUR 367m capitalised in FY2025; EUR 128m impairment in H1 2026) and restructuring of about EUR 450m guided for 2026 (company, 16 October 2026) offset the savings, and is restructuring recurring? What is the cost of the Multi-Hub 2.0 duplication of research, factories and suppliers against its savings? What do precedents (AVEVA, OSIsoft) say about Schneider's ability to deliver the EUR 250m of PTC cost synergies targeted by year three (company, 5 October 2026)? Test the Bernstein estimate that one point of organic growth moves adjusted EBITA by about EUR 90m. How did peers' margins behave when lead times normalised, and what is the margin risk from competitor capacity additions such as Eaton's investment of about USD 1.5bn across 24 North American facilities? Focus: How much of Schneider's 2026 margin expansion is structural and repeatable, and how much more support-function, productivity, Industrial Automation and AVEVA improvement can realistically reach adjusted EBITA in 2027 to 2030 after the R&D step-up and restructuring costs. How far would margins fall if data-centre growth slowed sharply while competitor capacity arrived?

Request: T-P2

Peers: ABB (ABBN-CH), low- and medium-voltage pricing peer with disclosed pricing contribution; Eaton (ETN-US), North American electrical and data-centre pricing leader; Legrand (LR-FR), low-voltage building products sold through the same distributors; Siemens (SIE-DE), automation list-price setter and Smart Infrastructure competitor; Vertiv (VRT-US), data-centre power and cooling competitor in large tenders; Shenzhen Inovance (300124-CN), the Chinese automation price competitor; Rexel (RXL-FR), the distributor whose disclosures split volume from cable and other price effects; Hager Group (private), European low-voltage competitor. Operating conditions: sharp copper, silver and steel inflation (2021 to 2022 and 2025 to 2026); US tariff shocks (2025 to 2026, including Section 232); demand downturns and distributor destocking (2009, 2020 and the 2023 to 2025 short-cycle slowdown); capacity scarcity with long lead times (2022 to 2026); normalisation of lead times after industry capacity additions (precedents in transformers and cables); large concentrated hyperscaler and colocation tenders; Chinese price competition in automation and standard low-voltage products; large currency moves. Inputs: How has Schneider's product price realisation moved quarter by quarter in 2024 to 2026 (about 2% in Q1 2026, about 4% in Q2 and 5.1% in Q3 per Deutsche Bank, 19 October 2026) against raw-material and tariff inflation, given H1 2026 net price of −EUR 154m (gross pricing +EUR 280m, raw materials −EUR 330m, tariffs −EUR 104m; company, 30 July 2026) and an expected H2 2026 net price-cost tailwind of at least EUR 150m (Deutsche Bank)? How does pricing work on systems and data-centre contracts compared with products, given Bernstein's statement that pricing acts directly on less than half of sales, and what are the indexation clauses, escalation mechanisms and lags in backlog contracts? Did Schneider and its peers hold list prices when copper and silver fell in past cycles, or give price back? What evidence is there of Schneider pricing aggressively to win large data-centre projects (Vertiv national sales specialist, October 2026), and of customers paying premiums for delivery slots through capacity reservation agreements (Compass, USD 3bn over five years; Switch)? How do French distributors' "prix dérogés" project-pricing arrangements work after the French Competition Authority's EUR 207m fine (October 2024), and do distributors accept list increases in a slowdown? What does the turn to positive pricing in China in Q1 2026 (Bernstein) and Inovance's 8% to 35% human-machine-interface price increases (Deutsche Bank, October 2026) say about Chinese price discipline? How do peers' pricing contributions compare (ABB just above 2% in Q3 2026; Legrand about 3% targeted; Siemens Digital Industries list increases of 5% to 20% in late 2026)? How strong are switching costs and specification share (Bernstein survey: more than 20% of power spend for 64% of procurement managers)? What do brokers assume for price contribution in 2027 to 2029? Focus: Whether Schneider's 2026 price acceleration is a real gain above input inflation that it will keep as metal costs and tariffs ease, lead times normalise and competitor capacity arrives, especially in data-centre systems and short-cycle products. How fast price contribution fades, and whether price is given back, in a demand downturn.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Schneider's growth and margins from 2027 depend on several self-reinforcing and self-limiting dynamics (prefabrication and single-sourcing, backlog repricing, installed-base services, competitor capacity additions, and the financing of AI data-centre capital spending) whose balance decides whether margins keep rising after 2027. Peers: Vertiv (VRT-US), integrated data-centre power and cooling competitor running the same prefabrication loop; Eaton (ETN-US), building grid-to-chip capability and large capacity; ABB (ABBN-CH), medium-voltage and data-centre orders peer; Legrand (LR-FR), white-space peer exposed to the same architecture shift; Siemens (SIE-DE), software and automation scale peer; Siemens Energy (ENR-DE), grid equipment peer with a record backlog and capacity build; Delta Electronics (2308-TW), power-electronics competitor in data centres; Rittal (private, Friedhelm Loh Group), enclosure and cooling competitor. Inputs: Possible reinforcing loops: prefabricated modular delivery leading to single-sourcing by hyperscalers, share gains and more prefabricated capacity (Bernstein, July to October 2026; Schneider's prefabricated capacity grown to more than one million square feet on eight sites); a growing installed base feeding services and digital attach (digital services can be about 10% of data-centre capex, company, Q1 2026 call); backlog repricing as contracts signed before 2022 run off; design-in to NVIDIA and AMD reference designs raising specification share; AVEVA's subscription transition and the Cognite and PTC data layer raising switching costs; data-centre urgency supporting pricing. Possible balancing loops: scarcity pricing prompting competitor capacity additions (Eaton, Siemens, Vertiv, Siemens Energy), lead-time normalisation and price fade; data-centre load growth prompting grid-connection queues, local opposition and permitting pauses (Texas, Virginia, Spain); debt-financed hyperscaler and neocloud capital spending prompting wider credit spreads and slower spending; high margins attracting Chinese, Korean and Indian entrants in standard products; acquisition-driven leverage forcing a buyback pause and pressure on the valuation; software talent attrition slowing integration. Focus: Which of these loops are operating now, with dated evidence, and how long the reinforcing data-centre and prefabrication loop can run before the capacity and financing balancing loops dominate. What are the earliest observable signals that the balance is shifting?

Request: T-P4

Why requested: The PTC acquisition, the planned equity issue, the buyback pause, rising leverage and four other pending or recent acquisitions make the bridge from operating profit to earnings per share unusually complex for 2026 to 2029. Peers: ABB (ABBN-CH), large acquisition (Rotork) alongside buybacks and low leverage; Eaton (ETN-US), large acquisitions (Boyd Thermal, Fibrebond) with higher leverage; Siemens (SIE-DE), precedent of large industrial-software acquisitions and their EPS effect; Legrand (LR-FR), serial bolt-on acquirer with disclosed acquisition multiples and returns; Rockwell Automation (ROK-US), automation peer with software acquisitions; Dassault Systemes (DSY-FR), a PLM competitor of PTC for software growth and margin benchmarks. Inputs: PTC: expected closing date and conditions; standalone revenue (about USD 2.7bn in 2026, Deutsche Bank), adjusted EBITA margin (about 40%, Deutsche Bank), expected growth (about 10% a year to 2029, Jefferies) and the reporting segment after consolidation. Financing: EUR 16bn to 17bn of new debt and EUR 5bn to 6bn of equity (company investor presentation, 5 October 2026); instruments, cost of debt, timing and pricing of the equity; net debt to EBITDA in 2027 of 2.3x (Deutsche Bank) against 1.8x (Rothschild & Co Redburn) and the credit-rating commitment. Purchase-price amortisation, integration costs and whether brokers' adjusted EPS excludes them. Synergies: EUR 250m of cost by year three and about EUR 800m of revenue (company); Bernstein's estimate of about EUR 300m of conventional cross-sell; Rothschild & Co Redburn's modelled EPS accretion of 1.9% in 2028 and 5.3% by 2032; Deutsche Bank's estimate of a 4% return on capital employed in year one. Buyback: EUR 600m in 2026, paused in 2027 and 2028, accelerated to complete EUR 2.5bn to 3.5bn by 2030; employee share plans. Interest: FactSet net interest expense of EUR 553m in 2025 and its path. Tax: 24% in H1 2026 and the effect of PTC's US profile. Minorities after the buyout of Temasek's 35% Indian stake (EUR 5.5bn, late 2025) and the 2028 purchase of the remaining 25% of Motivair. Timing and contribution of Cognite (USD 3.1bn), AiDASH (USD 350m enterprise value) and Shelly (about EUR 1.2bn). Currency: a 10% US-dollar move changes revenue by EUR 1,505m and adjusted EBITA by EUR 427m (2025 annual report). Disposals of EUR 1bn to 1.5bn of revenue by 2030. How brokers' FY2027 EPS estimates differ in their treatment of PTC (FactSet broker detail, October 2026). Focus: The realistic EPS path for 2026 to 2029 once PTC's financing, the new shares and the buyback pause are included, and how far current broker estimates differ in their treatment of PTC. Which inputs move EPS most, and what management guides against what the sell-side assumes for each?

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 26 October 2026, 08:15 to 08:36 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Set 2 answers: the operating and financial model

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Schneider Electric SE (SU-FR), written 2026-10-26, price EUR 231.40 (23-Oct-26)

Preliminary notes for the Consumer: the footing of this model, and where I depart from the first set

These notes are not answers. They are the reading rules and corrections I applied. They matter because several of them move earnings per share (EPS) by more than the operating debate does.

1. The equity raise has, on the evidence, already happened. The first set treated it as a pending overhang. The AlphaSense report on the EPS bridge (citing the company's prospectus supplement of 21 October 2026) records an accelerated bookbuild priced at EUR 240 per share on 19 October 2026, a 4.99% discount to EUR 252.60. FactSet's capital-structure snapshot backs this up: it shows 593.3m diluted shares on 25 October, against 562.2m basic shares at end-2025. That gap is about 22.9m new shares (EUR 5.5bn at EUR 240) plus convertible and performance-share dilution. I model 22.9m new shares from late October 2026. The supply overhang is gone, but the dilution is now locked in. The Consumer should still confirm the exact number of shares issued.

2. PTC is expected to close by Q3 2027, not at the start of 2027. Deutsche Bank (5 October 2026) and the prospectus give closing "by the third quarter of 2027", subject to the PTC shareholder vote, HSR clearance and CFIUS. I consolidate PTC from 1 October 2027, so it contributes one quarter in FY2027. The consequence is the most important correction in this set. In FY2027 the share count is about 4% higher for the whole year, while PTC contributes only about EUR 650m of revenue and EUR 260m of adjusted EBITA. FY2027 is therefore the year of peak PTC dilution, not a year of low-single-digit accretion. The company's "immediately accretive" claim holds only on a full-year pro forma basis.

3. The basis of my EBITDA input. Brokers' adjusted EPS (FY2025: EUR 8.59, adjusted net income EUR 4,829m) is struck after restructuring costs. It is before amortisation of purchased intangibles and before "other operating income and expenses" (M&A costs, impairments, fines). I verified this by reconciling FY2024 and FY2025: only a restructuring-inclusive build reproduces the reported adjusted net income. So that the Financial Analyst's model lands on the brokers' EPS basis, my EBITDA margin input is: company adjusted EBITA, minus restructuring, plus depreciation and amortisation excluding purchased-intangible amortisation (this includes IFRS 16 right-of-use depreciation). On this basis FY2025 was EUR 8,412m, or 21.0%, which matches FactSet's brokers'-basis actual of EUR 8,384m (20.9%). FactSet's history line ("EBITDA, FactSet operating", EUR 8,699m, 21.7%) is before restructuring. Do not read my margins against that line without adding back restructuring (EUR 287m in FY2025 and EUR 450m guided for FY2026).

4. Below-the-line leakage is larger than FactSet's net interest line suggests. FactSet shows net interest of −EUR 553m for FY2025. Reconciling the company's adjusted net income of EUR 4,829m (adjusted EBITA EUR 7,508m, restructuring EUR 287m, tax 23.5%, minorities EUR 188m) implies about −EUR 715m of total net financial items. Of that, the cost of net debt is about −EUR 255m (Bernstein's EUR 258m for 2025 is consistent). The other −EUR 460m is lease interest, pension interest, currency losses in high-inflation countries, fees and the unwinding of put liabilities. My "net financial items" line carries all of this, calibrated so that FY2025 reproduces EUR 8.59 exactly.

5. Where else I depart from the first set.

  • Currency in 2027. The first set assumed about a 1% translation drag. Q3 2026 already showed a small positive currency and scope effect (+1 point). The FY2026 currency guide (−EUR 300m to −400m), set against −EUR 747m in H1, implies a Q4 tailwind. I assume a neutral currency effect for FY2027: positive in H1, negative in H2. This is uncertain: a 10% US-dollar move is worth EUR 1,505m of revenue and EUR 427m of adjusted EBITA.
  • Restructuring. The first set modelled EUR 300m to 450m a year. The company's own framework says cumulative incremental charges of EUR 500m over 2025 to 2027 sit on top of a normal EUR 100m to 150m a year. With EUR 287m spent in 2025 and EUR 450m guided for 2026, almost all of that increment is spent by end-2026. I use EUR 250m (2027), EUR 200m (2028) and EUR 175m (2029 and 2030), keeping an allowance above the stated norm because the first set's point that restructuring recurs is correct.
  • Support-function leverage is mainly volume leverage, not net cost cutting (see P-01 and P-03). That makes the margin path more dependent on growth than the "self-help" narrative implies.

6. Consensus net income is close to mine; consensus EPS is not, and the share count is the reason. For FY2027 to FY2029, my adjusted net income (EUR 6,950m, EUR 8,084m and EUR 9,060m) is within 1% of the FactSet section-3 consensus net income (EUR 6,995m, EUR 8,047m and EUR 9,065m). My EPS (EUR 11.85, EUR 13.73 and EUR 15.37) is nevertheless 5% to 6% below consensus EPS (EUR 12.61, EUR 14.49 and EUR 16.11). Consensus EPS divided by consensus net income implies about 555m shares (section 3) or about 575m (section 4). The post-raise count is about 586m to 589m. Many models were dated 16 to 20 October, before or around the raise. This is the single most useful fact for the fourth set: FY2027 and FY2028 EPS consensus should drift down by 2% to 5% as share counts are updated, even if profit estimates hold or rise.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: Financial forecasts

Appendix Forecast inputs the Investment Analyst handed the Financial Analyst

Forecast inputs: Schneider Electric SE (SU-FR), as at 26-Oct-26

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Read this first: the EBITDA basis. My EBITDA margin is company adjusted EBITA, minus restructuring, plus depreciation and amortisation excluding purchase-price amortisation. This matches the brokers' adjusted basis (FY2025: EUR 8,412m, 21.0%, against FactSet's brokers'-basis actual of EUR 8,384m, 20.9%). It is about 0.7 to 1.0 points below FactSet's history line "EBITDA (FactSet operating)" (FY2025: EUR 8,699m, 21.7%), which is before restructuring. Add back restructuring of EUR 450m, 250m, 200m, 175m and 175m (FY2026 to FY2030) to compare with that line. With this basis, EBITDA minus D&A plus net financial items, taxed, gives brokers'-basis adjusted net income, and my EPS cross-check reproduces FY2025's EUR 8.59.

  • Shelly (smart home, from about February 2027) and AiDASH (grid software, from Q4 2026) sit in Energy Management. Cognite and PTC sit in Industrial Automation.
  • Energy Management carries about 83% of revenue and nearly all of the data-centre and grid growth.
  • The FY2026 Energy Management margin includes most of the EUR 100m tariff refund (about 25bp of segment margin), so FY2027's +20bp is about +45bp underlying.
  • Industrial Automation excluding PTC is haircut against the company's 18% target for 2028 (I model 16.3%). PTC is modelled at its standalone 40% adjusted EBITA margin with no revenue synergies.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Organic growth: 13.9% (2026), 10.5% (2027), 8.0% (2028), 7.5% (2029), 7.5% (2030).
  • Data centres (about 30% to 33% of sales): about +25% to +30%, +18%, +10%, +9% and +9%. Triple-digit order growth in Q2 and Q3 2026 converts in 12 to 24 months, then normalises.
  • Rest of the group (grid, non-residential, Industrial Automation, residential flat): about +8.5%, +7%, +7%, +6.5% and +6.5%.
  • Price inside organic growth: about 2.7, 2.2, 1.3, 1.0 and 1.0 points.
  • Currency: about -0.8% in FY2026 (guide -EUR 300m to -400m); 0% in FY2027 (H1 +1.5%, H2 -1.5%); 0% thereafter. Each 10% move in the US dollar is worth EUR 1,505m of revenue and EUR 427m of adjusted EBITA.
  • Scope (EURm):
  • FY2027: +960 (PTC Q4 650; Shelly 200; Cognite 220; AiDASH 40; disposals -150).
  • FY2028: +1,660 (PTC January to September 2,040; disposals -400).
  • FY2029: -400. FY2030: -300.
  • The disposal programme totals EUR 1.25bn of revenue against a target of EUR 1bn to 1.5bn.
  • PTC: USD 2.7bn of revenue in 2026, +10% a year, EUR/USD about 1.17, closing assumed 1-Oct-27.
  • Margin:
  • Underlying standalone adjusted EBITA margin +75bp in 2027 (H1-weighted: price carry-over against H1 2026's -EUR 154m net price, and a frozen overhead), then +40bp, +35bp and +30bp.
  • Support-function ratio falls from about 22.3% (2026) to 20.3% (2030). R&D rises to about 6.6% of sales. Net price-cost is about 0, +400, +100, 0 and 0 (EURm).
  • FY2026 includes a EUR 100m tariff refund that does not recur.
  • Restructuring (EURm): -450, -250, -200, -175, -175 (deducted in EBITDA).
  • D&A (excluding purchase-price amortisation, including right-of-use depreciation): about 2.8% of revenue.
  • Net financial items: all items between EBIT and pre-tax profit on the adjusted basis (cost of net debt, lease interest, pension interest, currency losses, fees), calibrated to about -EUR 715m in FY2025.
  • Cost of new PTC debt 3.75% on EUR 16.5bn.
  • Net financial debt (company definition) about EUR 9.7bn at end-2026 (after the EUR 5.5bn equity raise and Cognite), EUR 28.8bn at end-2027, then 24.9bn, 21.7bn and 18.2bn.
  • Tax: 24.0% rising to 24.8% from 2028 (PTC's US mix). Minorities less associates about -EUR 20m to -30m a year; not entered, but included in the EPS cross-check.
  • Shares:
  • End-2025: 562.2m. 2026: buyback of EUR 600m (about -2.2m shares); equity raise of about 22.9m shares at EUR 240 from late October 2026; employee share plan about +1.4m for cash.
  • Every year: performance shares +1.0m (the non-cash line).
  • No buyback in 2027 or 2028. Buybacks of EUR 1.1bn (2029) and EUR 1.3bn (2030).
  • Average shares 566.7m, 586.5m, 588.9m, 589.4m and 587.9m.
  • Buybacks less new shares for cash (EURm):
  • 2026: -600 + 5,440 (net equity proceeds) + 270 (employee plan) = +5,110.
  • 2027 and 2028: +270.
  • 2029: -1,100 + 270 = -830. 2030: -1,300 + 270 = -1,030.
  • Acquisitions less disposals (EURm):
  • 2026: Cognite -2,650 and AiDASH -270.
  • 2027: Shelly -1,200, PTC -21,000 (equity value plus refinanced PTC debt plus fees, assumed paid at closing), disposals +150.
  • 2028: Motivair 25% put -350, disposals +400.
  • 2029: disposals +400, bolt-ons -300.
  • 2030: disposals +300, bolt-ons -300.
  • Working capital: the FactSet definition (receivables plus inventories less payables).
  • It rises from 14.6% (end-2025) to 14.8% on lost factoring (debtor days +4).
  • Caution: PTC's receivables are high relative to revenue because of annual billing in advance, and its deferred revenue is not in this definition. Acquired working capital is not a cash outflow. My cash flow uses organic revenue growth at about 14.8%.
  • Capital spending: about 3.4% to 3.5% of revenue, including capitalised development of about EUR 370m or more a year.
  • Leases: principal repayments only; lease interest sits in net financial items. Right-of-use assets were EUR 1,509m at end-2025. Lease payments are my estimate and not disclosed in the files.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

FY2026 guidance (16 October 2026) against my inputs.

  • Organic growth 12% to 14%: mine 13.9%, the top. The guide implies about 8% in Q4; I assume about 11%.
  • Organic adjusted EBITA growth +18% to +21%: mine about +21%.
  • Adjusted EBITA margin 19.7% to 19.9%: mine 19.9%, the top.
  • Currency on revenue -EUR 300m to -400m: mine about -350m.
  • Restructuring about EUR 450m: mine 450m.
  • Tax 23% to 25%: mine 24%.
  • Buyback EUR 600m: mine 600m.
  • I sit at the top of guidance, following the 2026 pattern of beats and two raises.

FY2027 guidance (due 18 February 2027). I expect organic growth in or slightly above 7% to 10%, and organic margin expansion of +50bp to +80bp. My inputs are 10.5% and +75bp underlying (+55bp against the reported FY2026 base, which includes the refund). The guide is likely to be beaten in H1 2027, when price carry-over peaks.

The 2026 to 2030 framework (Capital Markets Day, December 2025) against my inputs.

  • Organic growth 7% to 10% a year: mine averages about 9.5%.
  • Cumulative organic margin +250bp: mine is +260bp excluding PTC, front-loaded into 2026.
  • Free cash flow conversion about 100%: mine is 84% to 92% of adjusted net income, about 95% to 100% of reported net income.
  • Industrial productivity EUR 2bn to 2.5bn: exceeded in gross terms, but largely absorbed by inflation.
  • Support-function ratio -1.5 to -2 points: mine -3 points from 2025.
  • R&D towards 7% of sales: mine 6.6% by 2030.
  • Industrial Automation 18% by 2028: mine 16.3%. AVEVA 30% by 2028: about two-thirds delivered.
  • Data centres 12% to 14% CAGR to 2030: mine about 15%.
  • Buyback EUR 2.5bn to 3.5bn by 2030: mine 3.0bn.
  • Return on capital employed 15% to 20%: mine falls to about 11% to 12% in 2028 once PTC's capital is included, so I sit below this target.

PTC (5 October 2026) against my inputs.

  • "Immediately low-single-digit accretive to adjusted EPS": mine is about -2.5% to -3% in FY2027 (shares for the full year, PTC for one quarter), about +1.5% in 2028 and +3% to +4% by 2030.
  • Cost synergies EUR 250m by year three: mine 250m by 2030.
  • Revenue synergies about EUR 800m: mine 0.
  • Net debt to EBITDA below 1.5x within two years of consolidation: mine 1.4x at end-2029.
  • A-category rating: maintained in my base case.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Company headline. Adjusted EBITA is before restructuring, before other operating income and expenses (M&A and integration costs, impairments such as the EUR 128m capitalised-development write-down in H1 2026, fines) and before amortisation of purchased intangibles.
  • Brokers' adjusted EPS (the consensus basis) = (adjusted EBITA - restructuring + net financial items) x (1 - tax rate) - minorities + associates, divided by average basic shares. Purchase-price amortisation and other operating items, and their tax, are excluded.
  • FY2025: (7,508 - 287 - about 715) x (1 - 23.5%) = 4,977; less minorities 188, plus associates about 40 = 4,829; divided by 562.2m = EUR 8.59.
  • FY2026 (mine): (9,022 - 450 - 820) x 0.76 - 20 = 5,871; divided by 566.7m = EUR 10.36.
  • Reported EPS additionally deducts purchase-price amortisation after tax and other operating items after tax.
  • Purchase-price amortisation: about EUR 430m (2025), 420m (2026), 600m (2027) and 1,250m (2028 onward, my estimate of PTC's intangibles; the opening balance sheet will be audited only in early 2028).
  • FY2025 reported basic EPS was EUR 7.41 against adjusted 8.59, a 16% gap that included a EUR 388m associate impairment.
  • Expect a gap of about EUR 1.0 a share in 2026 and about EUR 2 a share from 2028.
  • FactSet's lines.
  • "EBIT (adjusted)" (FY2025: 7,047) is about adjusted EBITA minus purchase-price amortisation, before restructuring.
  • "EBITDA (FactSet operating)" (8,699) is adjusted EBITA plus D&A excluding purchase-price amortisation, before restructuring.
  • Brokers'-basis EBITDA (8,384) is about after restructuring, which is my basis.
  • FactSet "free cash flow" history (FY2025: 2,868) is after dividends. Before dividends it is 4,588, against the company's EUR 4,635m.
  • FactSet debt includes leases (net debt 14,663 at end-2025, against the company's net financial debt of 13,721).
  • My free cash flow cross-check is operating cash flow less capital spending (including intangibles) less lease principal, before dividends, acquisitions and buybacks.
  • The company's own definition is before lease payments. On that basis mine is 4,909, 5,955, 7,299, 8,234 and 9,213 (EURm), against FactSet consensus "FCF" of 5,076, 6,460, 7,743 and 8,953.
  • Consensus EPS share count. For FY2027 to FY2029, consensus EPS divided by consensus net income implies about 555m to 575m shares. The post-raise count is about 586m to 589m. My net income is within about 1% of the section-3 consensus net income, so the EPS gap of 5% to 6% is mostly share count.

Research requests for the next set

Company: Schneider Electric SE (SU-FR): global energy-management and industrial-automation group; segments are Energy Management (low- and medium-voltage electrical distribution, secure power and data-centre systems, liquid cooling, building and grid software; about 83% of revenue) and Industrial Automation (discrete, process and hybrid automation hardware plus industrial software including AVEVA, with the Cognite and PTC acquisitions pending)

Request: T-D1

Peers: ABB (ABBN-CH), the reference case for decentralised margin expansion in electrification; Eaton (ETN-US), the electrical and data-centre margin leader running a large capacity build; Legrand (LR-FR), the margin-first low-voltage peer selling through the same distributors; Siemens (SIE-DE), the industrial-software acquirer (UGS 2007, Mentor Graphics 2016, Altair 2024) and automation list-price setter; Vertiv (VRT-US), the data-centre power and cooling pure play running the same prefabrication model; Rockwell Automation (ROK-US), the automation margin benchmark; Dassault Systemes (DSY-FR), the PLM competitor and margin benchmark for PTC; Hager Group (private), European low-voltage competitor Operating conditions: data-centre orders normalising after triple-digit growth; competitor capacity additions and lead-time normalisation in switchgear and transformers; copper, silver and steel inflation and deflation; a short-cycle destocking and recovery; high interest rates and rising funding costs; integration of several large software acquisitions at once Inputs: Margin catch-up. Can a company whose Energy Management margin rose about 430bp from 2019 to 2024, against about 990bp at ABB Electrification and about 730bp at Eaton Electrical (Rothschild & Co Redburn), sustain 2026's pace of margin expansion (organic adjusted EBITA margin +100 to +120bp guided for FY2026, company, 16 October 2026) through 2028? Support-function costs: cut or outgrown? In H1 2026 support-function costs fell 1.1 points to 23.2% of sales, while the company's bridge showed gross savings of EUR 182m against EUR 160m of reinvestment and EUR 129m of inflation (company, 30 July 2026). Energy Management headcount had grown 24% in three years, against 1% to 7% at peers (Rothschild & Co Redburn, 30 March 2026). In which precedents did industrial companies hold margins when growth slowed after a hiring surge of this kind? Industrial Automation from about 14% (H1 2026) to the 18% target for 2028, and AVEVA from 17% (2024) to the 30% target for 2028. What have comparable automation-hardware recoveries and software subscription transitions delivered, and over how long? Software integration. What happened at Siemens and UGS, Mentor and Altair, at AVEVA and OSIsoft (2021), and at Schneider's own past integrations (Invensys 2014, AVEVA 2018 and 2023, OSIsoft) on cost-synergy delivery as a share of target sales, on revenue-synergy delivery and timing, and on talent retention? What do they imply for PTC's EUR 250m cost and about EUR 800m revenue synergy targets (company, 5 October 2026)? Pricing. Can product price realisation of 5.1% (Q3 2026, Deutsche Bank, 19 October 2026) be held when lead times normalise? What happened to electrical-equipment pricing in past normalisations (transformers, switchgear, cables)? Prefabrication capacity. Prefabricated capacity has grown fourfold since 2025, to 1.35 million square feet on eight sites (company, 21 October 2026). How did comparable capacity builds (Eaton's ramp in 2025 to 2026, Vertiv's 2018 to 2019 cycle) behave when orders slowed? Focus: Whether Schneider's 2027 to 2030 margin and earnings progression can be delivered with strengths it has already demonstrated (pricing on specified products, volume leverage on a semi-fixed overhead, integrated data-centre delivery), or whether it depends on strengths it has not yet shown (removing overhead rather than outgrowing it, recovering Industrial Automation margins, and integrating PTC, Cognite and AVEVA at scale).
